Neck-pinching of ℂ ​ P 1 \mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1} -structures in the PSL 2 ​ ℂ {\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C} -character variety
Shinpei Baba

Abstract
. We characterize a certain neck-pinching degeneration of (marked) ℂ ​ P 1 \mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1} -structures on a closed oriented surface S S of genus at least two. In a more general setting, we take a path of ℂ ​ P 1 \mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1} -structures C t ​ ( t ≥ 0 ) C_{t}\penalty\ (t\geq 0) on S S which leaves every compact subset in its deformation space, such that the holonomy of C t C_{t} converges in the PSL 2 ​ ℂ {\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C} -character variety as t → ∞ t\to\infty . Then it is well known that the complex structure X t X_{t} of C t C_{t} also leaves every compact subset in the Teichmüller space of S S . In this paper, under an additional assumption that X t X_{t} is pinched along a loop m m on S S , we describe the limit of C t C_{t} from different perspectives: namely, in terms of the developing maps, holomorphic quadratic differentials, and pleated surfaces. The holonomy representations of ℂ ​ P 1 \mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1} -structures on S S are known to be non-elementary (i.e. strongly irreducible and unbounded). We also give a rather exotic example of such a path C t C_{t} whose limit holonomy is the trivial representation.



1.
Introduction

Let
S
be a (connected) closed oriented surface of genus at least two, throughout this paper.
For a (marked)
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C
on
S
, the holonomy of
C
is a homomorphism
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
uniquely determined up to conjugation by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
; see §
2.2
.
This correspondence yields the
holonomy map

\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}},

where
\mathsf{P}\,(\cong\mathbb{R}^{12g-12})
is the deformation space of all
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
and
\chi
is the
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-character variety of
S
.
Note that there are many
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures whose holonomy is not discrete.

Hejhal
[
Hej75
]
proved that
\operatorname{Hol}
is a local homeomorphism (moreover, it is a local biholomorphic map
[
Hub81
]
,
[
Ear81
]
).
However, it is not a covering map onto its image (
[
Hej75
]
).
Thus it is a natural question to ask how the path-lifting property fails:

Problem 1.1
.

(Kapovich
[
Kap95
, Problem 1]
, see also
[
GKM00
, Problem 12.5.1]
.)
Let
C_{t}\penalty\ (t>0)
be a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
such that

(1)

C_{t}
leaves every compact subset in
\mathsf{P}
at
t\to\infty
, and

(2)

the holonomy
\eta_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
of
C_{t}
converges to
\eta_{\infty}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
as
t\to\infty
.

What is the asymptotic behavior of
C_{t}
?

In this paper, we give various limiting behaviors to answer Question
1.1
in the “neck-pinching” case.

1.1.
Pinching loops on Riemann surfaces

For each
t\geq 0
, let
X_{t}
denote the complex structure on
S
induced by
C_{t}
.
Then, by the work of Kapovich (
[
Kap95
]
, see also
[
GKM00
,
Dum17
]
), the conditions (
1
) and (
2
) imply that
X_{t}
must also leave every compact subset in the Teichmüller space
\mathsf{T}
(see Corollary
2.3
).

We focus on the following basic type of degeneration of
X_{t}
.
Given a path
X_{t}\in\mathsf{T}\penalty\
,
X_{t}
is
pinched along a loop

m
if

•

\operatorname{length}_{X_{t}}m\to 0
, and

•

if an essential loop
\ell
in
S\setminus m
is
not
homotopic to
m
, then
\operatorname{length}_{X_{t}}\ell
is bounded between two positive numbers for all
t\geq 0
.

Here
``\operatorname{length}_{X_{t}}"
is either the extremal length of
X_{t}
or the hyperbolic length of the uniformization of
X_{t}
.
(In the augmented Teichmüller space, this definition of pinching is equivalent to saying that
X_{t}
accumulates to a compact subset of the boundary stratum corresponding to
m
being pinched.)

A multiloop
is a union of disjoint finitely many essential simple closed curves.
Then, similarly, we say that
X_{t}
is
pinched along a multiloop

M
on
S
, if,

•

for each loop
m
of
M
,
\operatorname{length}_{X_{t}}m\to 0
as
t\to\infty
, and

•

for each loop
\ell
in
S\setminus M
not homotopic to a loop of
M
,
\operatorname{length}_{X_{t}}\ell
is bounded between two positive numbers for all
t\geq 0
.

The
quasi-Fuchsian representation

\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is a discrete faithful representation whose limit set is a Jordan curve in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, the quasi-Fuchsian Space
\mathsf{QF}
is an open subset of the character variety
\textstyle\chi
.
There is no path
C_{t}
in Problem
1.1
, whose limit holonomy
\eta_{\infty}
is in
\mathsf{QF}
.
On the other hand, a dense subset of the boundary of
\mathsf{QF}
consists of holonomy representations of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures pinched along loops (
[
McM91
]
), and it has been quite important to study such degeneration for the study of Kleinian groups.

1.2.
Asymptotic behaviors

One of our main results is that

\operatorname{tr}\eta_{\infty}(m)
must be
\pm 2
.
In other words, the holonomy along
m
at
t=\infty
corresponds to either (i) a parabolic element (which is not the identity) or (ii) the identity of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
We will describe, in both Cases (i) and (ii), the asymptotic behavior of
C_{t}
from three different perspectives of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures:

(A)

A holomorphic quadratic differential on a marked Riemann surface homeomorphic to
S
(
Schwarzian parameters
).

(B)

A hyperbolic structure on
S
and a measured lamination, which induces an equivariant pleated surface
\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
(
Thurston parameters
).

(C)

A developing map
f\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
and a holonomy representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
. (
Developing pair
)

The
residue
of a meromorphic quadratic differential
q
at a pole is the integral of
\pm\sqrt{q}
around the pole, which is well-defined up to sign (see
[
GW19
]
).
Given a pole of order two, letting
r
be its residue,
q
is expressed as
r^{2}/z^{-2}dz^{2}
for an appropriate parametrization in a neighborhood of the pole (see
[
Str84
, Theorem 6.3]
).

Let
X
be a nodal Riemann surface, and let
\mathring{X}
be the smooth part of
X
.
Then the
normalization
\overline{X}
of
X
is the smooth Riemann surface together with a continuous map
\xi\colon\overline{X}\to X
such that
\xi
is a biholomorphic in
\xi^{-1}(\mathring{X})
and for each node
p
of
X
,
\xi^{-1}(p)
consists of exactly two points.
A
regular quadratic differential
on
X
is a meromorphic quadratic differential
\bar{q}
on
\overline{X}
such that

•

every pole of
\bar{q}
has an order at most two and it maps to a node of
X
, and

•

if
z_{1},z_{2}
on
\overline{Z}
map to the same node on
X
, then the residue around
z_{1}
is equal to that of
z_{2}

(see
[
Ber74
]

[
Loftin_Zhang(19)
]
).

For Perspective (A),
the path
C_{t}
corresponds to a path of pairs
(X_{t},q_{t}),\penalty\ t\geq 0
in Schwarzian coordinates, where
X_{t}
is a marked Riemann surface homeomorphic to
S
and
q_{t}
is a holomorphic quadratic differential
q_{t}
on
X_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Theorem A
.

•

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
Then, exactly one of the following holds:

(i)

X_{t}
converges to a nodal Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
with a single node, and
q_{t}
converges to a regular quadratic differential on
X_{\infty}
such that the node is at worst a pole of order one (Theorem
10.12
.)

(ii)

For every diverging sequence
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, up to a subsequence,
X_{t_{i}}
converges to a nodal Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
with a single node and
q_{t_{i}}
converges to a regular quadratic differential
q_{\infty}
on
X_{\infty}
such that the residue of each pole is a non-zero integral multiple of
\sqrt{2}\pi
. (Theorem
13.20
.)

•

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop
M
consisting of
n
loops.
Then, for every diverging
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, there is a subsequence such that
X_{t_{i}}
converges a nodal Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
with
n
nodes and
q_{t}
converges to a meromorphic quadratic differential
q_{\infty}
on
X_{\infty}
such that each node of
X_{\infty}
is, at most, a pole of order two.
(Corollary
7.6
.)

The convergence of the holomorphic quadratic differential in Theorem
A
is normal convergence, and in particular, the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C_{t}
converges to the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure corresponding to
(X_{\infty},q_{\infty})
minus the node, uniformly on every compact subset.

The space of homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is called the
representation variety
, and
the
character variety

\textstyle\chi
is the GIT-quotient of the representation variety (see §
3
).
In order to obtain an equivariant object as a limit of
C_{t}
, we pick a (continuous) lift
\rho_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
of
\eta_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
, such that
\rho_{t}
converges, as
t\to\infty
, to a homomorphism
\rho_{\infty}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
which maps to
\eta_{\infty}
.
In fact, we prove the existence of such a lift in Proposition
3.2
, since it is not obvious when
\eta_{\infty}
is an elementary representation.

Note that for every discrete faithful representations
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, there is a unique equivariant continuous map
\partial_{\infty}\pi_{1}(S)\cong\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
called the Cannon-Thurston map (
[
Mj14
]
).
This map is closely related to the question which we consider, by identifying the ideal boundary of
\tilde{S}
with
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Let
N
be a regular neighborhood of the loop
m
in
S
.
For
t\geq 0
,
let
C_{t}\cong(\tau_{t},L_{t})
be Thurston parameters, where
\tau_{t}
is a path of marked hyperbolic structures on
S
and
L_{t}
is a path of measured laminations on
S
(§
2.2.2
).
Fixing a marking
\iota_{t}\colon S\to\tau
in its isotopy class,
(\tau_{t},L_{t})
yields to a
\rho_{t}
-equivariant pleated surface
\beta_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which changes continuously in
t\geq 0
.
Then, in fact,
\beta_{t}
converges to a continuous equivariant map:

Theorem B
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
Then, by taking an appropriate path of markings
\iota_{t}\colon S\to\tau_{t}\penalty\ (t\geq 0)
, exactly one of the following holds:

(i)

\rho_{\infty}(m)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is a parabolic element, and
\beta_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\beta_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets, such that
\beta^{-1}_{\infty}(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})
is a
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant multicurve on
\tilde{S}
which is
\pi_{1}(S)
-equivariantly homotopic to
\phi^{-1}(m)
, where
\phi\colon\tilde{S}\to S
is the universal covering map. (Theorem
10.5
).

(ii)

\rho_{\infty}(m)
is the identity in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, and, for every sequence
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
, up to a subsequence,
\beta_{t_{i}}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\beta_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
such that
\beta_{\infty}^{-1}(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})
descends either to the loop
m
or to a subsurface isotopic to one or two components of
S\setminus N
(§
13.0.1
.)

Let
f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
be the developing map of
C_{t}
which is a
\rho_{t}
-equivariant local homeomorphism.
As
C_{t}
changes continuously in
t
, we may assume that
f_{t}
also changes continuously in
t\geq 0
.
Such a family
(f_{t})
is unique up to a path of isotopies
S\to S
in
t\geq 0
homotopic to the identity.

Pick a regular neighborhood
N
of
m
.
Pick a component
\tilde{N}
of
\phi^{-1}(N)
.
By abuse of notation, we regard the loop
m
also as the element of
\pi_{1}(S)
which preserves
\tilde{N}
.
We show that the developing map
f_{t}
converges in the complement of
\phi^{-1}(N)
, and the asymptotic behavior on
\partial\phi^{-1}(N)
is well controlled by the holonomy
\rho_{t}(m)
.
Hyperbolic structures are in particular
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures.
If a hyperbolic surface has a cusp, it has a neighborhood obtained by quotienting a horodisk in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
by the cyclic group generated by a parabolic holonomy around the puncture.

Theorem C
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
Then, by an appropriate isotopy of
S
in
t\geq 0
homotopic to the identity, exactly one of (i) and (ii) holds.

(i)

•

\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic;

•

the cusps of
C_{\infty}
have horodisk quotient neighborhoods;

•

f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets, and moreover, there is a multiloop
M
which is a union of finitely many parallel copies of
m
such that
f_{\infty}
is a local homeomorphism exactly on
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(M)
,
and
f_{\infty}
takes each component
\tilde{m}
of
\phi^{-1}(M)
to its corresponding parabolic fixed point (Theorem
10.9
).

(ii)

\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
, and
for every diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, up to a subsequence,

•

the restriction of
f_{t_{i}}
to
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, and

•

\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t_{i}}(m))
converges to a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
or a point in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
so that
f_{\infty}
takes the boundary components of
\tilde{N}
onto the ideal points (in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
) of
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t_{i}}(m))
(Theorem
13.1
), where
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t_{i}}(m))
is the convex hull of the fixed point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
(
Definition

3.6
).

Remark 1.2
.

If a general
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure has a cusp with parabolic peripheral holonomy, there is its cusp neighborhood isomorphic to either a horodisk quotient or a grafting of a horodisk quotient. (See Proposition
5.2
.)

A (2
\pi
-)grafting is a cut-and-paste operation of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure, and it yields a new
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure with the same holonomy, by inserting an appropriate cylinder along an (admissible) loop (
[
Gol87
]
, see also
[
Kap01
,
Bab20
]
).
Let
n
be the number of parallel copies of
m
constituting
M
in (i).
Then there is another diverging path
C_{t}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
with holonomy
\rho_{t}
and a path of admissible loops
m_{t}^{\prime}
on
C_{t}^{\prime}
for
t\gg 0
such that
C_{t}
is obtained by
2\pi(n-1)
-grafting of
C_{t}^{\prime}
.

In fact, Cases (i) and (ii) in Theorem
A
, Theorem
B
, and Theorem
C
correspond.
In particular, the Type (i) degeneration occurs on the boundary of the quasi-Fuchsian space, by pinching a loop on a Bers slice.

On the other hand, Type (ii) degeneration is new indeed.
In particular,
\eta_{t}
must be a non-discrete representation for all sufficiently large
t>0
, possibly except at
t=\infty
(Theorem
13.21
).
Notice that if the peripheral loop of a cusp of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure has trivial holonomy, then the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure can be deformed without changing its holonomy (of the entire surface), by moving the cusp (c.f. Theorem
5.6
).
Then, since
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
, therefore it is necessary to take a subsequence.
In §
14
, we give examples of Type (ii) degenerations.

Next, we explain a certain uniform bound of
C_{t}
, which yields the convergence of
C_{t}
away from the pinched loop
m
.
This uniform bound holds for a more general path
C_{t}
with a multiloop being pinched.
The integration of
\sqrt{q_{t}}
along paths on
X_{t}
yields a singular Euclidean structure
E_{t}
on
X_{t}
such that a zero of order
d
of
q_{t}
is the singular point of cone angle
(d/2+1)\pi
of
E_{t}
(see for example,
[
FM12
,
Str84
]
).
Recall that the upper injectivity radius of
E_{t}
is the supremum of the injectivity radii over all points in
E_{t}
(as
E_{t}
is compact, it is indeed maximum).

Theorem D
.

(Theorem
6.1
)
Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop.
Then the upper injectivity radius of
E_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
is bounded from above.

It is a classical theorem that the holonomy map
\operatorname{Hol}
is a local homeomorphism for the closed surface
S
.
In the limit of
C_{t}
, we have a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure with cusps, such that cusp points are at most poles of order two in the Schwarzian coordinates.
The holonomy theorem is proved for such
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-surfaces cusps by Luo (
[
Luo93
]
) if punctures have non-trivial peripheral holonomy.
In this paper, we prove a more general holonomy theorem (Theorem
5.6
) for the developing pairs of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures allowing trivial holonomy around punctures.
We apply this holonomy theorem for the convergence on
C_{t}
in every thick part as
t\to\infty
.
This holonomy theorem is given by appropriately enlarging the character variety, and this enlargement is a certain ramification of the framed representation space introduced by Fock and Goncharov (
[
FG06
]
).
(For recent developments on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure corresponding to higher order poles, see
[
GM21
,
AB20
]
.)

Gallo, Kapovich, and Marden algebraically characterized the image of
\operatorname{Hol}
; in particular, it is almost onto one of the two components of the character variety
\textstyle\chi
(
[
GKM00
]
).
To be more precise,
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}\in\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Hol}
if and only if
\operatorname{Im}\rho
is non-elementary and
\rho
lifts to a homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(S)
into
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
As an example of Type (ii) degeneration, we construct a path
C_{t}
whose holonomy limits to an elementary representation, or even to the trivial representation in the representation variety (§
14
).

If the holonomy of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure around a puncture is trivial, as stated above, the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure can be deformed around the puncture without changing the holonomy of the entire surface.
A non-elementary subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
has a non-trivial stabilizer, a similar difficulty occurs when the limit holonomy of a component of
S\setminus m
is elementary.
As a result of such flexibility, we have rather exotic degenerations described in Case (ii) of
B
and
C
.

One may certainly hope that some of the results extend to a more general setting of Problem
1.1
.
In particular, Theorem
D
may hold in general:

Conjecture E
.

In the setting of Problem
1.1
(without the neck-pinching assumption),
let
E_{t}
be the singular Euclidean structure on
X_{t}
given by the Schwarzian parameters of
C_{t}
.
Then the upper injectivity radius of
E_{t}
is bounded from above uniformly in
t\geq 0
.

Recall that
\rho_{t}\,(t\geq 0)
is a topological path in the character variety
\chi
which converges to
\rho_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
without any regularity assumption.
It is plausible that Cases (
ii
) in
A
,
B
and
C
do not occur if
\rho_{t}
has a one-side derivative at
t=\infty
(in the ambient affine space of
\chi
).

Conjecture F
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
If the path
\rho_{t}
is tangential at
t=\infty
, Then
\eta_{\infty}(m)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is a parabolic element (not equal to the identity
I
).

1.3.
Outline of this paper

In §
2
, we recall
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures, the Schwarzian parameters, Thurston parameters, and the Epstein surfaces for
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures.
In §
3
, we prove a lifting property of paths in the character variety to paths in the representation variety.
In §
4
, we give some estimates of the Epstein surfaces, based on Dumas’ work
[
Dum17
]
.
In §
5
, we prove a holonomy theorem for the space of developing pairs of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on surfaces with punctures, where punctures are at most poles of order two.
In §
6
, we show that there is an upper bound for the upper injectivity radius of
E_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

In §
7
, we show that
C_{t}
converges on every thick part as
t\to\infty
, so that
C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a surface with two punctures homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
.
In §
8
, we state our main theorems and prove some properties of developing maps of a surface with punctures.
The limit holonomy around
m
can only be parabolic or the identity.
This will be shown, in §
11
and §
12
.
In §
10
, we determine the asymptotic behavior of
C_{t}
when
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
In §
13
, we give the asymptotic behavior of
C_{t}
when
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
.

In §
14
, we give new examples realizing (ii) in Theorem
A
, Theorem
B
, Theorem
C
.

1.4.

The work done here is partially supported by German Research Foundation (BA 5805/1-1),
GEAR Network (U.S. National Science Foundation grants DMS 1107452, 1107263, 1107367), and a JSPS Grant-in-Aid for Research Activity start-up (18H05833).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Hyperbolic geometry

Let
\tau
be a hyperbolic structure on
S
.
Let
L
be a geodesic measured lamination on
\tau
.
Given a geodesic loop
m
on
\tau
, for a point
x
in the intersection of
m
and
L
, let
\angle_{x}(L,m)\in[0,\pi)
denote the intersection angle of of the leaf
L
and
m
intersecting at
x
.
Then, the
angle

\angle_{\tau}(m,L)\in[0,1)
between
L
and
m
be the maximum of
\angle_{x}(L,m)
over all intersection points
x\in L\cap m
if
L\cap m\neq\emptyset
, and
\angle_{\tau}(m,L)=0
if
L\cap m=\emptyset
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\tau
denote the universal covering map.
Then the
\phi
-inverse image
\tilde{L}
of
L
is a
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant measured lamination on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
The pair
(\tau,L)
induces a
bending map

\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
which is equivariant via an associated homomorphism
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
This mapping
\beta
is defined by bending the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
of
\tau
along
L
, where the bending angle is given by the transversal measure of
\tilde{L}
(
[
EM87
]
).
Then the pair
(\tau,L)
determines
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
uniquely up to
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
; thus the pair
(\beta,\rho)
is identified with
(\alpha\circ\beta,\alpha\rho\alpha^{-1})
for
\alpha\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

It follows from Corollary 4.3 in
[
Bab15
]
(see also Theorem 5.1 in
[
Bab17
]
) that, if a geodesic loop on
\tau
intersects the lamination in a small angle, then the holonomy along the loop must be hyperbolic.

Theorem 2.1
.

There is a universal constant
\delta>0
such that if
\angle_{\tau}(L,m)<\delta
, then
\rho(m)
is hyperbolic.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{m}
be a lift of
m
to the bi-infinite geodesic in the universal cover
\tilde{\tau}=\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Then, the restriction of
\beta
to
\tilde{m}
is a
(1+\epsilon)
-bilipschitz embedding (Corollary 4.3 in
[
Bab15
]
).
Since
\beta
is
\rho
-equivariant,
\rho(m)
is a hyperbolic element whose axis connects the ideal point of the bilipschitz embedding
\beta(\tilde{m})
.
∎

2.2.
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures

(General references of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures are found in
[
Dum09
,
Kap01
]
.)

A
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure

C
, or a
complex projective structure
, on
S
is a
(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1},{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})
-structure, i.e. an atlas of charts embedding into
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
with transition maps given by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

Let
\tilde{S}
be the universal cover of
S
.
Then, equivalently, a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure is a pair
(f,\rho)
of a local homeomorphism
f\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
and a homomorphism
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
f
is
\rho
-equivariant.
The map
f
is called the
developing map
and
\rho
is called the
holonomy representation
of
C
.

The pair is defined up to
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, i.e.
(f,\rho)\sim(\alpha f,\alpha\rho\alpha^{-1})
for all
\alpha\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Thus the holonomy is in the character variety
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}={\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(S),{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C})\sslash{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

2.2.1.
Schwarzian parametrization

Each
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure corresponds to a holomorphic quadratic differential
q
on a marked Riemann surface
X
.
Thus the deformation space
\mathsf{P}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures is an (affine) vector bundle over the Teichmüller space
\mathsf{T}
, such that a fiber over a Riemann surface
X
is the vector space
Q(X)
of holomorphic quadratic differentials on
X
(in fact, it is the cotangent bundle).
In this paper, considering the projection map
\Pi\colon\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{T}
given by the uniformization, we regard the space of marked hyperbolic structures on
S
as our real analytic zero section.

Although
\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
is a highly non-proper map (
[
Hej75
]
), for each
X\in\mathsf{T}
, the restriction of
\operatorname{Hol}
to the space
Q(X)
is a proper embedding onto a complex analytic subvariety of
\textstyle\chi
(see
[
GKM00
, Theorem 11.4.1]
and its proof).
Moreover

Theorem 2.2

(
[
Kap95
,
Tan99
]
)
.

For every compact subset
K
of
\mathsf{T}
, the restriction of
\operatorname{Hol}
to
\Pi^{-1}(K)
is a proper map.

Corollary 2.3
.

Suppose that
C_{t}\in\mathsf{P}
leaves every compact subset in
\mathsf{P}
and its holonomy
\rho_{t}
converges in
\textstyle\chi
.
Then the complex structure
X_{t}
of
C_{t}
also leaves every compact subset in
\mathsf{T}
as
t\to\infty
.

2.2.2.
Thurston’s parametrization of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures

(
[
KP94a
,
KT92
]
, see also
[
Bab20
]
.)
Thurston gave a homeomorphism

\mathsf{P}\cong\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML},

where
\mathsf{T}
is the space of marked hyperbolic structures on
S
and
\mathsf{ML}
is the space of measured laminations on
S
.

A pair
(\tau,L)\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
yields a pleated surface
\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
equivariant under the holonomy
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
of its corresponding
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
.
Given a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C
on
S
,
its associated
collapsing map

\kappa\colon C\to\tau
is a marking preserving continuous map which relates the developing map and the bending map of
C
.
First, there is a measured lamination
\mathcal{L}
on
C
consisting of circular leaves, such that topologically
\mathcal{L}
is obtained by replacing each periodic leaf
\ell
of
L
by cylinder foliated circumferences so that the weight of
\ell
is equal to the total transversal measure of the foliated cylinder.
The collapsing map
\kappa
, conversely, collapses such foliated cylinders of
\mathcal{L}
to their corresponding periodic leaves of
L
, and
\kappa
takes the strata of
\mathcal{L}
to the strata of
L
.

Moreover,
\kappa
relates the developing map
f\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
and the pleated surface
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
in an equivariant manner:
For each
z\in\tilde{S}
, let
B_{z}
be the
maximal ball
in
\tilde{C}
whose core contains
z
.
Let
\Psi_{z}\colon B_{z}\to{\rm Conv}\partial_{\infty}B_{z}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
denote the orthogonal projection, where
{\rm Conv}\partial_{\infty}B_{z}
is the hyperbolic plane (
support plane
) bounded by the boundary circle.
Then, in fact, the commutativity

\beta\circ\tilde{\kappa}(z)=\Psi_{z}f(z),

holds equivariantly, where
\tilde{\kappa}\colon\tilde{C}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\tilde{\tau}
be the lift of
\kappa
to a map between universal covers.
Note that there is a canonical normal direction of the support plane
{\rm Conv}\partial B_{z}
at
\Psi_{z}f(z)
toward
f(z)
.

2.3.
Epstein maps

Let
C=(X,q)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
in the Schwarzian coordinates, where
X
is the complex structure of
X
, and
q
is a holomorphic quadratic differential on
X
.
Then, the integration of
\sqrt{q}
along paths yields a singular Euclidean metric
E
on
X
in the same conformal class (see for example
[
FM12
]
).
In the complex plane, the lines parallel to the real axis give a foliation of
\mathbb{C}
, and it has a transversal measure induced by the vertical length (
horizontal measured foliation
).
Similarly, the lines parallel to the imaginary axis give a foliation of
\mathbb{C}
, and it has a transversal measure induced by the horizontal length (
vertical measured foliation
).
Then, by pulling back the vertical and the horizontal foliations of
\mathbb{C}
, we obtain a vertical singular measured foliation
V
and a horizontal singular measured foliation
H
on
E
, where the singular points are the zeros of the differential
q
.
Moreover
H
and
V
are orthogonal, and the vertical and the horizontal foliation of
\mathbb{C}
are orthogonal.

Given a point
x\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we can normalize the unit disk model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
so that
x
is the center of the disk; then the ideal boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
has the spherical metric uniquely determined by
x\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Theorem 2.4

(Epstein
[
Eps
]
)
.

Given a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C=(f,\rho)
on
S
, there is a unique continuous
\rho
-equivariant map
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
, such that, for every point
z\in\tilde{X}
, the Euclidean metric of
\tilde{E}
at
z
agrees with the spherical metric at
f(z)\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
when
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
is identified with
\mathbb{S}^{2}
so that
\operatorname{Ep}(z)\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
is at the center of the disk model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Moreover
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
is smooth away from the singular points of
\tilde{E}
(see Equation (3.1) in
[
Dum17
]
).

Let
U\mathbb{H}^{3}
denote the unit tangent bundle of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then
\operatorname{Ep}
lifts to a (Lagrangian) immersion
\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}\colon T\tilde{E}\to U(\mathbb{H}^{3})
(
[
Dum17
, Lemma 3.2]
) which is a unit normal vector of the surface
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
in the complement of the singular points of
E
.
For
z\in\tilde{X}
, let
d(z)
denote the Euclidean distance from
z
to the set
Z
of the zeros of the differential
q
.

Lemma 2.5

(Lemma 2.6, Lemma 3.4 in
[
Dum17
]
)
.

Let
h^{\prime}(z)
and
v^{\prime}(z)
be the horizontal and vertical unit tangent vectors at
z\in\tilde{X}\setminus\tilde{Z}
.
If
\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}<\frac{3}{4}
, then

(1)

\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}h^{\prime}(z)\|<\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
,

(2)

\sqrt{2}<\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}v^{\prime}\|<\sqrt{2}+\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
,

(3)

h^{\prime}(z),v^{\prime}(z)
are the principal directions of
\operatorname{Ep}
at
z
, and

(4)

|k_{v}|<\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
, where
k_{v}
is the curvature of
\operatorname{Ep}
in the
v
-direction.

3.
A lifting property of paths in the character variety

Definition 3.1
.

A representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is
elementary
if
\operatorname{Im}\rho
fixes a point in
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
or preserves two points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Equivalently,
\rho
is elementary if
\operatorname{Im}\rho
is strongly irreducible and
\operatorname{Im}\rho
is unbounded in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Otherwise
\rho
is called non-elementary.

Let
\mathcal{R}
denote the
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-
representation variety
of
S
, the space of representations
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
By fixing a generating set
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, the topology of
\mathcal{R}
is the restriction of the product topology on
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which is independent on the choice of
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
.
The Lie group
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
acts on
\mathcal{R}
by conjugation, and its GIT-quotient

\Psi\colon\mathcal{R}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}=\{\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}\}\sslash{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

is called the
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-character variety of
S
.

Each fiber of this GIT-quotient is an
extended orbit equivalence
class: Namely, for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
,
\rho_{1}\sim\rho_{2}
if and only if the closure of the
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-orbit of
\rho_{1}
intersects that of
\rho_{2}
in
\mathcal{R}
.
In fact, equivalently

\rho_{1}\sim\rho_{2}
if and only if
\operatorname{tr}^{2}\rho_{1}(\gamma)=\operatorname{tr}^{2}\rho_{2}(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)

[
HP04
]
.
In particular, for a non-elementary representation
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, its
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-orbit is a closed subset of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
and form a single equivalence class (
[
New
]
).
For
\rho\in\mathcal{R}
, let
[\rho]
denote it equivalent class
\Psi(\rho)
in
\textstyle\chi
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
C_{t}

(t\geq 0)
is a one-parameter family of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
, such that its holonomy
\eta_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
converges to
\eta_{\infty}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
.
Then
\eta_{t}
lifts a path
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
which converges to
\rho_{\infty}\in\mathcal{R}
as
t\to\infty
, so that
[\rho_{\infty}]=\eta_{\infty}
.

Remark 3.3
.

The limit
\eta_{\infty}
can be an elementary representation (§
14
), and thus this proposition is nontrivial.
In addition, there is
\eta\in\mathcal{R}
with
[\eta]=\rho_{\infty}
such that there is no lift
\eta_{t}
of
\rho_{t}
ending at
\eta
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Fix a generating set
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of
\pi_{1}(S)
.
We divide the proof into three cases:

(1)

\eta_{\infty}
is non-elementary.

(2)

\eta_{\infty}
is elementary and there is
\gamma\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma)
is hyperbolic, i.e.
\operatorname{tr}^{2}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4]
.

(3)

\eta_{\infty}
is elementary and there is no hyperbolic element in its image, i.e.
\operatorname{tr}^{2}\eta_{\infty}(\gamma)\in[0,4]
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

Case
1
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
\eta_{\infty}
is non-elementary.
For every lift
\rho_{\infty}\in\mathcal{R}
of
\eta_{\infty}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
, there is a lift
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
of the path
\eta_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
such that
\rho_{t}\to\rho_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Over non-elementary representations,
\Psi
is a fiber bundle with fibers
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
This implies the lemma.
∎

Case
2
.
Suppose that
\eta_{\infty}
is elementary and there is
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
such that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma)
is hyperbolic.
Then, if
\rho\in\Psi^{-1}(\eta_{\infty})
, letting
\ell
be the axis of the hyperbolic element
\rho(\gamma)
, we have either:

(i)

\operatorname{Im}\rho
preserves
\ell
and contains an elliptic element which reverses the orientation of
\ell
, or

(ii)

\operatorname{Im}\rho
pointwise fixes the endpoints of
\ell
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Case (
i
).

Suppose that
\rho\in\Psi^{-1}(\eta_{\infty})
contains an elliptic element which exchanges the endpoints of
\ell
.

Claim 3.5
.

There are generators
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of
\pi_{1}(S)
, such that, for each
i=1,\dots,n,

(1)

\rho(\gamma_{i})
is a hyperbolic element for
i=1,\dots,n-1
, and

(2)

\rho(\gamma_{n})
is an elliptic element of order two about a geodesic orthogonal to
\ell
.

Proof.

By the hypothesis, one can pick generators
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of
\pi_{1}(S)
, such that
\rho(\gamma_{1})
is a (nontrivial) hyperbolic element.
Then we can, in addition, assume that
\rho(\gamma_{2}),\dots,\rho(\gamma_{n})
are not
I
, by composing
\gamma_{i}\,(i\geq 2)
with
\gamma_{1}
if necessary.
If
\rho(\gamma_{i})
is an elliptic element preserving the orientation of
\ell
, then
\rho(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{i})
is hyperbolic— thus without loss of generality, we can assume that if
\rho(\gamma_{i})
is an elliptic element, it must reverse the orientation of
\ell
.
Suppose that
\rho(\gamma_{i})
and
\rho(\gamma_{j})
are both elliptic elements reversing the orientation of
\ell
; then
\rho(\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j})
preserves the orientation of
\ell
.
Thus, by replacing
\gamma_{j}
with
\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}
, we can reduce the number of the generators which map to elliptic elements reversing the orientation of
\ell
.
We can repeat such replacements of generators, we obtain a desired generating set.
∎

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}
be the generating set of
\pi_{1}(S)
obtained by
3.5
.
We show that there is indeed a lift
\rho_{t}
in
\mathcal{R}
of
\eta_{t}
converging to
\rho
as
t\to\infty
.

One can easily find a lift
\rho_{t}\,(t\geq 0)
so that
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{1})
converges to
\rho(\gamma_{1})
.
Then
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{1}))
must converge to
\ell
.
For all
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{n}))
are asymptotically orthogonal, as
\eta_{\infty}
is an equivalence class of some elementary representation.
In particular, we can in addition assume that
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{n})
converges to
\rho(\gamma_{n})
, so that
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{n}))
converges to a geodesic
m
orthogonal to
\ell
.
Then, for
1<i<n
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
converges
\ell
, since it is asymptotically orthogonal to
m
and
\eta_{\infty}
is elementary.
Thus
\rho_{t}
converges to
\rho
as
t\to\infty
.

Case (
ii
).

Next suppose that
\rho\in\Psi^{-1}(\eta_{\infty})
preserves the endpoints of
\ell
.
Then, similarly to Claim
3.5
, we can find a generating set
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
such that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{n})
are all hyperbolic elements (i.e.
\operatorname{tr}^{2}\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{i})\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,4]
).

Pick any lift
\rho_{t}
of
\eta_{t}
for
t\geq 0
(which may not converge as
t\to\infty
).

Fix a
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-invariant metric on the projectivized unit tangent bundle
{\rm PT^{1}}\mathbb{H}^{3}
of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then, given two geodesics
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we can measure their distance by embedding
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
into the bundle.
Thus, similarly, for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
, the distance between
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j}))
goes to zero as
t\to\infty
, since otherwise
\eta_{\infty}
is an equivalent class of some non-elementary representations due to the limit of
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i})
and
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j})
.
Thus we can continuously conjugate
\rho_{t}
by elements of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that all axes of
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\rho_{t}(\gamma_{n})
converge to geodesics sharing an endpoint.
Therefore
\rho_{t}
converges as
t\to\infty
by this normalization.

Case
3
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{Im}\eta_{\infty}
contains no hyperbolic elements.
Given an elliptic element and a parabolic element in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
sharing a fixed point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
then their product is an elliptic element.
Therefore we can pick generators
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of
\pi_{1}(S)
, such that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{i})
are either all elliptic or all parabolic:
In fact, given a generating set
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, if the
\eta_{\infty}
-image of at least one
\gamma_{i}
is elliptic, then by replacing
\gamma_{j}
with parabolic
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{j})
with
\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}
, we obtain a generating set with elements whose
\eta_{\infty}
-images are all elliptic.
Pick any lift
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
of the path
\eta_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
for
t\geq 0
, which may not converge as
t\to\infty
.

Definition 3.6
.

For
\gamma\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, the axis of
\gamma
is the convex hull of the fixed point set in
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
of
\gamma
, and we denote it by
\operatorname{Axis}(\gamma)\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

In particular, if
\gamma
is hyperbolic or elliptic,
\operatorname{Axis}(\gamma)
is a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
plus its endpoints in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, and
if
\gamma
is parabolic,
\operatorname{Axis}(\gamma)
is a single point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Clearly an ideal point of
\operatorname{Axis}(\gamma)
is a fixed point of
\gamma
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Suppose that
\gamma,\omega\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be hyperbolic or elliptic elements with axes
\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\omega}
.
As above, we measure the distance between
\ell_{\gamma},\ell_{\omega}
by embedding them into the projective unit tangent bundle of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

(1)

Suppose that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{i})
and
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{j})
are both elliptic for distinct
1\leq i,j\leq n
.
Then the distance between
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j}))
in
{\rm PT^{1}}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
limits to zero as
t\to\infty
.

(2)

Suppose that

\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{i})
,
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{j})
,
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{k})
are all elliptic for distinct
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
.
Then there is a lift
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
of
\eta_{t}
for
t\geq 0
, such that
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j}))
, and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{k}))
converge to geodesics sharing a common endpoint on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Proof.

(
1
)
If there is a diverging sequence
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that the distance between
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j}))
in
{\rm PT^{1}}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
is bounded from below by a positive number, then
\eta_{\infty}
is non-elementary.
This is a contradiction.

(
2
)
By (
1
), if the assertion of (
2
) fails, there is a lift
\rho_{t}
such that
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i}))
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{j}))
, and
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t}(\gamma_{k}))
converge to the distinct edges of an ideal triangle in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then,
\eta_{\infty}
is non-elementary against the hypothesis.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Suppose that there is a generating set
\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
of
\pi_{1}(S)
, such that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{n})
are all elliptic.
Then, there is a lift
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
of
\eta_{t}
such that
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\rho_{t}(\gamma_{n})
converge to elliptic elements whose axes share an endpoint on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Last we suppose that
\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\eta_{\infty}(\gamma_{n})
are all parabolic, and we show that there is a lift of
\rho_{t}
of
\eta_{t}
to
\mathcal{R}
such that
\rho_{t}
converges to the trivial representation.

Pick a base point
O\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
For each
t\geq 0
, let
\delta_{t,i}=d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(O,\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i})O)
.
Let
i_{t}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
be such that

\delta_{t,i_{t}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\delta_{t,i}.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
be a sequence diverging to
\infty
, such that, at
t_{t_{k}}
, the indices
i_{t_{k}}\in\{1,\dots,n\}\ (k=1,2,\dots)
defined above are a fixed constant
h
.
Suppose that there is a sequence
\omega_{t_{k}}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that the conjugation
\omega_{t_{k}}\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{h})\omega_{t_{k}}^{-1}\eqqcolon\omega_{t_{k}}\!\cdot\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{h})
converges in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, as
k\to\infty
, to a parabolic element

\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
with
u\neq 0
.
Then, for every
j=1,\dots,n
, the conjugation
\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{j})
accumulates to a bounded subset of

\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{C}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
which has a diameter less than
|u|
in
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

First we show that, unless
\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\!\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{j})\to I
, the limit of the fixed point set of
\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{j})\subset\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
must converge to
\{\infty\}
.
Suppose, to the contrary, that this assertion fails.
Then, up to a subsequence, the limit set of the fixed point set of
\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{j})\subset\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to a point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
not equal to
\infty
.
For sufficiently large positive integers
p
,
\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\!\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{h}\gamma_{i}^{p})
are hyperbolic elements and their translation lengths diverge to
\infty
as
p\to\infty
(
[
GKM00
, Lemma 2.1.1 (iii)]
).
This contradicts that
\operatorname{Im}\eta_{\infty}
consists of only parabolic elements.

For each
k=1,2,\dots
,
set

(1)

\omega_{t_{k}}\!\!\cdot\rho_{t_{k}}(\gamma_{j})=\begin{pmatrix}a_{j,k}&b_{j,k}\\
c_{j,k}&d_{j,k}\end{pmatrix}

Thus
c_{j,k}\to 0
and
a_{j,k},d_{j,k}\to 1
as
k\to\infty
.
Then the definition of
h
implies that
b_{t_{k},h}-\max_{1\leq i\leq n}b_{t_{k},i}\to 0
.
Hence we have the upper bound on the image in
\mathbb{C}
.
∎

By a straight computation, we obtain the following.

Corollary 3.10
.

For every
j=1,\dots,n
, let
s_{j,k}>0
be a sequence in
k
, such that
s_{j,k}\to 0
and
\frac{\sqrt{\lvert c_{j,k}\rvert}}{s_{j,k}}\to 0
.
Then, using the notation from (
1
), we have

(2)

\begin{pmatrix}s_{j,k}&0\\
0&s_{j,k}^{-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{j,k}&b_{j,k}\\
c_{j,k}&d_{j,k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}s_{j,k}&0\\
0&s_{j,k}^{-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

as
k\to\infty
.

Moreover,
Corollary

3.10
implies that
the sequence
\max_{j=1,\dots,n}{s_{j,k}}
in
k
yields the convergence (
2
) for all
j=1,\dots,n
.
Therefore we have the following.

Proposition 3.11
.

There is a continuous path
\omega_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
\omega_{t}\!\cdot\rho_{t}(\gamma_{i})
accumulates to a bounded subset of parabolic elements in
\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{C}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
for each
i
.
Therefore there is a continuous path
\omega_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
\omega_{t}\cdot\rho_{t}
converges to the trivial representation in
\mathcal{R}
.

We have completed the proof for all cases.

3.2

3.1.
Approximation of moduli

Let
E
be a singular Euclidean surface induced by a holomorphic quadratic differential on a Riemann surface
X
.
A
regular annulus

A_{E}
is a cylinder embedded in
E
such that there is a closed geodesic loop
\ell
on
E
and the annulus
A_{E}
is foliated by loops equidistant from
\ell
.
Minsky gave a useful approximation of the modulus of cylinders.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
Min92
]
, Theorem 4.6; see also
[
Ser12
]
, Theorem 6.2)
.

Let
E
be a singular Euclidean surface induced by a holomorphic quadratic differential on a Riemann surface
X
.
There are constant
0<c<1
depending on the topology of the surface, such that, for every essential annulus
A
embedded in
X
, there is a regular annulus
A_{E}
in
E
homotopy equivalent to
A
satisfying

\operatorname{Mod}(E_{A})>c\operatorname{Mod}(A)
.

4.
Holonomy estimates away from zeros

In this section, based on Dumas’ work on Epstein surfaces
[
Dum17
]
, we give some further analysis of the Epstein surfaces in the horizontal direction.
We use Dumas’ notations as below.
Let
g_{1}=e^{\alpha_{1}}|dz|,g_{2}=e^{\alpha_{2}}|dz|
be two conformal metrics on a Riemann surface; then the Schwarzian derivative of
g_{2}
relative to
g_{1}
is the quadratic differential

B(g_{1},g_{2})=[(\alpha_{1})_{zz}-{\alpha_{2}}_{z}^{2}-(\alpha_{1})_{zz}+(\alpha_{1})_{z}^{2}]dz^{2}.

Let
C=(X,q)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
.
Then, we set the following notations associated with
C
:

•

Let
\tau
be the hyperbolic metric on
S
uniformizing
X
;

•

let
|\sqrt{q}|
denote the singular Euclidean metric on
X
obtain by integrating
\sqrt{q}
along paths;

•

let
g_{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}}
be the spherical metric on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
given by some conformal identification
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
;

•

let
f\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
be the developing map of
C
, and
f^{\ast}(g_{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}})
be the pull back of the conformal metric
g_{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}}
by
f
to the universal cover
\tilde{X}
.

Then set

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2B(\tau,f^{\ast}(g_{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}})),

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2B(|\sqrt{q}|,f^{\ast}(g_{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}})),

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2B(\sigma,\sqrt{q}),

which are holomorphic quadratic differentials on
\tilde{X}
.

4.1.
Curvature of Epstein surfaces in the horizontal direction

Let
k_{h}
and
k_{v}
be the principle curvatures of
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
in the horizontal and the vertical directions, respectively.
First
by Equation 3.7 in
[
Dum17
]

k_{v}=\frac{|\hat{\omega}|-|\omega|}{|\hat{\omega}|+|\omega|}.

As the Gaussian curvature
\kappa_{h}\kappa_{v}=1
(
[
Dum17
, p448]
), we have

k_{h}=\frac{|\hat{\omega}|+|\omega|}{|\hat{\omega}|-|\omega|}.

In addition, recalling that
h^{\prime}
denotes a unit tangent vector in the horizontal direction at a non-singular point, we have

\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}h\textquoteright\|^{2}=\frac{{(|\hat{\omega}|-|\omega|)^{2}}}{2|\omega\hat{\omega}|}

(Equation 3.6 in
[
Dum17
, p448]
).
Therefore

\displaystyle(k_{h}\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}(h\textquoteright)\|)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{|\hat{\omega}|+|\omega|}{|\hat{\omega}|-|\omega|})^{2}\cdot\frac{{(|\hat{\omega}|-|\omega|)^{2}}}{2|\omega\hat{\omega}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{(|\hat{\omega}|^{2}+|\omega|^{2})}{2|\omega\hat{\omega}|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{2}(\frac{|\hat{\omega}|}{|\omega|}+\frac{|\omega|}{|\hat{\omega}|}).

Since
\hat{\omega}=\omega-\nu
(
[
Dum17
, p447]
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\hat{\omega}}{\omega}\right|=\left|1-\frac{\nu}{\omega}\right|\,\text{ (\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dumas18HolonomyLimit}{}{}, p449]})}.

By
[
Dum17
, Lemma 2.6]
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\nu(z)}{\omega(z)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}\,.

Thus, recalling that
d(z)
is the distance from the singular points, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\hat{\omega}(z)|}{|\omega(z)|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+O(d(z)^{-2}),\text{\penalty\ and}

\displaystyle(k_{h}(z)\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}(h\textquoteright(z))\|)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2+O(d(z)^{-2}).

Therefore, we have the following.

Lemma 4.1
.

For all nonzero
z\in\tilde{X}
of the differential
\tilde{q}
,

\displaystyle k_{h}(z)\|\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}(h\textquoteright(z))\|=\sqrt{2}+O(d(z){{}^{-2}}).

4.2.
Holonomy estimates of long flat cylinders

Let
E
be a singular Euclidean surface.
A
flat cylinder
in
E
is a cylinder foliated by closed geodesics.
A cylinder
A
in
E
is
expanding
if there is a geodesic loop
\ell
or a puncture
p
on
E
, such that
A
is foliated by a one-parameter family of circles equidistant from
\ell
or
p
, respectively, whose length strictly increases as the distance to
\ell
or
p
increases.

Let
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the Epstein surface of a projective structure
C=(X,q)
on
S
.
Let
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\tilde{C}\cong\tilde{X}
be an arc such that
\alpha(0)
and
\alpha(1)
are in
\tilde{X}\setminus\tilde{Z}
and
\alpha
differentiable at both endpoints.
Then the curve
\operatorname{Ep}\circ\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
is differentiable at both endpoints.
Let
\zeta(\alpha)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be such that
\zeta(\alpha)
takes the unit tangent vector
\alpha^{\prime}(0)
to
\alpha^{\prime}(1)
on
\operatorname{Ep}
and the unit normal
\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}\alpha(0)
to the unit normal
\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}\alpha(1)
.
We call
\zeta(\alpha)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
the
holonomy (of
\operatorname{Ep}
) along

\alpha
.

Definition 4.2
.

For
\alpha\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, the rotation angle in
[0,\pi]
is the (unsigned) rotation angle of the tangent plane of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
at a fixed point of
\alpha
.

In the case that
\alpha
has two fixed points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, then the “signed” rotation angle of
\alpha
which takes a value in
[-\pi,\pi]/(\pi\sim-\pi)
at a fixed point is
-1
times the “signed” rotation angle at the other fixed point, where the sign is determined by the orientation from
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
; thus the unsigned rotation angle is well-defined in
Definition

4.2
.

Let
(E,V)
be the singular Euclidean surface given by
C=(X,q)
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a
C^{1}
-smooth arc on the Epstein surface
\tilde{E}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Let
v(t)
and
h(t)
denote the (unit) vector fields along
\alpha
tangent to the vertical and horizontal foliations of
E
, respectively.

Let
\ell
be a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the foliation of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
by the totally geodesic hyperbolic planes
H
orthogonal to
\ell
.
Note that these hyperbolic planes are isometrically identified by parallel transport along
\ell
, and thus their ideal boundary circles are also identified diffeomorphically.

Suppose that
v(t)
is transversal to the foliation
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
H_{t}
be the leaf of
\mathcal{H}
containing
\alpha(t)
.
The
translation length
of
\alpha
along
\ell
is the distance between
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
(i.e. the length of the segment of
\ell
between
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
).

As
v(t)
is transversal to
\mathcal{H}
, then, by the orthogonal projection
\mathbb{H}^{3}\to H_{t}
, the horizontal tangent vector
h(t)
projects to a non-zero vector at the tangent space
T_{\alpha(t)}H_{t}
.
This non-zero tangent vector determines a geodesic ray in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
by being its initial tangent direction.
Let
\theta(t)\in\partial_{\infty}H_{t}
be the endpoint of the geodesic ray in
H_{t}
given by the tangent vector.
As all ideal boundaries
\partial_{\infty}H_{t}
are identified,
\theta(t)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
lifts to
\tilde{\theta}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
.
The
rotation angle
of
\alpha
about
\ell
is the total increase of
\tilde{\theta}(t)
, which takes a value in
\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
C_{i}=(f_{i},\rho_{i})
be a sequence of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
, and let
(E_{i},V_{i})
be the pair of a singular Euclidean structure
E_{i}
and a vertical foliation
V_{i}
on
E_{i}
induced by the Schwarzian parameters of
C_{i}
.
Suppose that there are a loop
m
on
S
, a geodesic representative
m_{i}
of
m
on
E_{i}
for each
i
, and a flat cylinder
A_{i}
in
E_{i}
contains
m_{i}
, such that

•

m_{i}
is in the middle of
A_{i}
, so that
A_{i}\setminus m_{i}
is a union of two isometric flat cylinders,

•

\operatorname{Mod}(A_{i})\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
, and

•

the height
a_{i}
of
A_{i}
diverges to
\infty
as
i\to\infty
.

Let
\bar{m}_{i}
be a segment on the universal cover
\tilde{E}_{i}
obtained by lifting the simple closed curve
m_{i}
.
Then, by parametrizing
\bar{m}_{i}
by arc length
s\in[0,\operatorname{length}(m_{i})]
, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
i>0
is sufficiently large, then

(1)

the translation length of
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}\bar{m}_{i}(s)
along
\ell_{i}
is
(1+\epsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz to
\sqrt{2}\,({\rm Re}\int_{m_{i}}\sqrt{q_{t}})
,

(2)

the total rotation angle of
\operatorname{Ep}\bar{m}_{i}
about
\ell_{i}
is
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz to to
\sqrt{2}\,(\operatorname{Im}\int_{m_{i}}\sqrt{q_{t}})
, and

Proof.

Isotope
m_{i}
in
A_{i}
, fixing a point on
m_{i}
, so that
m_{i}
is a union of a vertical segment
u_{i}
and a horizontal segment
w_{i}
(Figure
1
).
Then
m_{i}
remains close to the middle of
A_{i}
.

\end{overpic}

Figure 1.
Isotope
m_{i}
to a union of a vertical and horizontal segment.

We first analyze the vertical segment
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}|u_{i}
.
In the principal direction, the normal vector is preserved by parallel transports.
Thus, the parallel transport along the curve
\Sigma_{i}|u_{i}
yields the holonomy
\zeta_{i}(u_{i}(s))\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
By the hypotheses, the distance from the loop
u_{i}\cup w_{i}
and the set
Z_{i}
of zeros of the differential
q_{i}
diverges to
\infty
.
Therefore, by Lemma
2.5
(
4
), the curvature along
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}|u_{i}
limits to zero, and it asymptotically has a constant speed
\sqrt{2}
by
Lemma

2.5
(
2
), so that its length is
\sqrt{2}
times the Euclidean length of
u_{i}
, which yields (
1
).

To analyze the total rotation angle in the vertical direction, we next consider the total curvature.
In a more general setting, the following holds.

Lemma 4.5
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then, if a vertical segment
u
on a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-surface
C
has Euclidean length less than
R/\epsilon
, then
total curvature of the curve
\operatorname{Ep}|u
is less than
\epsilon
, where
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{C}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
is the Epstein surface of
C
.

Proof.

The curvature of the curve
\operatorname{Ep}|u
at every point on
u
is bounded from
\frac{6}{R^{2}}
by Lemma
2.5
(
4
).
Since, by the hypothesis, the length of
u
is bounded from above by
\frac{R}{\epsilon}
, the total curvature along
u
is bounded from above by

\frac{R}{\epsilon}\cdot\frac{6}{R^{2}}=\frac{6}{\epsilon R}.

Therefore, if
R>\frac{6}{\epsilon^{2}},

then the total curvature along
u
is bounded from above by
\epsilon
.
∎

In our current setting,
as
a_{i}\to\infty
and
\operatorname{Mod}(A_{i})\to\infty
, one can easily show that, for every
\epsilon
, the vertical segment
u_{i}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

4.5
when
i
is sufficiently large.
Thus the following corollary holds.

Corollary 4.6
.

The total (principal) curvature of the vertical segment
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}|u_{i}
limits to zero as
i\to\infty
.

We next show that the rotational holonomy along
u_{i}
asymptotically vanishes as
i\to\infty
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then, if a vertical segment
v
on a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-surface
C
has length less than
R/\epsilon
and a distance at least
R
from the singular set w.r.t. the singular Euclidean structure of
C
, then, letting
\operatorname{Ep}
be its Epstein surface,
the derivative of rotation of its
\operatorname{Ep}
-image is bounded from above by
\epsilon
.
Moreover, the total rotation of its
\operatorname{Ep}
-image bounded from above by
\epsilon
with respect to the geodesic
\ell
connecting the endpoints of
\operatorname{Ep}
.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon>0
.
Let
v
be a vertical segment on
C
of length less than
R/\epsilon
.
Let
\alpha\colon[0,\ell]\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the curve
\operatorname{Ep}\circ v
, where
\ell
is the Euclidean length of
v
.
Let
s(t)
be the geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
\alpha(0)
and
\alpha(t)
for each
t\in[0,\ell]
.
For
u\in[0,\ell]
, let
\operatorname{Ep}(u)
be the surface which
s(t)
sweeps out over
t\in[0,u]
, so that
\operatorname{Ep}(u)
is bounded by
\alpha([0,u])
and the geodesic segment
s(u)
connecting its endpoints.
Then, the intrinsic metric of
\operatorname{Ep}(u)
is a hyperbolic surface.
Then, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then
\operatorname{Ep}(u)
isometrically embeds into a hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
so that its image is bounded by a geodesic segment isometric to
s(u)
and a curve isometric to
\alpha(u)
.
The curvature of the second segment is bounded from above the curvature of
\alpha|[0,u]
at every point.

Therefore, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then the area of
\operatorname{Ep}
is less than
\epsilon
by the Gauss-Bonnet theorem to
\operatorname{Ep}
, since the total curvature
\alpha
is small.
Let
n(t)
denote the unit normal vector
\operatorname{Ep}_{\ast}
at
u(t)
.
Let
n^{\prime}(t)
be the parallel transport of
n(t)
along the geodesic segment
s(t)
, so that
n^{\prime}(t)
be a tangent vector at
\alpha(0)
.
By the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the norm of the derivative
dn^{\prime}(t)/dt
is bounded from above by the curvature of
\alpha
and the derivative of the area of
\operatorname{Ep}(t)
(
Figure

2
).
Thus, the total rotation of
n^{\prime}(t)
from
t=0
to
t=\ell
is bounded from above by the sum of the total curvature of
\alpha
and the total area of
\operatorname{Ep}
.
Therefore, by the combination of the small upper bounds above if
R>0
is sufficiently large, the total rotation is bounded by
\epsilon
.

\put(70.0,18.0){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n(t)$ }\hss}\mbox{$n(t)$ } }
\put(-10.0,20.0){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$n^{\prime}(t)$ }\hss}\mbox{$n^{\prime}(t)$ } }
\end{overpic}

Figure 2.
Infinitesimal change of the rotation angle
n^{\prime}(t)
.

∎

Next we analyze the holonomy along the horizontal segment
w_{i}
.
By Lemma
2.5
(
1
),

\operatorname{length}_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\operatorname{Ep}_{i}w_{i}<\frac{6\operatorname{length}_{E_{i}}m_{i}}{(a_{i}/3)^{2}}\to 0,

as
i\to\infty.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
v_{i}(t)
denote the tangent vector of
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}
at
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}w_{i}(t)
in the direction of
V_{i}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is large enough, then
along
w_{i}
,
\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}w_{i}(t)
is contained in an
\epsilon
-ball in the unit tangent bundle
{\rm T^{1}}\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{E}_{i}
be the universal cover of
E_{i}
.
Pick a lift
\tilde{u}_{i}
of the vertical segment
u_{i}
in
E_{i}
to
\tilde{E}_{i}
.
Let
R_{i}
be a Euclidean rectangle, in
\tilde{E_{i}}
, bounded by vertical and horizontal edges, such that
w_{i}
divides
R_{i}
into two isometric rectangles of half height (Figure
3
, left).
We may in addition assume that the height of
R_{i}
divided by the width of
R_{i}
goes to zero as
i\to\infty
.

The vertical foliation
V_{i}
and the horizontal foliation
H_{i}
of
E_{i}
induce a vertical and a horizontal foliation of
R_{i}
.
By Lemma
2.5
(
2
), for every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is large enough, the restrictions of
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}
to vertical leaves in
R_{i}
are
(\sqrt{2}-\epsilon,\sqrt{2}+\epsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz.
By Lemma
2.5
(
1
), the
\operatorname{Ep}_{i}
-images of the horizontal leaves in
R_{i}
have diameters less than
\epsilon
.
Therefore, for sufficiently large
i
, the images of vertical leaves of
R_{i}
are pairwise
\epsilon
-close in the Hausdorff metric (Figure
3
below).
As
v_{i}
is tangent to the image of such a vertical leaf, we have the lemma.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/ImHorizontalSegment] \put(10.0,28.0){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$R_{i}$}\hss}\mbox{$R_{i}$}}
