\put(10.0,52.0){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}\hss}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$w_{i}$}}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3.

∎

We have already shown a good approximation of the holonomy along the vertical segment
u_{i}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then the translation length along
u_{i}
is
(1+\epsilon)
-bilipschitz to
\sqrt{2}
times the Euclidean length of
u_{i}
and the rotation is less than
\epsilon
(
Lemma

4.7
).
On the other hand, by Proposition
4.8
and
Lemma

4.1
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then the total rotation along the horizontal segment
w_{i}
is
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz to
\sqrt{2}
times the Euclidean length of
w_{i}
and the translation is less than
\epsilon
.
Thus we obtained, (
1
) and (
2
).

4.4

4.3.
The exponential map and Epstein surfaces

Recall that, given a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C=(X,q)
on
S
, for
x\in C
,
\penalty\ d(x)
is the Euclidean distance from the singular set of the singular Euclidean structure
E
induced by the holomorphic quadratic differential
q
.
Note that, if
x\in C
is not a singular point of
E
, then there is a neighborhood
U
of
x
in
E
so that
U
is isometrically embedded in the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}
so that
vertical leaves of
E
in
U
map into horizontal lines of
\mathbb{C}
, and horizontal leaves map into vertical lines.

Consider the
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
Its domain
\mathbb{C}
is isometrically identified with the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{E}^{2}
, and the codomain
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
admits a push-forward Euclidean metric.
Note that this induced Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
is invariant under the dilations
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}:z\mapsto kz
for all
k\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
Therefore, given, any two distinct points
p,q
in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, by a conformal mapping from
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}
to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, the complement
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}
has the push-forward Euclidean metric.
By abuse of notation, we denote this composition by
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\setminus\{p,q\}
and call it the
normalized exponential map.

Let
(p,q)
be the geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
p
to
q
.
Recalling that
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
is the ideal boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
,
let
\Psi\colon\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}\to(p,q)
be the orthogonal projection along a geodesic rays in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Let
\Psi_{\ast}\colon\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}\to T^{1}\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the map taking
z\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}
to the unit tangent vector at
\Psi(z)\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
which is tangent to the geodesic ray from
\Psi(z)
to
z\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

For
r>0
, let
Q_{r}(z)
be a
r
-neighborhood of a point
z
of the singular Euclidean surface
E
in the
L^{\infty}
-metric (w.r.t. the vertical and the horizontal directions).
If
Q_{r}(z)
contains no singular point, then it is a square with horizontal and vertical edges of length
2r
.

Proposition 4.9
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
R>0
such that, if
z\in\tilde{C}
satisfies
d(z)>R
, then we have a normalized exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p,q\}
and can isometrically embed the
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
-neighborhood
Q_{1/\epsilon}(z)
of
z
in
\mathbb{C}
exchanging the horizontal and the vertical directions, such that, in the
C^{0}
-metric,

(1)

the restriction of the Epstein surface
\Sigma
to
Q_{1/\epsilon}(z)
is
\epsilon
-close to
w\mapsto\Psi_{\ast}\exp(\frac{w}{\sqrt{2}})
,

(2)

the restriction of
\Sigma_{\ast}
to
Q_{1/\epsilon}(z)
of
z
is
\epsilon
-close to
w\mapsto\Psi_{\ast}\exp(\frac{w}{\sqrt{2}})
, and

(3)

the restriction of the developing map
f
to
Q_{1/\epsilon}(z)
is
\epsilon
-close to the normalized exponential map.

Proof.

we prove the desired approximations by showing them along all leaves of the restriction of the vertical foliation
V
and the horizontal foliation
H
to the square
Q_{1/\epsilon}(z)
.

For every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
,
by Lemma

2.5
and
Lemma

4.7
, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then

(i)

the restriction of
\Sigma
to each leaf of the vertical foliation
V
in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(z)
is a smoothly
(\sqrt{2}-\epsilon^{\prime},\sqrt{2}+\epsilon^{\prime})
-bilipschitz embedding,

(ii)

the restriction of
\Sigma
to each leaf of the horizontal foliation
H
in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(z)
has derivative less than
\epsilon^{\prime}
, and

(iii)

the derivative of the rotation of
\Sigma_{\ast}
along a vertical leaf in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{\prime}}}(z)
is bounded from above by
\epsilon^{\prime}
, and the total rotation along the leaf is also bounded from above by
\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Pick a vertical leaf
v_{0}
in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
, and let
\ell
be the geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
passing through the endpoints of the
(\sqrt{2}-\epsilon^{\prime},\sqrt{2}+\epsilon^{\prime})
-bilipschitz curve
\Sigma|v_{0}.

We normalize the exponential map with respect to the endpoints of this geodesic.
Then (
i
) and (
ii
) implies (
1
) with this normalization.

We next show (
3
).
We first analyze
f
on each vertical leaf.
By (
i
) and (
iii
), the restriction of the developing map
f
to
v_{0}
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the normalized exponential map, by isometrically embedding
e
onto
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}
in the scaled Euclidean metric
\sqrt{2}E
(i.e. the metric on
v_{0}
is scaled by
\sqrt{2}
).

The
\Sigma
-images of horizontal segments are very short curves in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Therefore, for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then for each vertical leaf
v
of
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
, the restriction of
f
to
v
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the restriction of the normalized exponential map to a vertical segment in
\mathbb{C}
by isometrically embedding
v
w.r.t.
\sqrt{2}E
.

Next, we analyze
f
on horizontal leaves.
Let
h
be a horizontal leaf in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
.
Consider the vector field along
h
consisting of the unit vectors in the vertical direction.
Then, for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, if
R>0
is sufficiently large, then, as in the proof of
Proposition

4.8
,
the image of the tangent vectors are
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to each other in the
C^{0}
-topology.
By the curvature estimate along the horizontal direction in
Lemma
4.1
, for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
if
R>0
is large enough, the amount of the total rotation of
f
along every horizontal segment in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
is close to the horizontal length times
\sqrt{2}
.
Therefore, a restriction of
f
to every horizontal segment
h
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the restriction of
\exp
when
h
is isometrically embedded onto a horizontal segment after scaling the length of
h
by
\sqrt{2}
.
Therefore, a restriction of
\Sigma_{\ast}
to every horizontal segment
h
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the restriction of
\Psi_{\ast}\exp
when
h
is isometrically embedded onto a horizontal segment w.r.t the
\sqrt{2}E
-metric.

We proved that the restrictions of
f
to horizontal and vertical leaves in
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
are
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the normalized exponential map when
Q_{\frac{1}{\epsilon}}(z)
is isometrically embedded in
\mathbb{C}
.
This immediately implies (
3
).

Finally (
1
) and (
3
) immediately imply (
2
), since
f(z)
and
\Psi(z)
determines
\Psi_{\ast}(z)
.

4.9

5.
Holonomy maps for surfaces with punctures

5.1.
Classification of cusps of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures

Definition 5.1
.

Let
F
be a surface with punctures.
A
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
is a pair
(X,q)
of a Riemann surface structure
X
on
F
and a holomorphic quadratic differential
q
, such that at each puncture of
X
,
q
is at most a pole of order two.

This class is a natural class to consider, especially in our setting due to the upper injectivity radius bound (see Theorem
6.1
).

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
F
be a closed surface with at least one puncture
c
such that the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
Let
C=(f,\rho)
denote a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
expressed by a developing pair.
Denote by
\ell_{c}
the peripheral loop around
c
.
Let
C\cong(\tau,L)
denote Thurston parameters, and
(E,V)
be the singular Euclidean structure
E
with the vertical foliation
V
given by the Schwarzian parameters of
C
.

(1)

Suppose that a cusp neighborhood of
c
in
E
is an expanding cylinder of infinite modulus shrinking towards
c
.
Then

•

\rho(\ell_{c})
is parabolic,

•

c
has a horodisk quotient neighborhood, and

•

in Thurston parameters
(\tau,L)
,
c
also has a horodisk quotient neighborhood where the lamination
L
is the empty lamination.

(2)

Suppose that a cusp neighborhood of
c
in
E
is a (half-infinite) flat cylinder
F
of infinite modulus.
Then exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

The circumferences of
F
are not orthogonal to
V
,
\rho(\ell_{c})
is hyperbolic, and
\sqrt{2}\int_{\ell_{c}}\sqrt{q}
is its complex translation length.

In Thurston parameters, the cusp
c
corresponds to boundary component
b
of
\tau
whose length is the real part of the translation length (in
\mathbb{C}/2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
).

(b)

The circumferences of
F
are orthogonal to
V
.

•

If
\sqrt{2}\,V(\ell_{c})
is not a
2\pi
-multiple, then
\rho(\ell_{c})
is an elliptic element of angle
\sqrt{2}\int_{\ell_{c}}\sqrt{q}\ (\in\mathbb{R})
.
In Thurston parameters,
c
is a cusp of
\tau
and the total weight of leaves of
L
around
c
(counted with multiplicity) is, modulo
2\pi
, equal to
the rotation angle of
\rho(\ell_{c})
.

•

If
\sqrt{2}\,V(\ell_{c})
is a
2\pi
-multiple, then
\rho(\ell_{c})
is either the identity
I
or a parabolic element.
In Thurston parameters,
c
is a cusp of
\tau
and the total weight of
L
around
c
is the
2\pi
-multiple.

In (
2b
), by “counted with multiplicity”, we mean that, if a single leaf of
L
has both endpoints at
c
, the weight of the leaf is counted twice.

Proof.

(
1
)
We first describe an intuition, and then make it precise.
As the Euclidean distance to the cusp is finite in
E
, in the hyperbolic metric on
X
, the quadratic differential
q
vanishes asymptotically towards the cusp
c
.
A Riemann surface with the zero differential (in our parametrization) corresponds to a hyperbolic structure.

To make it precise, for
t>0
, let
D_{t}
be the punctured disk of radius
t
centered at
c
.
Note that the
c
may be the zero of the quadratic differential
q
induced by
C
.
Thus, if
t>0
is small enough,
D_{t}
is a union of the Euclidean semi-disks of radius
t
foliated by geodesics parallel to the diameter segment.
Consider the restriction of
q
to
D_{t}
.
Then, by conformally identifying a once-punctured unit disk with
D_{t}
, the holomorphic quadratic differential on
D_{t}
, the differential goes to zero uniformly on every compact subset as
t\to\infty
.

The solution of the Schwarzian equation depends continuously in the differential.
As a punctured disk with the zero differential corresponds to a hyperbolic structure
h
with a cusp at the puncture, and the holonomy around the cusp is parabolic.
Therefore, the developing map of
D_{t}
converges to the developing map of the hyperbolic cusp-neighborhood structure
h
, which is a quotient of horodisk by the infinite cyclic group generated by a parabolic element.
By the equivariance property of the developing maps, the holonomy of
D_{t}
around the cusp must converge to a parabolic element, and as the holonomy of
D_{t}
around the cusp is independent of
t>0
, the holonomy is genuinely parabolic.
Moreover, if one deforms a little bit the hyperbolic structure
h
on the punctured disk to any other
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on the punctured disk keeping the holonomy around the cusp parabolic, it still contains a horodisk quotient as a cusp neighborhood.
Therefore
c
has a horodisk quotient neighborhood in
C
.

In Thurston parameters,
c
is a cusp of
\tau
, and
L
is the empty lamination in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
c
.

(
2
) By
Proposition

4.9
, the developing map of the half-infinite flat cylinder becomes closer and closer to the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
as a point in the domain approaches the cusp, where, in the domain
\mathbb{C}
, the vertical direction corresponds to the real direction and the horizontal direction corresponds to the imaginal direction (to be precise, the
\exp
is composed with the calling to the domain
\mathbb{C}
by
\sqrt{2}
).
Thus the assertions about the holonomy along
\ell_{c}
hold.

It remains only to show the description in Thurston parameters.

(
2a
)
By Proposition
4.4
,
outside of a large compact set of
F
, all circumferences of
F
are admissible loops.
Therefore an appropriate neighborhood of
c
corresponds to an infinite grafting cylinder.
By
[
Bab17
, Proposition 8.3]
, the hyperbolic surface
\tau
has a (possibly open) boundary component corresponding to
c
, and its boundary length is indeed the translation length of the hyperbolic element
\rho(\ell_{c})
.

(
2b
)
The developing map in an appropriate cusp neighborhood is the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
so that the deck transformation corresponds to the translation in the imaginary direction in the domain.

Therefore,
c
is a cusp of
\tau
and the total weight of leaves of
L
near
c
must be the length of the circumference times
\sqrt{2}
(Proposition
4.4
(
2
)).

5.2

5.2.
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
and fixed points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}

In order to construct an appropriate holonomy map for a surface with punctures, we will make
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
slightly bigger as a topological space, by carefully pairing its elements with their fixed points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Let
(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
denote the set of unordered pairs of points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Let

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

be the set of all pairs
(\gamma,\Lambda)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}\times((\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2})
such that

•

if
\gamma
is a hyperbolic element with zero rotation (i.e.
\operatorname{tr}\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2]
when
\gamma
is lifted to
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
), then
\Lambda
is a pair of (not necessarily distinct) fixed points of
\gamma
, and

•

otherwise,
\Lambda
is the pair
(a,a)
of identical fixed points
a\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
of
\gamma
.

We call the pair
\Lambda
a
framing
.
In particular, if
\gamma=I
, then
\Lambda
can be
(a,a)
for any
a\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
The second case also includes the case where
\gamma
is a hyperbolic element with non-zero torsion.
(By abuse of notation, if
\Lambda
is a pair
(a,a)
of identical points on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, for simplicity, we may regard
\Lambda
as a single point
a
.)

Fock and Goncharov introduced a framing of a representation, which equivariantly assigns a single fixed point to each peripheral element (
[
FG06
]
).
What is new here is that we are assigning a pair of fixed points in the first case.

Next we define a (non-Hausdorff) topology on

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

by the following open base of neighborhoods at each
(\gamma,\Lambda)\in\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}
.

•

If
\gamma
is hyperbolic, then,
for every (small) connected neighborhood
U
of
\gamma
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
consisting of hyperbolic elements,
the set of all pairs
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}
such that

–

if
\operatorname{tr}\gamma
is real and
\sharp\Lambda=2
, then for
\gamma^{\prime}\in U
with
\operatorname{tr}\gamma^{\prime}
real,
\sharp\Lambda^{\prime}=2
, and

–

otherwise
\gamma^{\prime}\in U
and,
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a pairs of identical points identified with
\Lambda
by identifying the fixed points of
\gamma
with those of
\gamma^{\prime}
by a path connecting
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
in
U
.

•

If
\gamma
is
not
hyperbolic, then the topology near
(\gamma,\Lambda)
is given by the product topology of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}\times(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
equipped with the Hausdorff topology on
(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Remark 5.3
.

Let
C=(f,\rho)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a surface with punctures.
Let
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(S)
be such that its free homotopy class is the peripheral loop around a cusp
c
of
C
.
Then
\gamma
corresponds to a unique element in
(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as follows:
As the universal cover
\tilde{C}
of
C
is conformally identified with
\mathbb{H}^{2}
by the uniformization, let
\tilde{c}
be the point on the ideal boundary of
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
fixed by
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Let
(\tau,L)\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
be the Thurston parametrization of
C
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be the circular measured lamination on
C
which descends to
L
.
For each leaf
\ell
of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
ending at
\tilde{c}
, the corresponding endpoint of the circular arc
f(\ell)
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
is a fixed point of
\rho(\alpha)
.
If
\mathcal{L}
is non-empty in a small neighborhood of the cusp, let
\Lambda
be the set of such half leaves of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
ending at
c
.
Then
\alpha
corresponds to a unique element
(\rho(\alpha),\Lambda)
in

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

.
If
\mathcal{L}
is empty near the cusp, an appropriate cusp neighborhood of
c
is a horodisk quotient, and
\alpha
corresponds to
(\rho(\alpha),\Lambda)
, where
\Lambda
is the parabolic fixed point of
\rho(\alpha)
.

5.3.
Cusp neighborhoods in Thurston parameters

The following lemma determines the isomorphism classes of cusp neighborhoods of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures in Thurston coordinates.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
C=(f,\rho)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a surface
F
with cusps.
Let
C\cong(\tau,L)
be Thurston parameters of
C
.
Then, for each cusp
c
of
C
, its small neighborhood (i.e. its germ) in
C
is determined by

•

the holonomy
h\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
around
c
,

•

the transversal measure of a peripheral loop around
c
given
L
, and

•

if
h
is hyperbolic, the direction in which the leaves of
L
spirals towards the boundary component.

(See Figure
4
.)

\begin{overpic}[Figure/CuspNeighborhoodsInThurston] \put(0.0,0.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\small elliptic, parabolic, identity}}
\put(45.0,46.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\small hyperbolic}}
\put(60.0,7.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Different spiraling directions}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.
Cusp neighborhoods in Thurston parameters

Proof.

Let
(E,V)
be the pair of a singular Euclidean structure
E
on
F
, and
V
be a vertical foliation on
E
induced by
C
.

Hyperbolic Case.
First suppose that
h\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is hyperbolic.
Then, by Proposition
5.2
, its cusp neighborhood, in
(E,V)
, corresponds to a half-infinite cylinder
A
, and the complex translation length is
\sqrt{2}\int_{\ell_{c}}\sqrt{q}
, where
\ell_{c}
is a peripheral loop of
c
.

The developing map
f
of a small neighborhood of
c
is a restriction of the exponential map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
.
Thus the complex translation length determines the deck transformation on the domain
\mathbb{C}
by
\mathbb{Z}\cong\langle\ell_{c}\rangle
, which determines the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure of a small cusp neighborhood.

The cusp
c
corresponds to the geodesic boundary circle
b
of
\tau
whose length is equal to the translation length of
h
.
By the properties of bending maps, one can show that the total weight of
L
along
\ell_{c}
times
\sqrt{2}
is the rotational angle of
h
and the direction of rotation in which leaves of
L
spiral towards
b
determines the orientation of the angle (Figure
5
).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/DirectionOfTwist] \end{overpic}

Figure 5.
The opposite spiral directions give the holonomy the opposite rotational directions.

Parabolic Case.

Suppose that
h
is parabolic.

If a neighborhood of a cusp
c
in
E
is an expanding cylinder shrinking towards
c
, then a neighborhood of
c
in
(\tau,L)
is a hyperbolic cusp with the empty lamination
(Proposition
5.2
(
1
)).

Next suppose that the cusp neighborhood of
c
in
(E,V)
is a half-infinite flat cylinder
A
in
E
.
Then the circumferences of
A
are orthogonal to
V
, and
\sqrt{2}V(\ell_{c})
is a positive
2\pi
-multiple.

Let
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{X}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the Epstein map associated with
C=(X,q)
.
Let
\tilde{V}
be the pull-back of
V
to the universal cover of
E
, and let
\tilde{c}
be the lift of
c
to the ideal boundary of
\tilde{X}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Let
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(F)
be the element which fixes
\tilde{c}
such that its free homotopy class is
\ell_{c}
.
Then, for every leaf
\ell
of
\tilde{V}
ending at
\tilde{c}
, its image
\operatorname{Ep}(\ell)
is indeed a quasi-geodesic limiting to the parabolic fixed point of
\rho(\gamma)
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, and its curvature of
\operatorname{Ep}(\ell)
converges to zero as it approaches the fixed point by Lemma
2.5
.
Therefore
c
corresponds to a cusp of
\tau
.
By
Proposition

4.4
, the total weight of the leaves must be
\sqrt{2}V(\ell_{c})
.

Elliptic Case.

The proof when
h
is elliptic is similar to the parabolic case.

5.4

Let
D
be the unit closed disk in
\mathbb{C}
centered at the origin
O
.
Let
D^{\ast}=D\setminus\{O\}
, and let
\ell
be the peripheral loop around the origin.
Let
\mathcal{P}(D^{\ast})
denote the space of all developing pairs
(f,h)
for the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
D\setminus\{O\}
(not up to
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
) so that
O
is a cusp and the boundary circle is smooth, where
f\colon\tilde{D^{\ast}}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
is the developing map and
h\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is the holonomy along
\ell
.
Recall from Remark
5.3
that each cusp corresponds to a unique element
(\gamma,\Lambda)
in

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

.
Let
\check{D}^{\ast}
be a subsurface of
D^{\ast}
obtained by removing a regular neighborhood of the boundary circle of
D^{\ast}
.

By the following proposition, the deformation of the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures of the cusp neighborhoods is locally modeled on

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
F
be a closed surface minus finitely many points, and let
C
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
, and pick its developing pair
(f,\rho)
.
Then, each cusp
c
of
C
has a disk neighborhood
\Sigma=(f,\gamma)\in\mathcal{P}(D^{\ast})
of
c
in
C
with the following properties:

(1)

Let
(\gamma,\Lambda)\in\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-441.65944pt\hskip 441.65944pt}
be the element corresponding to the peripheral loop around
c
.
Then, for every
\epsilon>0
and every compact subset
K
of the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
, there is a subset
U=U(K,\epsilon)
of
(\gamma,\Lambda)
in

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

, such that, for every
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in U
,

(a)

if
\sharp\Lambda=1
, then there is
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}(D^{\ast})
with holonomy
\gamma^{\prime}
and the framing
\Lambda
, such that its developing map
f^{\prime}
of
\Sigma(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
is
\epsilon
-close, in
C^{1}
-topology, to the developing map
f
of
\Sigma
in
K
,

(b)

if
\sharp\Lambda=2
, then there is a neighborhood
W
of
\gamma
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, such that, for every
\gamma^{\prime}\in W
, there is
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}(D^{\ast})
with holonomy
\gamma^{\prime}
and a unique framing
\Lambda
, such that its developing map
f^{\prime}
of
\Sigma(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
is
\epsilon
-close, in
C^{1}
-topology, to the developing map
f
of
\Sigma
in
K
.

(2)

Moreover,
\Sigma^{\prime}
is uniquely determined on
\check{D}^{\ast}
by an isotopy of
D^{\ast}
(uniqueness near the cusp).

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

We divide the proof by the isometry type of
\gamma
.
In each case, we construct a deformation of
\Sigma
in a small neighborhood in

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

by specifying the deformation of a fundamental membrane.

Elliptic Case.

First,
suppose that
\gamma=I
or
\gamma
is an elliptic element.
Then the puncture
O
corresponds to a unique point
f(O)
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
by continuously extending
f
.
Then pick a cusp neighborhood
\Sigma
biholomorphic to a punctured disk, such that the development of the boundary circle is a round circle
\alpha
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
and there is a unique Lie subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
isomorphic to
SO(2)
which preserves
\alpha
and
f(O)
.
We identify
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
with
\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
so that the puncture
f(O)
is at the origin and
\alpha
is the unit circle of
\mathbb{C}
centered at the origin
f(O)
.

Pick a “fan-shaped fundamental domain” in
\tilde{D^{\ast}}
bounded by three circular arcs
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
such that

•

f|e_{1}
and
f|e_{2}
are radii of
\alpha
connecting
f(O)
to points on
\alpha
, so that
\gamma f(e_{1})=f(e_{2})
are orthogonal to
\alpha
, and

•

f|e_{3}
immerses into
\alpha
, and it connects the endpoints of
e_{1}
and
e_{2}

(Figure
6
, left).
Let
q
be the endpoint of the arc
f(e_{1})
on
r
.

If the neighborhood
U
of
(\gamma,f(O))
is sufficiently small, then given
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in U
, one can easily construct a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
\Sigma^{\prime}=(f^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
close to
\Sigma
on
D^{\ast}
realizing
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda\textquoteright)
.
Indeed, we pick
z\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, we can construct a fundamental membrane bounded by
e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}
such that,

(1)

f^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime})
is a straight line on
\mathbb{C}
connecting
z
and
fq
,

(2)

f^{\prime}(e_{2}^{\prime})
is
\gamma^{\prime}(f^{\prime}(e_{1}^{\prime}))
(which is a circular arc connecting
z
and
\gamma(q)
),

(3)

f^{\prime}(e_{3}^{\prime})
is an arc connecting
q
to
\gamma(q)
so that
f(e_{3}^{\prime})
is a segment of a trajectory under a one-dimensional Lie subgroup of the affine transformations of
\mathbb{C}
preserving
z
, and

(4)

f^{\prime}(e_{i})
is close to
f(e_{i})
in the Hausdorff topology on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

(see Figure

6
, right).
(The choice of
z
may not be unique if
r
is identity and
\operatorname{tr}r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2]
, i.e. hyperbolic without screw motion)

On the other hand, one can easily see that, for every small deformation
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
, there is a “fan-shaped” fundamental membrane satisfying all conditions (1) - (4) such that the fundamental membranes coincide on
\check{D}^{\ast}
.
Therefore, we have the uniqueness property of
\Sigma^{\prime}
near the cusp.

Generic hyperbolic case.

Let
(\tau,L)
be the Thurston parametrization of
C
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be the Thurston lamination on
C
.
Let
\ell
be the peripheral loop around
O
.
Suppose that
\gamma
is hyperbolic and
L(\ell)\neq 0
, so that
\Lambda
is a single point.
Then
\tau
has a geodesic boundary loop
b
corresponding to the cusp
c
and, as
L(\ell)>0
, leaves of
L
spiral towards
b
.
Let
\tilde{b}
be a lift of
b
to the universal cover
\tilde{\tau}
of
\tau
, so that
\tilde{b}
is a boundary geodesic of
\tilde{\tau}
.
Then those spiraling leaves lift to geodesics in
\tilde{\tau}
having a common endpoint at an endpoint of
\tilde{b}
; by the bending map
\beta\colon\tilde{\tau}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
, the endpoint maps to the point
\Lambda
.
Accordingly, the leaves of
\mathcal{L}
near the cusp
O
develop onto circular arcs ending at
\Lambda
.

Normalize
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
by an element of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, so that
0=\Lambda
and the other fixed point of
\gamma
is at
\infty
.
Let
(E,V)
be the foliated singular Euclidean structure given by
C
.
Then, there is a half-infinite flat cylinder
A
in
E
which corresponds to a cusp neighborhood of
c
; then each circumference has a positive transversal measure given by the horizontal foliation.
Therefore, one can take a cusp neighborhood
\Sigma
bounded by a loop
m
such that
m
develops onto a
spiral
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, i.e. a curve invariant under a one-parameter subgroup in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
which contains
\gamma
.

Take, similarly, a “fan-shaped” fundamental domain
F
in the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
which is bounded by three smooth segments
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
such that

•

e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are half-leaves of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\gamma e_{1}=e_{2}
and the circular arcs
f(e_{1})
and
f(e_{2})
end at
0\in\mathbb{C}
,, and

•

f(e_{3})
is in a segment of the spiral which connects the other endpoints of
f(e_{1})
and
f(e_{2})

(Figure
7
, left).
Then
\gamma(f(e_{1}))=f(e_{2})
by the equivariant property.

Take a sufficiently small neighborhood
U
of
(\gamma,\Lambda)
such that the subset
W\subset{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
of holonomy elements of pairs in
U
consists of only hyperbolic elements closed to
\gamma
; then, for all
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})\in U
, the fixed point
\Lambda^{\prime}
of the hyperbolic element
\gamma^{\prime}
uniquely corresponds to the fixed point of
\gamma
in
\Lambda
by every short path connecting
\gamma^{\prime}
to
\gamma
in
W
.
Then, similarly to the elliptic case, one can easily find a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
D^{\ast}
close to
\Sigma
which realizes
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
, by constructing a fundamental membrane close to
F
(Figure
7
).

On the other hand, for every small deformation
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
of
\Sigma
realizes
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
, one can easily find a fundamental membrane of
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
so that it coincides, on
\check{D}^{\ast}
with that of
\Sigma^{\prime}
constructed above.

Special hyperbolic case (
\sharp\Lambda=2
).

Suppose that
\gamma
is hyperbolic and
L(\ell)=0
(in particular
\operatorname{tr}\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
).
Then the boundary component
b
of
\tau
is a leaf of
L
with weight infinity (
[
Bab17
, Proposition 8.3]
).

Let
\kappa\colon C\to\tau
be the collapsing map.
Then
\kappa^{-1}(b)\eqqcolon F
is a half-infinite cylinder.
The developing map of
F
is the restriction of
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
to a half-space bounded by a horizontal line in
\mathbb{C}
.
Then we identify the universal cover
\tilde{F}
of
F
with the half-space, so that
\gamma
acts as a horizontal translation
t_{\gamma}
.
Take a fundamental domain
Q
in
\tilde{F}
such that
Q
is a vertical half-infinite strip in
\mathbb{C}
bounded by two vertical rays and one horizontal segment (
Figure

8
).

If
W
is a small neighborhood of
\gamma
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
consisting of hyperbolic elements, for every
\gamma^{\prime}\in W
, there is a translation
t_{\gamma^{\prime}}
of
\mathbb{C}
(close to the horizontal translation
t_{\gamma}
), such that
t_{\gamma^{\prime}}
descends to
\gamma^{\prime}
by the exponential map up to
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Therefore, there is a small deformation of
\Sigma
realizing
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
, and (
1
) holds.

On the other hand, arbitrary deformations of the cusp neighborhood
F
contain such a deformation of such a half-infinite strip fundamental domain on
D
.
Moreover, if
U
is sufficiently small, then if there are two
\epsilon
-small deformations of
F
with the same framed holonomy
(\gamma^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
, up so isotopy, the structures on
D^{\ast}
coincide by the
\epsilon
-closeness to
F
.
Thus the uniqueness holds (
2
).

Parabolic case.

Suppose that
\gamma
is parabolic.
Then in Thurston parameters, the puncture corresponds to a cusp of the hyperbolic surface
\tau
, and the total weight of
L
along the peripheral loop
\ell
is a non-negative
2\pi
-multiple.
Then, similarly to the case that
\gamma=I
, we can show the claim
by finding a cusp neighborhood and a fundamental domain in its universal cover which is bounded by circular arcs.

5.5

5.3.1.
Holonomy maps of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures with cusps

Let
F
be a closed surface minus finitely many points
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{P}(F)
denotes the space of all developing pairs
(f,\rho)
for
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
F
.
Let
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
.
Then
(f,\rho)
gives a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
, and we let
X
be its induced complex structure on
F
.
Identify the universal cover
\tilde{X}
of
X
with
\mathbb{H}^{2}
; then for each
i=1,\dots n
, pick a lift
\tilde{p}_{i}
of
p_{i}
to a point on the ideal boundary of
\tilde{X}
.
Then, by Remark
5.3
, for every
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
and a puncture
p_{i}
, we have a corresponding element in
(\gamma_{i},\Lambda_{i})\in\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}
.
Thus, by the definition of the topology of

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

, we have a continuous mapping from
\operatorname{hol}\colon\mathcal{P}(F)\to(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt})^{n}\times\mathcal{R}(F)
taking
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
to
((\gamma_{i},\Lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\rho)
.
In fact,
\operatorname{hol}
yields a holonomy theorem in our setting.

Theorem 5.6
.

Every
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
has a neighborhood
W
such that

\operatorname{hol}|W

is a local homeomorphism onto its image.
Moreover, for any
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
, if there is a path
\rho_{t}\penalty\ (t>0)
converging to
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}(F)
as
t\to\infty
, then there is a lift of
\rho_{t}
to a path in
\mathcal{P}(F)
for
t\gg 0
converging to
(f,\rho)
.

Remark 5.7
.

The image of
\operatorname{hol}(W)
is contained in

\{\,((\gamma_{i},\Lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\rho)\mid\rho\in W,\rho(\alpha_{i})=\gamma_{i}\,(i=1,2,\dots,n)\,\}.

Furthermore, its subset cut by the condition on the framing given by
Proposition

5.5
(
1
) determines the local image
\operatorname{hol}(W)
.

Proof.

Let
(f,\rho)\in\mathcal{P}(F)
, and
let
C
be the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
given by the developing pair
(f,\rho)
.
Applying Proposition
5.5
to a small
\epsilon>0
, we obtain, for each
i=1,\dots,n,
a (small) cusp neighborhood
C_{i}
of the puncture
p_{i}
of
C
, and a neighborhood
U_{i}
of
(\gamma_{i},\Lambda_{i})
in

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

modeling the deformation of
C_{i}
.
Let
N_{i}
be the underlying topological cusp neighborhood of the punctured surface
F
supporting
C_{i}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}
are disjoint in
C
.
Let
C_{i}^{\prime}
be an open cusp neighborhood of
p_{i}
smaller than
C_{i}
and
U_{i}
be a subset of

{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

containing
\operatorname{hol}((f,\rho))
given by
Proposition

5.5
(
2
), such that the small deformation of
C_{i}
on
C_{i}^{\prime}
is parametrized the framed holonomy in
U_{i}
.

Let
N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
be a (even smaller) cusp neighborhood of
p_{i}
whose closure is contained in the interior of
N_{i}^{\prime}
.
Let
\check{F}
be
F\setminus\sqcup_{i}N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, and let
\check{C}
be the restriction of
C
to
\check{F}
.
For every
(\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\Lambda_{i}^{\prime})\in U_{i}
, let
C_{i}(\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\Lambda_{i}^{\prime})
denote the unique
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
N_{i}^{\prime}
with the framed holonomy
(\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\Lambda_{i}^{\prime})\in U_{i}
such that
C_{i}(\gamma_{i}^{\prime},\Lambda_{i}^{\prime})
is sufficiently close to
C_{i}
.

We shall regard
(f,\rho)
as a smooth section
\Sigma
of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-bundle
B
over
F
such that
\Sigma
is transversal to the horizontal foliation
H_{\rho}
associated with
\rho
(see for example
[
Gol22
]
)
Let
\check{\Sigma}
be the restriction of
\Sigma
to the bundle over the subsurface
\check{F}
.
Then, there is a neighborhood
U
of
\rho
in the representation variety
\mathcal{R}(F)
such that, for each
\xi\in U
, letting
H_{\xi}
be the horizontal foliation of
B
associated with
\xi
,

\check{\Sigma}
is still transversal to
H_{\xi}
by the openness of transversality; then
\check{\Sigma}
yields a projective structure
\check{C}_{\xi}
on
\check{F}
with holonomy
\xi
.
In this way, we obtain a unique
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
\check{F}
close to
(f,\rho)
on
\check{F}
.
This new structure is unique in a compact subset of
\check{F}
whose interior contains the closure of
F\setminus\sqcup_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}^{\prime}
.

For each
i
, pick any
\Lambda_{i}
in
\operatorname{Fix}\xi_{i}(\gamma_{i})\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
so that
(\xi_{i}(\gamma_{i}),\Lambda_{i})\in U_{i}
.
Then
C_{i}(\xi_{i}(\gamma_{i}),\Lambda_{i})
is its associated deformation.
Then we can glue
\check{C}_{\xi}
and
C_{i}(\xi_{i}(\gamma_{i}),\Lambda_{i})
in the overlapping region, and obtain a desired developing pair for a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
.
Consider the subset
W
in
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\times\mathcal{R}(F)
consisting
(\gamma_{i},\Lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\rho)
satisfying
\rho(\alpha_{i})=\gamma_{i}\,(i=1,2,\dots,n)
; clearly
W
contains
\operatorname{hol}(f,\rho)
.
In this way, given a sufficiently small neighborhood of
\operatorname{hol}((f,\rho))
in this subset
W
, for every element in this neighborhood, we construct a developing pair realizing it.
This new
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
is unique by the uniqueness of the thick part
\check{C}_{\xi}
on
F\setminus\sqcup{N^{\prime}_{i}}
and the uniqueness of the cusp neighborhoods
C_{i}(\xi_{i}(\gamma_{i}),\Lambda_{i})
on
N_{i}^{\prime}
.

Notice that
W
projects to a neighborhood of
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}
.
The path lifting along a path in
\mathcal{R}
easily follows from the construction as
U
is a neighborhood of
\rho
in
\mathcal{R}(F)
.

5.6

6.
Bound on the upper injectivity radius

Recall that
C_{t}=(f_{t},\rho_{t})
is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
structures on
S
such that
C_{t}
diverges to
\infty
and the equivalence class
[\rho_{t}]\eqqcolon\eta_{t}
converges in the character variety as
t\to\infty
.
Recall also that
C_{t}=(X_{t},q_{t})
is the expression in the Schwarzian parameters.

Let
E_{t}
be the singular Euclidean structure on
X_{t}
given by
|q_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}|
.
Let
R(E_{t})\geq 0
denote that the
upper injectivity radius
of
E_{t}
.
In this section we show

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop
M
.
Then the upper injectivity radius
R(E_{t})
of
E_{t}
is bounded from above for all
t\geq 0
.

Immediately we have the following.

Corollary 6.2
.

There is an upper bound for the area of the expanding cylinders in
E_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

The rest of this section is a proof of Theorem
6.1
.
We suppose, to the contrary, that
\limsup R(E_{t})=\infty
and show that
\rho_{t}
cannot converge.
Let
M_{t}
be a geodesic representative of
M
on
E_{t}
(in the Euclidean metric) such that, for every
\epsilon>0
if
t>0
is sufficiently large, then
M_{t}
is contained in the
\epsilon
-thin part of
X_{t}
.
We will find a conformally thick part which is, in the Euclidean metric, bigger than its adjacent thick parts:

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that there is a diverging sequence
(0<)\,t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that
E_{t_{i}}
contains a flat cylinder
A_{t_{i}}
homotopy equivalent to a fixed loop
m
of
M

such that

(1)

\operatorname{Mod}{A_{t_{i}}}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
, and

(2)

the circumference of
A_{t_{i}}
limits to
\infty
(equivalently
\operatorname{Area}A_{t_{i}}\to\infty
) as
i\to\infty
.

Then,
leaves of the vertical foliation
V_{t_{i}}
must be asymptotically orthogonal to the circumferences of
A_{t_{i}}
.

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that
V_{t_{i}}
is
not
asymptotically orthogonal to circumferences.
Then, up to a subsequence, we may assume that there is a limiting angle
\theta_{\infty}\in[0,\pi/2)
between the angle between
V_{t_{i}}
and the circumferences of
A_{t_{i}}
.
Let
m_{t_{i}}
be a geodesic representative of
m
which sits in the middle of
A_{t_{i}}
.
Since
\theta_{\infty}\neq\pi/2
, Hypotheses (
1
) and (
2
) imply that the transversal measure of the horizontal foliation
H_{t_{i}}
along
m_{t_{i}}
diverges to infinity as
i\to\infty
.
By
Proposition

4.9
, the translation length of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m_{t_{i}})
is asymptotically
\sqrt{2}
times the transversal measure.
Therefore, the translation length of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m_{t_{i}})
must diverge to infinity, which contradicts the convergence of
[\rho_{t}]
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose that there are a component
F
of
S\setminus M
and a diverging sequence
(0<)\penalty\ t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that, letting
F_{t_{i}}
be the component of
E_{t_{i}}\setminus M_{t_{i}}
homotopic to
F
on
S
,

•

\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t_{i}}}F_{t_{i}}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
, and.

•

for each boundary component
\ell
of
F
, there is an expanding cylinder
B_{\ell,t_{i}}
in
F_{t_{i}}
bounded by the boundary component
\ell_{i}
of
F_{t_{i}}
homotopic to
\ell
on
S
such that

–

B_{\ell,t_{i}}
shrinks toward
\ell_{i}
, i.e.
\ell_{i}
is the shorter boundary component of
B_{\ell,t_{i}}
, and

–

\operatorname{Mod}B_{\ell,t_{i}}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
.

Then
[\rho_{t_{i}}]|\pi_{1}F
diverges to
\infty
in
\textstyle\chi
as
i\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
k_{i}>0
be such that
k_{i}\operatorname{Area}(F_{t_{i}})=1
for each
i=1,2,\dots
.
Then, as
\operatorname{Area}F_{t_{i}}\to\infty
, thus
k_{t_{i}}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
.
All ends of
F_{t_{i}}
have conformally long expanding cylinders shrinking towards adjacent components.
Take a base point in the thick part of
F_{t_{i}}
.
Let
\hat{F}
denote the compact surface with finitely many punctures, obtained by pinching the boundary loops of
F
to puncture points.
Then the space of all holomorphic quadratic differentials on Riemann surfaces structures on
\hat{F}
with Euclidean area one is a sphere of finite dimension.
Then, by compactness, up to a subsequence

•

k_{i}E_{t_{i}}
converges, in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology, to a compact singular Euclidean surface minus finitely many points,
E_{\infty}
, which is homeomorphic to
F
, and

•

the restriction of
k_{i}V_{t_{i}}
to
k_{i}E_{t_{i}}
converges to a measured foliation
V_{\infty}
on
E_{\infty}
.

Take a piecewise geodesic loop
\ell
on
E_{\infty}
such that

(1)

\ell
does not cross any singular point of
E_{\infty}
,

(2)

each segment of
\ell
is either vertical or horizontal, and
\ell
contains at least one vertical segment, and

(3)

\ell
is a geodesic in the
L^{\infty}
-metric, so that at adjacent singular points,
\ell
bends in the different direction by an angle
\pi/2
.

In fact, if
V_{\infty}
contains a periodic leaf, then take it as
\ell
, which obviously satisfies the conditions.
Otherwise,
V_{\infty}
contains a minimal irrational subfoliation, using the density of each leaf in the subfoliation, a standard closing lemma gives a desired loop
\ell
as in
Figure

9
(see
[
CEG87
, I.4.2.15]
).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/ClosingLemma] \end{overpic}

Figure 9.
Staircase closed loops
\ell
consisting of long vertical segments and short horizontal segments

By the convergence
k_{i}E_{i}\to E_{\infty}
,
for
i
large enough, we pick a piecewise geodesic loop
\ell_{i}
on
E_{i}
satisfying the properties (1), (2), (3) such that
\ell_{i}
has the same number of horizontal and vertical segments as
\ell
has,
and
k_{i}\ell_{i}
on
k_{i}E_{i}
converges to
\ell
on
E_{\infty}
smoothly on each segment as
i\to\infty
.
Then the distance from
\ell_{i}
to the singular set of
E_{i}
goes to
\infty
as
k_{i}\to 0
.
Therefore, by
Proposition

4.9
,
\rho_{t_{i}}(\ell)
is a hyperbolic element of translation length close to
\sqrt{2}
times the total length of the vertical segment of
\ell_{i}
.
Then, as
k_{i}\to 0
, the total vertical length of
\ell_{i}
on
E_{i}
goes to infinity, and therefore
\operatorname{tr}\rho_{i}(\ell)
must diverge to infinity.
∎

Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{p}
be the loops of the multiloop
M
.

Proposition 6.5
.

For every (large)
T>0
, there are
t>T
and
k\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that

\frac{1}{2}<\frac{\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}(m_{k})}{\max_{i=1,\dots,p}\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}(m_{i})}\leq 2,

and

\operatorname{Mod}_{E_{t}}(m_{k})
is
\frac{1}{3}
-dominated by an expanding cylinder
B_{k,t}
homotopic to
m_{k}
, i.e.

\frac{\operatorname{Mod}B_{k,t}}{\operatorname{Mod}_{E_{t}}m_{k}}>\frac{1}{3}.

Proof.

For
u>T
, let
m_{k_{u}}
be the loop realizing
\max_{i=1,\dots,q}\operatorname{length}_{E_{u}}(m_{i})
.
We may assume that
\max_{i=1,\dots,p}\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}(m_{i})\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
: in fact, otherwise, since
\limsup R(E_{t})=\infty
, Proposition
6.4
implies that
[\rho_{t}]
diverges in
\textstyle\chi
.

We first show that if a long flat cylinder persists, then its circumference must stay almost the same.
Namely

Claim 6.6
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
K>0
such that, if
there are
w>u>K
and a flat cylinder in
E_{t}
of height at least
K
homotopic to
m
, then, for every
t\in[u,w]
,
then

1-\epsilon<\frac{\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}m}{\operatorname{length}_{E_{u}}m}<1+\epsilon

for all
t\in[u,w].

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.3
,
for every
\epsilon>0
, if
K>0
is sufficiently large, then the vertical foliation
V_{t}
is
\epsilon
-almost orthogonal to circumferences of the flat cylinder homotopic to
m
.
Then, by Proposition
4.4
, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
K>0
is sufficiently large, then the total rotation angle along
m
is
(1+\epsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz to
\sqrt{2}\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}m
for
t\in[u,w]
.
As the holonomy of
\rho_{t}(m)
converges as
t\to\infty
, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
K
is sufficiently large, then the total rotation along
m
must be
\epsilon
-almost constant for all
t\in[u,w]
.
Thus, if
K
is sufficiently large, then the ratio of
\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}m
and
\operatorname{length}_{E_{u}}m
is
\epsilon
-close to
1
.
∎

By Claim
6.6
, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
K>0
is sufficiently large, then, if a flat cylinder
\frac{1}{4}
-dominates
\operatorname{Mod}m_{k_{u}}
for all
t\in[u,w]
for some
u>K
; then

1-\epsilon<\frac{\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}m_{k_{u}}}{\operatorname{length}_{E_{u}}m_{k_{u}}}<1+\epsilon
for all
t\in[u,w]
.
Suppose, in addition, that there is a loop
m_{h}
of
M
not
m_{k_{u}}
, such that
m_{h}
on
E_{t}
becomes exactly twice as long as
m_{k_{u}}
on
E_{u}
for the first time at
t=w<v
after
t=u
.
Then, by applying Claim
6.6
to
m_{h}
, we can show that there is
t\in[u,w)
such that
\operatorname{Mod}_{E_{t}}{m_{h}}
is
1/3
-dominated by an expanding cylinder:
Indeed, otherwise,
\max_{i=1,\dots,p}\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}(m_{i})
must bounded from above by
\frac{3}{2}\operatorname{length}_{E_{t}}(m_{k_{u}})
for all
t\in[u,w]
.

6.5

Corollary 6.7
.

There are a component
F
of
S\setminus M
and a diverging sequence
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that the corresponding component
F_{t_{i}}
of
E_{t_{i}}\setminus M_{t_{i}}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
6.4
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.5
,
there is a loop
m
of
M
and a diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that

•

\operatorname{length}_{E_{t_{i}}}m\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
,

•

\frac{1}{2}<\frac{\operatorname{length}_{E_{t_{i}}}m}{\max\{\operatorname{length}_{E_{t_{i}}}m_{1},\dots,\operatorname{length}_{E_{t_{i}}}m_{p}\}}<2

for all
i=1,2,\dots
, and

•

there is an expanding cylinder
B_{t_{i}}
homotopic
m
which
\frac{1}{3}
-dominates
\operatorname{Mod}_{E_{t_{i}}}m
.

Then, up to a subsequence, we may in addition assume that
B_{t_{i}}
is expanding in the same direction.
Then, let
F
be the connected component of
S\setminus M
such that
m
is a boundary component of
F
and
B_{t_{i}}
expands towards
F
.
As the size of
F_{t_{i}}
becomes bigger and bigger than the length of
\operatorname{length}_{E_{t_{i}}}m
, the first assumption of
Proposition

6.4
holds.
Thus, by the second condition on the loop
m
and the sequence
\{t_{i}\}
, the second assumption of
Proposition

6.4
is satisfied.
∎

By this corollary, we obtained a contradiction by
Proposition

6.4
against the convergence of
\rho_{t}
.
Hence we obtain Theorem
6.1
.

7.
Convergence of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures away from pinched loops

We continue to suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop.
We will first see that the holonomy
\rho_{\infty}(m)
determines the type of a conformally long Euclidean cylinder in
E_{t}
which is homotopic to
m
for
t\gg 0
.

Lemma 7.1
.

(1)

Suppose that there are a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
and a sequence of expanding cylinders
B_{t_{i}}
in
E_{t_{i}}
homotopic to
m
at time
t_{i}
, such that
\operatorname{Mod}_{E_{t_{i}}}B_{t_{i}}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
Then
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.

(2)

Suppose that there is a sequence of flat cylinders
A_{t_{i}}
in
E_{t_{i}}
homotopic to a fixed loop
m
on
S
such that
\operatorname{Mod}{A_{t_{i}}}
diverges to
\infty
and the circumference of
A_{t_{i}}
is bounded from below and above by positive numbers.
Let
w\in\mathbb{C}
be such that the Möbius transformation
z\mapsto(\exp w)z
conjugates to
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
Then,
\sqrt{2}\int_{m}\sqrt{q_{t_{i}}}
converges
to
w\mod 2\pi i
up to a sign.

Proof.

(
1
)
If a puncture of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure corresponds to a regular point of its holomorphic quadratic differential, its peripheral holonomy is parabolic.
Suppose that there are a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
and an expanding cylinder
B_{t_{i}}
in
E_{t_{i}}
homotopic to
m
such that
\operatorname{Mod}{B_{t_{i}}}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
Then, by Corollary
6.2
, the length of the shorter boundary component of
B_{t_{i}}
goes to zero as
i\to\infty
, and it asymptotically corresponds to, at most, a pole of order one of the quadratic differential.
(A pole of order at least two corresponds to an infinite area end.)
Therefore
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic, against the hypothesis.

(
2
) follows immediately from Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Given a compact surface
F
with boundary, let
\hat{F}
denote the surface with punctures obtained by pinching each boundary component of
F
to a (puncture) point.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be a number less than the Bers constant.
Let
F
be a component of
S\setminus M
, and let
F_{t}^{\epsilon}
be the component of the conformally
\epsilon
-thick part of
E_{t}
isotopic to
F
for
t\gg 0
.
Then, if

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t}}(F_{t}^{\epsilon})>0,

there is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
\hat{F}_{t}
on the punctured surface
\hat{F}
such that

(1)

for every
\epsilon>0
, if
t>0
is sufficiently large, then
F_{t}^{\epsilon}
isomorphically embeds into
\hat{F}_{t}
,

(2)

for each boundary component
\ell
of
F
, there is a cylinder
A_{\ell,t}
in
E_{t}
homotopic to
\ell
such that

•

\operatorname{Mod}A_{\ell,t}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
;

•

A_{\ell,t}
is either a flat cylinder for all
t\gg 0
or an expanding cylinder shrinking towards the adjacent component of
S\setminus M
across
m
for all
t\gg 0
;

(3)

\hat{F}_{t}
contains
A_{\ell,t}
for every boundary component
\ell
of
F
.

Proof.

We first show that,
for each boundary component
\ell
of
F
, there is a cylinder
A_{\ell}
homotopic to
\ell
, such that

(i)

\operatorname{Mod}A_{\ell,t}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
, and

(ii)

A_{\ell,t}
remains either a flat cylinder for all sufficiently large
t>0
or an expanding cylinder shrinking forwards
\ell
for all sufficiently large
t>0
,

Let
Y_{t},Z_{t},W_{t}
be disjoint cylinders homotopic to
\ell
, such that
Z_{t}
is a maximal flat cylinder,
Y_{t}
is the maximal expanding cylinder expanding towards the thicker part of
F_{t}
and
W_{t}
is the maximal expanding cylinder expanding towards the adjacent component across the geodesic representative
\ell_{t}
of
\ell
.

As
X_{t}
is pinched along
M
, by
Theorem

3.12
,

\max\{\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t},\operatorname{Mod}Z_{t},\operatorname{Mod}W_{t}\}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
Let
\operatorname{diam}W_{t}
and
\operatorname{diam}Y_{t}
denote the diameters of
W_{t}
and
Y_{t}
, respectively, in the Euclidean metric
E_{t}
.
Then, by
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t}}(F_{t}^{\epsilon})>0
and the upper injectivity radius bound (Theorem
6.1
), the ratio
\frac{diamW_{t}}{diamY_{t}+1}
is bounded from above for all
t>0
.
Thus
\frac{\operatorname{Mod}W_{t}}{\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}+1}
is bounded from above for all
t>0
.
Therefore
\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}+\operatorname{Mod}Z_{t}
diverges to
\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
We claim, moreover, that either
\lim\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}=\infty
or
\lim\operatorname{Mod}Z_{t}=\infty
holds.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose that
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}=\infty
.
Then
\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
be a sequence with
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t_{i}}=\infty
.
Then the circumference of
Z_{t_{i}}
limits to zero, and by Lemma
7.1
(
1
),
\rho_{\infty}(\ell)
is parabolic.

Suppose to the contrary that there is a sequence
s_{1}<s_{2}<\dots
diverges to
\infty
such that

\operatorname{Mod}Y_{s_{i}}
is bounded from above by some constant
b>0
.
Then
\operatorname{Mod}Z_{s_{i}}\to\infty
, and the circumference of
Z_{s_{i}}
is bounded from below
c>0
.
On the other hand, since
\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t_{i}}\to\infty
,
the circumference of
Z_{t_{i}}
goes to zero as
i\to\infty
.
We can assume that
s_{1}<t_{1}<s_{2}<t_{2}<\dots
by taking subsequences of
s_{i}
and
t_{j}
if necessary.

Therefore, for every
r\in(0,c)
, for every sufficiently large
i
, there is
u_{i}\in[s_{i},t_{i}]
, such that the circumference of
Z_{u_{i}}
is
r
.
Then, as
\operatorname{Mod}Y_{u_{i}}
is bounded from above,
\operatorname{Mod}Z_{u_{i}}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
.

Then, by
Lemma

7.1
(
2
), the limit holonomy of
\rho_{u_{i}}(m)
is determined by the complex length of the circumference.
For almost all
r\in(0,c)
,
\rho_{u_{i}}(m)
is not parabolic.
This contradicts the convergence of
\rho_{t}
as
\rho_{\infty}(\ell)
is parabolic.
∎

Then,
Y_{t}
satisfies (
i
) and (
ii
).

Next suppose that
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}<\infty
.
Then
\operatorname{Mod}Z_{t}
diverges to
\infty
as
t\to\infty
, and the circumference of
Z_{t}
converges to a positive number.
Then
Z_{t}
satisfies (
i
) and (
ii
).

We shall construct
\hat{F}_{t}
satisfying (
3
) as follows.
Suppose that, for a boundary component
\ell
of
F
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}=\infty
.
Let
\hat{Y}_{t}
be an expanding cylinder of infinite modulus, obtained by extending the expanding cylinder
Y_{t}
only in the shrinking direction, so that
\hat{Y}_{t}
is conformally a punctured disk.
Then replace
Y_{t}
by
\hat{Y_{t}}
in
E_{t}
by gluing
E_{t}\setminus Y_{t}
and
\hat{Y}_{t}
along the boundary component of
\hat{Y}_{t}
.
Then the boundary component
\ell
of
F
corresponds to the puncture of
\hat{Y}_{t}
.

Next suppose that
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Mod}Y_{t}<\infty
.
Then, since
\operatorname{Mod}Z_{t}\to\infty
and the circumference
\operatorname{Circ}(Z_{t})
converges to a positive number as
t\to\infty
, we extend the flat cylinder
Z_{t}
, in the direction of
W_{t}
,
to the half-infinite flat cylinder
\hat{Z_{t}}
; then
\hat{Z}_{t}
is conformally a punctured disk.
Then replace
Z_{t}
in
E_{t}
with
\hat{Z_{t}}
so that it has a puncture corresponding to
\ell
.

By applying, such a replacement for all boundary component
\ell
of
F
, we obtains a desired complete singular Euclidean surface
\hat{F}_{t}
satisfying (
1
), (
2
), (
3
), as (
2
) follows from (
i
) and (
ii
).

7.2

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
F
be a component of
S\setminus M
.
Let
\epsilon>0
be less than the Bers constant of
S
.
For every
t>0
large enough, let
F_{t}^{\epsilon}
be the component of the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{t}
isotopic to
F
.

(1)

Suppose that

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t}}(F_{t}^{\epsilon})=0.

Then, there is a continuous function
\epsilon_{t}>0
in
t
with
\lim_{t\to\infty}\epsilon_{t}=0
, such that
F_{t}^{\epsilon_{t}}
converges (in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology) to a complete hyperbolic structure on a closed surface with finitely many punctures, denoted by
\hat{F}_{\infty}
, which is homeomorphic to
F
, as
t\to\infty
.

(2)

Suppose that

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t}}(F_{t}^{\epsilon})>0.

Then,
\hat{F}_{t}
accumulates to a bounded subset on the space of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
\hat{F}
.
Moreover, if
\rho_{\infty}(m)\neq I
for each boundary component
m
of
F
, then
\hat{F}_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
\hat{F}
as
t\to\infty
.

Remark 7.5
.

In Case (
2
), similarly to (
1
), one can take a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
so that
\hat{F}_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
\hat{F}_{\infty}
on
\hat{F}
.
Then, for every
\epsilon>0
less than the Bers’ constant, the
\epsilon
-thick part
F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
converge to a subsurface of
\hat{F}_{\infty}
.
If, in addition, the
\rho_{\infty}(m)\neq I
for every boundary component of
F
, then
F_{t}^{\epsilon}
converge to a subsurface of
\hat{F}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

(
1
)
Let
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
be a diverging sequence such that
\operatorname{Area}(F_{t_{i}})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.
Then the holomorphic quadratic differential on
F_{t_{i}}
asymptotically vanishes.
Thus, for every small
\epsilon>0
,
F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
and
X_{t_{i}}|F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
asymptotically identical, where
X_{t_{i}}
is regarded as a hyperbolic surface by the uniformization theorem for each
i
.
Here, by asymptotically identical, we mean that, for every
\upsilon>0
and every compact set
K
in the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
of
X_{t_{i}}
, if
i
is sufficiently large, the developing map of
F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
is
\upsilon
-close to the developing map of the hyperbolic structure
X_{t_{i}}|F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
on
K
.

The holonomy representations of
F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon_{i}}
and
X_{t_{i}}|F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
are asymptotically identical in the character variety.
As the holonomy of
F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon_{i}}
converges in the representation variety, the holonomy of
X_{t_{i}}|F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
must converge in the representation variety.
Thus
X_{t_{i}}|F_{t_{i}}^{\epsilon}
converges to a complete hyperbolic structure
\sigma_{\infty}
on
F
.
Therefore
F_{t}^{\epsilon_{t}}
must genuinely converge to
\sigma_{\infty}
(without taking a subsequence).
In particular
\operatorname{Area}_{E_{t}}F_{t}^{\epsilon_{t}}\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.

(
2
)
Suppose that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\operatorname{Area}F_{t}^{\epsilon}>0
for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Then let
\hat{F}_{t}
denote the singular Euclidean structure on
\hat{F}
obtained from
F_{t}
by Proposition
7.2
.
Then
\hat{F}_{t}
induces a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
\hat{F}
.
Let
(Y_{t},w_{t})
be the Schwarzian parameterization of
\hat{F}_{t}
.
Then, indeed, every puncture of
Y_{t}
is, at most, a pole of order two.

As
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop
M
,
Y_{t}
is bounded in the Teichmüller space
\mathsf{T}(\hat{F})
.
By
Theorem

6.1
, the upper injectivity radius of
\hat{F_{t}}
is also bounded from above, and
(Y_{t},w_{t})
is also bounded in the parameter space.
Thus, the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
\hat{F}_{t}
are contained in a compact subset of the deformation space of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
\hat{F}
.
Therefore
\hat{F}_{t}
accumulates to a bounded subset in the deformation space of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
\hat{F}
.

Moreover, if each peripheral loop has non-trivial holonomy at
t=\infty
, by
Theorem

5.6
, the convergence of the holonomy of
\hat{F}_{t}
implies the convergence in
(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-15.15976pt\hbox{${\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}$}\hskip-15.15976pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1065.37274pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16081pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-196.16142pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{44994723}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\
