451.29065pt})^{n}\times\mathcal{R}(F)
.
Therefore
\hat{F}_{t}
has a unique limit in
\mathcal{P}(\hat{F})
.
∎

Theorem
7.4
immediately implies

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a multiloop
M
.
Then, for every sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
, up to a subsequence,
X_{t_{i}}
converges to a nodal Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
and
q_{t_{i}}
converges to a regular quadratic differential on
X_{\infty}
.

8.
Degeneration by neck-pinching

In this section, we summarize our main theorems on asymptotic behavior under neck-pinching.

Let
C_{t}=(f_{t},\rho_{t}),\penalty\ t\geq 0
be a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures which diverges to
\infty
in the deformation space, such that its holonomy
[\rho_{t}]\eqqcolon\eta_{t}
converges in the character variety
\textstyle\chi
.
By Proposition
3.2
, we can assume that the holonomy
\rho_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
also converges in the representation variety.
Let
X_{t}
be the complex structure of
C_{t}
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
Then
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is either
I
or a parabolic element.
Moreover
\rho_{t}(m)\neq I
for large enough
t>0
.

Recall that
\phi\colon\tilde{S}\to S
is the universal covering map.
Let
N_{m}
be a regular neighborhood of
m
in
S
.
Regard the loop
m
also as a fixed element of
\pi_{1}(S)
representing
m
, and let
\tilde{N}_{m}
be the component of
\phi^{-1}(N_{m})
preserved by
m\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

Theorem 8.2

(Convergence of developing maps)
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
.
Then, exactly one of the following two holds.

(1)

•

\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic;

•

the cusp neighborhoods of
C_{\infty}
are horodisk quotients;

•

f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets;

•

there is a multiloop
M
on
S
consisting of finitely many parallel copies of
m
, such that

f_{\infty}
is a local homeomorphism on
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(M)
and it takes each component of
\phi^{-1}(M)
to its corresponding parabolic fixed point.

(2)

\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
, and, for every sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
, up to a subsequence, there is a path of markings
S\to C_{t}
such that, as
i\to\infty
,

•

C_{t_{i}}|S\setminus N_{m}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a surface with punctures homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
;

•

the axis
a_{i}
of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
converges to a point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
or a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
;

•

the restriction of
f_{t_{i}}
to
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N_{m})
converges to a continuous map, and each boundary component of
\tilde{N}_{m}
maps to an ideal point of
\lim_{i\to\infty}a_{i}
.

For each
t\geq 0
,
let
(\tau_{t},L_{t})\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
be the Thurston parameterization of
C_{t}
, and let
\beta_{t}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the
\rho_{t}
-equivariant pleated surface.
In fact,
\beta_{t}
converges a continuous map to
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
:

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
on
S
.
Let
N_{m}
be a regular neighborhood of
m
on
S
.
Then, by taking an appropriate path of markings
\iota_{t}\colon S\to\tau_{t}
, exactly one of the following two holds:

(1)

\rho_{\infty}(m)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is parabolic, and
\beta_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\beta_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets as
t\to\infty
, such that
\beta^{-1}_{\infty}(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})
is a
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant multicurve which is
\pi_{1}(S)
-equivariantly homotopic to the multicurve
\phi^{-1}(m)
.

(2)

\rho_{\infty}(m)=I\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, and for every diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, up to a subsequence,
\beta_{t_{i}}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\beta_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
as
i\to\infty
and the axis
a_{i}
of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
converges to a point
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
or a geodesic of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that

•

if
\lim_{i\to\infty}a_{i}
is a point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, then
\beta_{\infty}^{-1}(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})=\phi^{-1}(m)
, and

•

if
\lim_{i\to\infty}a_{i}
is a geodesic
a_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, then
\beta_{\infty}
takes each component of
\phi^{-1}(N_{m})
to its corresponding limit geodesic
a_{\infty}
and each component of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N_{m})
to either a pleated surface in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
or a single point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

In order to prove Theorem
8.1
, Theorem
8.2
and Theorem
8.3
,
we carefully observe the behavior of
C_{t}
, fixing the isometry type of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
In particular, for Theorem
8.1
, we will show that, supposing, to the contrary, that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is hyperbolic (§
11
) or elliptic (§
12
), then
\rho_{t}
cannot converge.
The convergence when
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
is given in §
13
and the convergence when
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic is given in §
10
.

9.
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on punctured surfaces with elementary holonomy

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
F
be a closed surface with finitely many punctures, such that the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
Let
C=(f,\rho)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
such that

•

\rho
is an elementary representation, and

•

for each puncture of
C
, its peripheral holonomy is non-hyperbolic (so that its developing image is a single point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
).

Let
\Lambda
be the subset in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
of cardinality
0,1,
or
2
which
\operatorname{Im}\rho
preserves as a set.
Then, there is at least one puncture of
C
which maps to a point in the complement
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\Lambda\eqqcolon\Omega
by
f
.

Proof.

The discrete subset
f^{-1}(\Lambda)
in
\tilde{F}
descends a finite subset
D
on
F
.

We can assume that
\Lambda
is a non-empty set, since
if
\Lambda
is the empty set, then the assertion is obvious.
First, suppose that the cardinality of
\Lambda
is two, then
\Omega
admits a complete Euclidean metric invariant under
\operatorname{Im}\rho
.
Then, if all cups of
F
map to
\Lambda
,
F\setminus D
admits a complete Euclidean metric, which is a contradiction against the Euler characteristic of
F
.

Next, suppose that the cardinality of
\Lambda
is one.
Suppose, to the contrary, that all cups of
C
map to the point
\Lambda
.
Then
C\setminus D
has a complex affine structure.

We claim that
C\setminus D
is
complete
, i.e. the developing map of
C\setminus D
is a diffeomorphism onto
\mathbb{C}
, when we normalize
\operatorname{dev}C
so that
\{\infty\}
corresponds to the punctures.
Suppose, to the contrary, that
C\setminus D
is incomplete.
As the cardinality of
\Lambda
is not two,
\operatorname{Im}\rho
does not preserve an incomplete point of
C\setminus D
in
\mathbb{C}
.
Thus
C
admits Thurston’s parametrization
(\tau,L)
where
\tau
is a finite area hyperbolic structure on
F
and
L
is a measured lamination on
\tau
(Theorem
[
KP94b
, Theorem 11.6]
, cf
[
Bab17
, Theorem 3.1]
).
Since
F
is incomplete and the cardinality of
\Lambda
is not two, there is a maximal ball
B
of
\operatorname{dev}F
such that its ideal point set contains two distinct points in
\mathbb{C}
.
Then the holonomy of
F
must contain a hyperbolic element in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
whose fixed points are in
\mathbb{C}
, whose endpoints are close to those two points in
\mathbb{C}
.
This leads to a contradiction to all cups mapping to the same point.
Therefore, the
C\setminus D
is complete.

Thus, the holonomy of
F
consists of parabolic elements fixing
\infty
.
Then the Euler characteristic of
F\setminus D
is zero, since
F\setminus D
admits Euclidean structure.
Therefore
F
has a positive Euler characteristic, which is a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
F
be a closed surface with two punctures
p
and
q
such that the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
Suppose that
C=(f,\rho)
is a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
such that

•

the holonomy of
C
is elementary, and the stabilizer of
\operatorname{Im}\rho
(in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
) is non-discrete, and

•

the degrees of
f
around the two punctures are the same.

Then, no cusp of
F
maps to the subset
\Lambda
defined in
Lemma

9.1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.1
, we can assume that
p
does not develop to
\Lambda
.
As the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative, we let
C\cong(\tau,L)
be the Thurston parameters of
C
; then by the assumption of the holonomy,
p
and
q
correspond to cusps of
\tau
.
Then, as the degrees at
p
and
q
agree, the total weights of leaves of
L
around the punctures are the same.

Suppose, to the contrary, that a puncture
q
develops to a point of
\Lambda
.
Then
f
takes all lifts of
q
to the same point
r
of
\Lambda
:
Otherwise, as
\Lambda
has cardinality two,
\operatorname{Im}\rho
contains hyperbolic elements, and it also contains an elliptic element exchanging the points of
\Lambda
; then the stabilizer of
\operatorname{Im}\rho
must be discrete against the hypothesis.

Let
\ell
be a leaf of
L
initiating from
q
.
Then its lift
\tilde{\ell}
to the universal cover of
\tau
maps, by the bending map
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
, to a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
initiating from
q
.
As all lifts of
p
map to
r
, the other endpoint of
\beta(\tilde{\ell})
is the image of a lift of
q
.
Therefore all leaves of
L
initiating from
q
must end at
p
.
For every complementary region
R
of
\tau\setminus L
, letting
\tilde{R}
be the universal cover of
R
(in
\tilde{\tau}=\mathbb{H}^{2}
), at most, one ideal point of
\tilde{R}
maps to
q
by the pleated surface.

Moreover, every leaf of
L
initiating from
p
must end at
q
, since the total weights of
L
around
p
and
q
agree.
Let
L_{p,q}
be the sublamination of
L
consisting of the isolated leaves of
L
connecting
p
and
q
.
This implies that each component
\sigma
of
\tau\setminus L_{p,q}
has a negative Euler characteristic.
Since no leaves of
L\setminus L_{p,q}
has an endpoint on the boundary of
\tau\setminus L_{p,q}
,
the restriction of
\rho
to
\pi_{1}(\sigma)
is non-elementary, which is a contradiction.
∎

10.
Parabolic limit

In this section, we assume that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic, and analyze the limit of
C_{t}
as
t\to\infty
in terms of its bending map and developing map.
First, by Theorem
7.4
, for each component
F
of
S\setminus m
, by taking an appropriate base point
b_{t}
in the thick part of
C_{t}
homotopic to
F
,
(C_{t},b_{t})
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
F_{\infty}
on a compact surface with one or two punctures, such that
F_{\infty}
is homeomorphic to
F
.
Let
C_{\infty}
be the disjoint union of all such geometric limits
F_{\infty}
over all thick parts.
Then
C_{\infty}
is a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a closed surface with two cusps homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
.
Note that
C_{\infty}
is not connected if and only if
m
is separating.
Then the limit holonomy has the following algebraic property.

Lemma 10.1
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
Then, for each component
F
of
S\setminus m
,
\rho_{\infty}(F)
is non-elementary.

Proof.

Since
S
is a closed oriented surface of genus at least two, each component of
S\setminus m
is also of hyperbolic type.
Thus let
(\sigma,\nu)
be the Thurston parameterization of
F_{\infty}
, where
\sigma
is a complete closed hyperbolic with one or two cusps homeomorphic to
F
and
\nu
is a measured lamination on
\sigma
.
Clearly, the cusps of
F_{\infty}
correspond to the cusps of
\sigma
.
Then there is a bi-infinite simple geodesic
\ell
properly embedded in
\sigma
such that
\ell
is a leaf of
\nu
or disjoint from
\nu
(note that each endpoint of
\ell
is at a cusp of
\sigma
).

Let
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the bending map given by
(\sigma,\nu)
, such that
\beta
is equivariant via
\rho_{\infty}|\pi_{1}(F)
.
Let
\tilde{\ell}
be a lift of
\ell
to the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
of
\sigma
.
Then the endpoints of
\tilde{\ell}
are parabolic fixed points in the ideal boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\pi_{1}(F)
be the peripheral elements fixing the endpoints.
As
\ell
does not cross
\nu
, its image
\beta(\tilde{\ell})
is a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Moreover, as
\beta
is
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant,
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma_{1})
and
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma_{2})
are parabolic elements fixing the different endpoints of
\beta(\tilde{\ell})
.
Therefore
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma_{1})
and
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma_{2})
are non-commuting parabolic elements in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, and they generate a non-elementary subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
∎

Proposition

5.2
implies that the developing map extends to cups with parabolic holonomy.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
C=(f,\rho)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a closed surface with finitely many punctures, denoted by
F
, such that the holonomy around each puncture is parabolic.
Then the developing map
f\colon\tilde{F}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
extends continuously to the lift of cups so that they map to their corresponding parabolic fixed points.

Proof.

Set
C\cong(\tau,L)
in Thurston’s parameters, where
\tau
is a hyperbolic surface homeomorphic to
F
and
L
is a measured lamination on
\tau
.
For each cusp
c
of
C
,
by Proposition

5.2
, as the holonomy
\rho
around
c
is parabolic element in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
,
c
corresponds to a cusp of
\tau
and the total weight of leaves of
L
ending at the cusp is either
0
or a positive multiple of
2\pi
.
Let
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the bending map, and let
\tilde{L}
be the
\pi_{1}(F)
-invariant measured lamination on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
by pulling back
L
by the universal covering map
\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\tau
.
Let
r
be a geodesic ray in the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
ending at a parabolic fixed point
p
of a peripheral element of
\pi_{1}(S)
.
Then
r
eventually does not cross the
\tilde{L}
.
Thus the curve
\beta(r)
is eventually a geodesic ray in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
ending at
p
.
By the correspondence between the developing map and the pleated surface, the assertion follows.
∎

Recall that
\phi\colon\tilde{S}\to S
denotes the universal covering map.
Then the above lemmas imply a good convergence of the developing map of
C_{t}
away from
m
.

Theorem 10.3
.

Suppose
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
Then there is a regular neighborhood
N
of
m
such that

f_{t}|\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets, such that the developing image of each boundary component of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)
maps to its corresponding parabolic fixed point.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.4
(
2
),
the restriction
C_{t}
to
S\setminus N
converges to
C_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
by taking an appropriate isotopy of
S
uniformly.
Since
\rho_{\infty}(F)
is non-elementary (
Lemma

10.1
), the restriction of
f_{t}
to
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)
converges to the developing map of
C_{\infty}
uniformly on compact subsets.
By
Proposition

10.2
, each boundary component
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)
converges to its corresponding parabolic fixed point uniformly on compact subsets.
∎

In the rest of this section, we show the convergence of the developing map of
C_{t}
on the entire surface.
First we analyze the holonomy of
C_{t}
along
m
.

Proposition 10.4
.

For sufficiently large
t>0
,

\rho_{t}(m)
is not the identity element of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover, if the cusp neighborhoods of
C_{\infty}
are horodisk quotients.
Then, for sufficiently large
t>0
,

\rho_{t}(m)
is hyperbolic.

Proof.

Set
C_{t}\cong(\tau_{t},L_{t})\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
in Thurston’s parameters for
t>0
.
Similarly set
C_{\infty}\cong(\tau_{\infty},L_{\infty})
, where
\tau_{\infty}
is a complete hyperbolic structure on
F\setminus m
with finite volume, and
L_{\infty}
is a measured geodesic lamination on
\tau_{\infty}
.

Let
m_{t}
denote the geodesic representative of
m
on
\tau_{t}
.
Then, the length of
m_{t}
on
\tau_{t}
converges to
0
as
t\to\infty
since
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.

Suppose, to the contrary, that there is a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
such that
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is
not
hyperbolic.
Then a leaf
\ell_{i}
of
L_{t_{i}}
intersects the geodesic loop
m_{t_{i}}
for each
i=1,2\dots
.
Pick a point
p_{i}
on
m_{t_{i}}\cap L_{t_{i}}
.
Pick a lift
\tilde{m}_{t}
of
m_{t}
to the universal cover
\tilde{\tau}_{i}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}
which is preserved by an element
\gamma_{m}
in
\pi_{1}(S)
whose free homotopy class is
m
.
Then, for each
i
, let
p_{i,j}\penalty\ (j\in\mathbb{Z})
be the lifts of
p_{t_{i}}
on
\tilde{m}_{t_{i}}
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
indexed linearly, so that
p_{i,j}=\gamma_{m}^{j}\cdot p_{i,0}
.

For
t>0
,
let
\beta_{t}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the
\rho_{t}
-equivariant bending map induced by
(\tau_{t},L_{t})
.
Then, since
\{p_{i,j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orbit of the infinite cyclic group generated by
\gamma_{m}
, its image
\{\beta_{t_{i}}(p_{i,j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an orbit of the cyclic group generated by
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{m})\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Then, since
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is elliptic or parabolic (possibly the identity), by basic hyperbolic geometry, the points
\beta_{t_{i}}(p_{i,j})
is contained in a totally geodesic hyperbolic plane
H_{t_{i}}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
(In comparison, if
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is hyperbolic and its screw rotation angle is not a multiple of
\pi
, then most of its orbits do not lie in a totally geodesic plane.)

Note that
H_{t_{i}}
is uniquely determined by the choice of
p_{i}
and the lift
\tilde{m}_{i}
, unless
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is the identity.

If
\rho_{t_{i}}
is the identity element in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, then, letting
\tilde{\ell}_{i}
be the leaf of
\tilde{L}_{t_{i}}
intersecting
\tilde{m}_{i}
in
p_{i,j}
, let
H_{t_{i}}
be the hyperbolic plane orthogonal to the geodesic
\beta_{t_{i}}(\tilde{\ell}_{i})
in the point
\beta_{t_{i}}(p_{i,j})
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Clearly
H_{t_{i}}
is independent on the choice of
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is the identity.

The infimum of
\angle_{\tau_{t_{i}}}(m_{t_{i}},L_{t_{i}})\geq 0
over
i=1,2,\dots
is positive, since
\angle_{\tau_{i}}(m_{t_{i}},L_{t_{i}})
is close to zero, then
\rho_{t_{i}}
must be hyperbolic (
Theorem

2.1
).
Then, there is
\delta>0
, such that, if
i
is large enough, then, if a leaf
\ell
of
\tilde{L}_{t_{i}}
intersects
\tilde{m}_{t_{i}}
, then the angle between the geodesic
\beta_{t_{i}}(\ell)
and the hyperbolic plane
H_{t_{i}}
is at least
\delta
.
Indeed, otherwise,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\angle_{\tau_{i}}(m_{t_{i}},L_{t_{i}})=0
.

Recall that
\tau_{\infty}
is a complete hyperbolic surface of finite volume homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
, so that each boundary component of
S\setminus m
corresponds to a cusp of
\tau_{\infty}
.
Pick a loop
\alpha
on
S
such that

(1)

\alpha
essentially intersects
m
in a single point if
m
is non-separating, and in two points if
m
is separating,

(2)

each segment
\alpha\setminus m
descends to a geodesic
g
on
\tau_{\infty}
with endpoints at cusps, and
g
does not crossing
L_{\infty}
.

Below we show that the translation length of
\rho_{t_{i}}(\alpha)
diverges to
\infty
, which contradicts the convergence of
\rho_{t}
.
We assume that
m
is non-separating, and one can similarly prove the case when
m
is separating.

For each
i=1,2\dots
, let
\alpha_{i}
be the piecewise geodesic loop on
\tau_{t_{i}}
to homotopic to
\alpha
, such that

•

\alpha_{i}
is a union of two geodesic segments,

•

one geodesic segment
s_{i}
of
\alpha_{i}
has its interior contained in
\tau_{t_{i}}\setminus m_{t_{i}}
, and at each endpoint,
s_{i}
meets
m_{t_{i}}
orthogonally, and

•

the other geodesic segment
u_{i}
contained in
m_{t_{i}}
.

Since
\tau_{t_{i}}
is pinched along
m
as
i\to\infty
, the length of
s_{i}
goes to
\infty
.
Let
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
be a lift of
\alpha_{i}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
which is a simple piecewise geodesic, and it is a bilipschitz curve.

For each
i=1,2,\dots
, let
\tilde{u}_{i}
be a lift of
u_{i}
to a geodesic segment of
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
.
Then, let
\tilde{m}_{i}
be the lift of
m_{t_{i}}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
which contains
\tilde{u}_{i}
, and let
\gamma_{\tilde{u}_{i}}\in\pi_{1}(S)
be the element preserving
\tilde{m}_{t_{i}}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
if
i
is large, the
\beta_{t_{i}}(\tilde{u}_{i})
is contained in the
\epsilon
-neighborhood the
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{\tilde{u}_{i}})
-invariant hyperbolic plane
H_{\tilde{u}_{i}}
above, since
\operatorname{length}_{\tau_{t_{i}}}{m_{t_{i}}}
goes to
0
.

Let
\tilde{s}_{i}
be a lift of
s_{i}
to a segment of
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
.
Then, the length of
\tilde{s}_{i}
goes to
\infty
as
i\to\infty
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, by (
2
), the transversal measure of
s_{i}
by
L_{t_{i}}
in the
\epsilon
-thick part of
\tau_{t_{i}}
limits to
0
as
i\to\infty
.
In addition, there is
r>0
, such that, the intersection angle of
L_{t_{i}}
and
s_{i}
in the
r
-thin part of
\tau_{\tau_{t_{i}}}
goes to zero as
i\to\infty
.
Therefore, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then the restriction of
\beta_{t_{i}}
to
\tilde{s}_{i}
is a
(1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
-bilipschitz embedding.
Let
g_{i}
be the bi-infinite geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
passing through the endpoints of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\tilde{s}_{i})
.

Let
u_{i,1},u_{i,2}
be the lifts of
u_{i}
to the geodesic segments of
\tilde{\alpha}_{t_{i}}
which are adjacent to
\tilde{s}_{i}
.
Then let
H_{i,1}
and
H_{i,2}
be the hyperbolic planes corresponding to
u_{i,1}
and
u_{i,2}
, respectively.
Then,
g_{i}
transversally intersects
H_{i,1}
and
H_{i,2}
at angle at least
\delta/2
.
Moreover, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is large enough, then those intersection points are
\epsilon
-close to the endpoints of
\beta_{t_{i}}(\tilde{s}_{i})
.
Therefore, the distance between the hyperbolic planes
H_{i,1}
and
H_{i,2}
goes to
\infty
as
i\to\infty
(
Figure

10
).
Therefore the translation length of
\rho_{t}(\alpha)
goes to
\infty
as desired.
This contradicts the hypothesis.
Therefore
\rho_{t}(m)
must be parabolic for sufficiently large
t>0
.

\end{overpic}

Figure 10.
The quasi-geodesic
\beta_{t_{i}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{i})
preserved by the hyperbolic element
\rho_{t_{i}}(\alpha)
.

∎

Let
\phi\colon\tilde{S}\to S
be the universal covering map.
Let
\kappa_{t}\colon C_{t}\to\tau_{t}
denote the collapsing map of
C_{t}
, and
\tilde{\kappa}_{t}\colon\tilde{C}_{t}\to\mathbb{H}^{2}
denote its lift from the collapsing of the universal cover (§
2.2.2
).
We next show the convergence of the bending map.

Theorem 10.5
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
Then, up to an isotopy of
S
in
t
,

\beta_{t}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\alpha\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
such that

•

\alpha^{-1}(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1})
is a
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant multicurve on
\tilde{S}
isotopic to
\phi^{-1}(m)
though
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant multicurves, and

•

for each component
P
of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(m)
, the restriction
\beta_{t}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t}|P
converges to the pleated surface for the component of
C_{\infty}
corresponding to
P
.

Proof.

The second assertion holds immediately from
Theorem
10.3
.

The axis
a_{t}
of
\rho_{t}(m)
converges to the parabolic fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
By
Proposition

10.4
,
\rho_{t}(m)
is a hyperbolic element for sufficiently large
t>0
.
Let
D\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a horoball centered at the parabolic fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
Then we pick a continuous path of
\rho_{t}(m)
-invariant subsets
D_{t}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
bounded by the surface equidistant from the axis of
\rho_{t}(m)
so that

D_{t}
converges to
D
as
t\to\infty
.

Pick a sufficiently small
\delta>0
.
For sufficiently large
t>0
,
let
N_{t}^{\delta}
be the component of the
\delta
-thin part of
\tau_{t}
homotopic to
m
.
Let
\tilde{N}_{t}^{\delta}
be the lift of
N_{t}^{\delta}
to the universal cover
\tilde{\tau}\cong\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
If
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, by the convergence of
\rho_{t}
, the
\beta_{t}
-image of
\tilde{N}_{t}^{\delta}
is eventually contained in
D_{t}
.
This implies the first assertion.
∎

Next, we prove that cusp neighborhoods of the limit surface are isomorphic to cusp neighborhoods of a hyperbolic surface.

Proposition 10.6
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
The cusps of
C_{\infty}
must be horodisk quotients.

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that the cusp neighborhoods of
C_{\infty}
are
not
horodisk quotients.

Let
C_{t}\cong(\tau_{t},L_{t})
denote the Thurston’s parameters of
C_{t}
.
Then, as
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic,
L_{t}(m)
converges to a non-negative integral multiple
2\pi n
of
2\pi
.
As the limit cusp neighborhoods are assumed to be
not
horodisk quotients,
n
is a positive integer.
Similarly, let
C_{\infty}\cong(\tau_{\infty},L_{\infty})
denote Thurston parameters of
C_{\infty}
.
Thus the
L_{\infty}
-transversal measure of each peripheral loop of
C_{\infty}
is
2\pi n
.

For sufficiently large
t>0
,
\rho_{t}(m)
is not the identity; let
a_{t}
be its axis (
Definition

3.6
).
Pick
\delta>0
less than the two-dimensional Margulis constant.
Let
N_{t}
be the
\delta
-thin part of
\tau_{t}
homotopic to
m
.
Let
\tilde{N}_{t}
be the lift of
N_{t}
to the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
If
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, for all
t
large enough, each component of
N_{t}\cap L_{t}
is a geodesic segment connecting one boundary component of
N_{t}
to the other.
Since the transversal measure of each peripheral loop of
L_{t}
is close to
2\pi n>0

Thus, for
t\gg 0
, pick a fundamental domain
F_{t}
in
\tilde{N}_{t}
bounded by two leaves of
\tilde{L}_{t}
such that a component
F_{t,1}
of
F_{t}\setminus\tilde{m}_{t}
converges to a fundamental domain of the bending map
\beta_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
(
Theorem

10.5
) near a cusp of
\tau_{\infty}
.

Let
\ell_{t}
be a leaf of
\tilde{L}_{\infty}
bounding
F_{t}
, so that, for each component
r_{t}
of
\ell_{t}\setminus\tilde{m}_{t}
, the restriction of
\beta_{t}
converges to a bi-infinite geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as
i\to\infty
.
Clearly the length of
\ell_{t}\cap\tilde{N}_{t}
goes to
\infty
, and the length of each segment of
\ell_{t}\cap\tilde{N}_{t}\setminus\tilde{m}_{t}
goes to
\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Let
F_{t,2}
be the other component of
F_{t}\setminus\tilde{m}_{t}
.
Then there is an element
\gamma_{t}
of
G_{t}
such that the restriction of
\beta_{t}
to
\gamma_{t}F_{t,2}
converges to the fundamental domain of the other cusp of
C_{\infty}
.

We first show that if
\rho_{t}(m)
is hyperbolic, it must be “almost elliptic” for sufficiently large
t>0
.

Claim 10.7
.

Suppose that there is a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
diverging to
\infty
, such that
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is hyperbolic for each
i=1,2,\dots
.
Then, the complex translation of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
goes to zero from the imaginary direction as
i\to\infty
.
In other words, the sequence
\operatorname{tr}^{2}\rho_{t_{i}}(m)\in\mathbb{C}
converges to
4
tangentially to the real ray
\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid x\leq 4\}
.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that there is a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is hyperbolic and the complex translation length converges to
0
from the non-imaginary direction.
As
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is hyperbolic, the axis is a geodesic and it converges to the parabolic fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
Pick a point
p_{i}
on
\beta_{t_{i}}(F_{t_{i}})
closest to
a_{t_{i}}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Let
R_{i}
be the set of points in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
whose distance from
a_{t_{i}}
is at most the distance from
p_{i}
to the axis
a_{t_{i}}
.

For each
i
, let
G_{i}
be a one-dimensional Lie subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
such that the infinite cyclic group
\langle\rho_{t_{i}}(m)\rangle
is asymptotically dense in
G_{i}
as
i\to\infty
w.r.t. the path metric on
G_{i}
induced by the invariant metric on
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Since the complex translation length of
\rho_{t_{i}}
converges to
0
from a non-imaginary direction,
G_{t}
converges to a one-dimensional subgroup in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
consisting of only hyperbolic elements except the identity.
For every
i
, let
c_{i}
be the
G_{i}
-invariant smooth curve in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
passing
p_{i}
.
Then
c_{i}
spirals on the boundary of
R_{i}
limiting to the endpoints of
a_{i}
. (See
Figure

11
.)

The
\beta_{t_{i}}
-image of the leaf
\ell_{i}
is a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
tangent to
R_{i}
passing
p_{i}
.
Then, moreover, the geodesic
\beta_{t_{i}}(\ell_{i})
and the curve
c_{i}
are asymptotically tangent to each other at
p_{i}
as
i\to\infty
, because of the convergence of the bending map
\beta_{t_{i}}
and the holonomy
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
as
i\to\infty
.

Let
s_{i,1}
be the geodesic segment
\ell_{i}\cap F_{i,1}
, so that
\beta_{t_{i}}(s_{i,1})
converges to a geodesic ray limiting to the fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
Let
q_{1,i}
be the endpoint of
s_{i,1}
that is on the boundary of
\tilde{N}_{i}
, and let
q_{2,i}
be the other endpoint of
\ell_{i}\cap\tilde{N}_{i}
.
Then
\beta_{t_{i}}(q_{i,1})
converges to a point in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then
\beta_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}q_{i,2})
also converges to a point on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Since the length of each segment of
\ell_{i}\cap\tilde{N}_{i}\setminus\tilde{m}_{i}
goes to infinity, and
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i})
is asymptotically tangent to the curve
c_{i}
, therefore the distance between
\beta_{t_{i}}(q_{i,1})
and
\beta_{t_{i}}(q_{i,2})
diverges to
\infty
as
i\to\infty
.
This is a contradiction against the convergence of the bending map
\beta_{t_{i}}
as
i\to\infty
.

\end{overpic}

Figure 11.
The left figure is the normalization of the right figure so that
p_{i}
is at the center

∎

Next we show the convergence of
\rho_{t}
forces the convergence of twisting parameter along
m
.

Claim 10.8
.

The Fenchel-Nielsen twisting parameter of
\tau_{t}
along
m
must converge (in
\mathbb{R}
) as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

First, for each non-identity element of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, we describe an associated foliation.
For a hyperbolic isometry or an elliptic isometry of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, the hyperbolic planes containing its axis give a foliation on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
minus the axis.
For a parabolic isometry
\alpha\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, pick a hyperbolic plane
H
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
invariant under
\alpha
, which contains the parabolic fixed point.
Then there is a foliation of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
by hyperbolic planes orthogonal to
H
and containing the parabolic fixed point; this foliation is independent of the choice of
H
.
For sufficiently large
t>0
, as
\rho_{t}(m)
is not the identity (
Proposition

10.4
), let
\mathcal{F}_{t}
denote such a foliation for
\rho_{t}(m)
.

Let
\tilde{m}
be a lift of
m
to the universal cover
\tilde{S}
.
Let
P_{1},P_{2}
be the connected components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(m)
adjacent along
\tilde{m}
.
For each
i=1,2
, given a point
x_{i}
in
P_{i}
near
\tilde{m}
, let
v_{\infty,i}
be the tangent vector at the point
\beta_{\infty}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{\infty}(x_{i})
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
orthogonal its support hyperbolic plane of
x_{i}
in the normal direction (§
2.2.2
).
Since the
L_{t}
-transversal measure along
m_{t}
converges to
2\pi n>0
, we can pick
x_{i}
so that

v_{\infty,i}
is tangent to the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
.
Similarly, for each
t\gg 0
, pick a point
x_{t,i}
in
P_{i}
such that, letting
v_{t,i}
be the tangent vector of
\beta_{t}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t}
at
x_{t,i}
orthogonal to its support plane,
v_{t,i}
is tangent to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and
v_{t,i}
converges to
v_{\infty,i}
as
t\to\infty
. (See
Figure

12
.)

Let
\mathcal{L}_{t}
be the circular measured lamination on
C_{t}
which descends to the measured lamination of Thurston’s parametrization by the collapsing map.
Let
e_{t}
be the minimal transversal measure, given by
\mathcal{L}_{t}
, of arcs connecting
x_{1}
to
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})x_{t,2}
.
Note that, since the isometry
\rho_{t}(m)
preserves the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, the tangent vector
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})v_{t,2}
at
\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})x_{t,2}
is also tangent to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
.
By
10.7
,
\rho_{t}(m)
is either parabolic, elliptic, or “almost elliptic” for
t\gg 0
.
Therefore, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, then, for
t\gg 0
, the transversal measure
e_{t}
is
\epsilon
-close to a multiple of
2\pi
.
Thus the twisting parameter along
m
converges modulo
2\pi
.
By continuity, the twisting parameter of
\tau_{t}
along
m
must converge as
t\to\infty
.

Figure 12.
The right figure is a normalization of the left figure so that the axis
a_{t}
passes the center.

∎

By

10.8
, the Fenchel-Nielsen twisting parameter of
\tau_{t}
along
m
converges.
For all
t>0
, let
Q_{t,1}
and
Q_{t,2}
be the adjacent components of
\mathbb{H}^{2}\setminus\psi^{-1}(m_{t})
corresponding to
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively, so that
Q_{t,1}
and
Q_{t,2}
are separated by the geodesic
\tilde{m}_{t}
.
Then, as the restriction of
\beta_{t}
of the component
Q_{t,1}
converges, uniformly on compact subsets, to the bending map of the corresponding cusp neighborhood of
C_{\infty}
by
Theorem

10.5
.
Then, since the length of the geodesic loop
m_{t}
goes to
0
as
t\to\infty
, the convergence of the twisting parameter implies that the restriction of
\beta_{t}
to
Q_{t,2}
converges to the parabolic fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
uniformly on compact subsets.
This is a contradiction against the convergence of the bending map
\beta_{t}
of
Q_{t,2}
uniformly on compact subsets guaranteed by
Theorem

10.5
.

10.6

Theorem 10.9
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
Then, by an appropriate isotopy of
S
in
t
,
f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
such that, for some multiloop
M
on
S
consisting of finitely many parallel copies of
m
,

•

f_{\infty}
is a local homeomorphism on
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(M)
, and

•

f_{\infty}
takes each component of
\phi^{-1}(M)
to its corresponding parabolic fixed point.

Under the assumption of
Theorem

10.9
, each cusp of
C_{\infty}
is a horodisk quotient by
Proposition

10.6
.
Thus, by
Proposition

10.4
,
\rho_{t}(m)
is hyperbolic for all sufficiently large
t>0
, and it converges to the parabolic element
\rho_{\infty}(m)
as
t\to\infty
.

More generally, let
\gamma_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C},\penalty\ t\geq 0
be a path of hyperbolic elements such that
\gamma_{t}
converges to a parabolic element
\gamma_{\infty}
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
as
t\to\infty
.
Let
G_{t}
be the one-parameter subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\gamma_{t}
such that the cyclic group generated by
\gamma_{t}
is asymptotically dense in
G_{t}
with respect to the path metric on
G_{t}
induced by the (left) invariant metric on
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

Continuously conjugate
\gamma_{t}
by elements
\omega_{t}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that the axis of
\omega_{t}\cdot\gamma_{t}\coloneqq r_{t}w_{t}r_{t}^{-1}
remains, for all
t
, to be the geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
which connects
0
to
\infty
in the ideal boundary
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Proposition 10.10
.

Let
A
be a cylinder and homeomorphically identify
A
with
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and let
\tilde{A}
be the universal cover of
A
.
Let
A_{t}\penalty\ (t>0)
be a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on a cylinder
A
, and let
f_{t}
be its developing map which changes continuously in
t
, such that

•

the holonomy of
A_{t}
is the limit holonomy isomorphism
\pi_{1}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}\to\langle\gamma_{t}\rangle
,

•

each boundary of
A_{t}
develops onto a
G_{t}
-invariant curve on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
for all
t>0
.

•

for each boundary circle
b
of
A
, the restriction of
f_{t}
to the lift
\tilde{b}
to
\tilde{A}
converges to a
G_{\infty}
-invariant simple curve on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
(which is a
G_{\infty}
-invariant round circle minus the parabolic fixed point).

Then, by an isotopy of
A
fixing the boundary,
\operatorname{dev}A_{t}\colon\tilde{A}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to an continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{A}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
such that

•

f_{\infty}
is equivariant via the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\to\langle\gamma_{\infty}\rangle
;

•

there is a multiloop
M
consisting of loops homotopy equivalent to
A
, such that
f_{\infty}
is a local homeomorphism on
\tilde{A}\setminus\tilde{M}
;

•

f_{\infty}
takes
\tilde{M}
to the parabolic fixed point of
\gamma_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We construct a path of fundamental membranes
Z_{t}
for the developing maps
f_{t}
which give the desired limit as
t\to\infty
.

The normalized developing map
\omega_{t}\circ f_{t}\colon\tilde{A}\to\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
is identified with the restriction of
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
to a bi-infinite strip
I_{t}
bounded by parallel lines in
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}
.
Let
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
denote the boundary components of
A
.
Regarding
b_{1},b_{2}
as simple closed curves, we can lift
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
to segments
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, respectively, of segments of the boundary components of
\tilde{A}
.
For each
t>0
and
i=1,2
, let
s_{i,t}
be the segment of the boundary line of
I_{t}
such that
\omega_{t}\circ f_{t}(s_{i})=\exp(s_{i,t})
.
Then
s_{1,t}
and
s_{2,t}
are parallel and have the same length.
Thus
s_{2,t}
is the Euclidean translation of
s_{1,t}
by unique
z_{t}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Claim 10.11
.

(1)

The length of
s_{i,t}
goes to zero as
t\to\infty
, and

(2)

z_{t}
converges to an integer multiple of
2\pi i
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

(
1
)
As
0
and
\infty
are the fixed points of
\omega_{t}\gamma_{t}\omega_{t}^{-1}
and
\gamma_{t}
converges to
\gamma_{\infty}
, both

\omega_{t}^{-1}(0)
and
\omega_{t}^{-1}(\infty)
converge to the parabolic fixed point of
\gamma_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
.
Since the development of
b_{i}
converges to a
G_{\infty}
-invariant curve on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, clearly the development of
s_{i,t}
converges to a simple arc contained in the
G_{\infty}
-invariant curve.
Therefore, since
f_{t}=w_{t}^{-1}\exp
on
\tilde{A}
, the norm of the derivative of
f_{t}
at each point on the segment
s_{i}
goes to infinity as
t\to\infty
.
Hence the Euclidean length of
s_{i,t}
must go to zero as
t\to\infty
.

(
2
)
Since the Euclidean length of
s_{1,t}
goes to zero on
I_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}
, translating
I_{t}
by a multiple of
2\pi i
, we may assume that
s_{1,t}
converges to a point
p
on
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
q\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
be the parabolic fixed point of
\gamma_{\infty}
.
Let
K
be a compact subset
K
in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{q\}
and
U_{p}
be a neighborhood of
p
in
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
U
denote the union of translates of
U_{p}
by the integer multiples of
2\pi i
.
Then, if
t
is sufficiently large, then
\omega_{t}^{-1}\exp(I_{t}\setminus U)
is contained in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus K
.
Therefore, as the developments of
s_{1,t}
and
s_{2,t}
converge to simple arcs in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{q\}
, their difference
z_{t}
must converge to a multiple of
2\pi i
.
∎

Let
n
be the integer such that
z_{t}
converges to
2\pi in
.
Pick a polygonal fundamental domain
Z_{t}
of
A_{t}
in
I_{t}
with following properties:

Z_{t}
is a union of
(n+1)
-rectangles
R_{t,1},R_{t,2},\dots,R_{t,n+1}
and
n
parallelograms
P_{t,1},\dots,P_{t,n}
as in the figure (
Figure

13
) so that

•

for each
i=1,\dots,n,n+1
, a pair of edges of
R_{t,i}
are parallel to the boundary of the Euclidean strip
I_{t}
, the boundary segment
s_{1,t}
is an edge of
R_{t,1}
, the boundary segment
s_{2,t}
is an edge of
R_{t,n+1}
, and, for each
i=2,\dots,n-2
, the Euclidean translation of
s_{t,i}
by
2\pi i
decomposes
R_{t,i+1}
into two isometric rectangles, and

•

for each
i=1,\dots,n
, the parallelogram
P_{t,i}
have edges parallel to the boundary of
I_{t}
which are an edge
R_{t,i}
and an edge
R_{t,i+1}
.

In addition, we take
R_{t,1},R_{t,2},\dots,R_{t,n+1}
and
n
parallelograms
P_{t,1},\dots,P_{t,n}
appropriately so that

•

the development of
P_{t,i}
by
f_{t}
converges to the parabolic fixed point of
\gamma_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
;

•

the
f_{t}
-images of
R_{t,1}
and
R_{t,n+1}
converge to horodisks bounded by the limit of
f_{t}(\tilde{b})
in the hypothesis, and the restriction of
f_{t}
to
R_{t,1}
and
R_{t,n+1}
converge to a developing map of horodisk quotients;

•

for
i=2,\dots,n
, the restriction of
f_{t}
to
R_{t,i}
converges to a developing map of the Euclidean cylinder
(\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p\})/\langle\gamma_{\infty}\rangle

(
Figure

14
).
Let
M
be a multiloop on
A
consisting of
n
boundary parallel loops.
Pick a path of regular neighborhood
N_{t}
of
M
so that
N_{t}
converges to
M
as
t\to\infty
.
Isotope
A
so that a fundamental domain
F
of
\tilde{A}
maps to
Z_{t}
and that
N_{t}
are identified with
P_{t,1},\dots,P_{t,n}
.
Then we a desired convergence.

∎

Proof of Theorem
10.9
.

We already know the convergence of the developing map in every thick part by Theorem
10.3
.
There are two cusps
c_{1},c_{1}
of
C_{\infty}
, which are horodisk quotients by
Proposition

10.6
.
For each cusp
c_{i}
of
C_{\infty}
, pick a simple closed curve
\ell_{i}
which develops to a
G_{\infty}
-invariant simple curve on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Then, for large
t>0
, pick a simple closed curve
\ell_{i,t}
on
C_{t}
such that
\ell_{i,t}
develops onto a
G_{t}
-invariant curve on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
and
\ell_{i,t}
converges to
\ell_{i}
as
t\to\infty
.

Let
A_{t}
be the cylinder in
C_{t}
bounded by
\ell_{1,t}
and
\ell_{2,t}
.
Then we can take such a path of cylinders
A_{t}
in
C_{t}
and a constant
\delta>0
such that
A_{t}
contains the
\delta
-thin part of
C_{t}
for all sufficiently large
t
.
Thus, by applying
Proposition

10.10
to
A_{t}
, we obtain a multiloop for the desired convergence property of
\operatorname{dev}C_{t}
.
∎

10.1.
Convergence in holomorphic quadratic differential in the case of parabolic cusps

Under the assumption that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic, we already have the limit
C_{\infty}
of
C_{t}
as
t\to\infty
where
C_{\infty}
is a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
with two cusps homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
.
Moreover, each cusp of
C_{\infty}
has a neighborhood which is a horodisk quotient (i.e. isomorphic, as a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure, to a cusp neighborhood of a hyperbolic surface) by
Proposition

10.6
.
Then the holomorphic quadratic differential
\phi_{\infty}
on
X_{\infty}
representing
C_{\infty}
has, at worst, a first order pole at each cusp.
Therefore we have the following convergence of the differential.

Theorem 10.12
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is parabolic.
Then
X_{t}
converges to a nodal Riemann surface
X_{\infty}
such that
X_{\infty}
minus the node is homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
and
q_{t}
converges to a quadratic differential
q_{\infty}
on
X_{\infty}
such that the node is at worst first order pole.

11.
\rho_{\infty}(m)
cannot be hyperbolic

In this section, we show that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
cannot be a hyperbolic element.

Lemma 11.1
.

Suppose that
X_{t}
is pinched along a loop
m
and
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is hyperbolic.
Then

(1)

C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C_{\infty}
on a compact surface with two punctures, which is homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
, in the sense that, for every
\epsilon>0
, the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{t}
converges to the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{\infty}
uniformly, and

(2)

\rho_{\infty}(F)
is non-elementary for each component
F
of
S\setminus m
.

Proof.

(
1
) is an immediate corollary of
Theorem

7.4
.

(
2
)
Let
F_{\infty}
be the component of
C_{\infty}
corresponding to
F
.
Let
(\sigma,\nu)
denote the Thurston parametrization of
F_{\infty}
.
Then
\sigma
is a hyperbolic surface with geodesic boundary, such that the lengths of the boundary components are the translation length of
\rho_{\infty}(m)
(see the proof of Lemma
5.4
).
Let
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\nu})
be the universal cover of
(\sigma,\nu)
so that
\tilde{\sigma}
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
bounded by geodesics and that
\tilde{\nu}
is a
\pi_{1}(\sigma)
-invariant lamination on
\tilde{\sigma}
.

Let
\alpha\colon\tilde{\sigma}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be its pleated surface equivariant by the holonomy of
F_{\infty}
.
Let
\ell
be a boundary geodesic of
\tilde{\sigma}
.
Then the endpoints of
\alpha(\ell)
are in the limit set
\Lambda
of
\operatorname{Hol}F_{\infty}
, as
\alpha(\ell)
is the axis of the hyperbolic
\rho_{\infty}(m)
.
Every component
R
of
\tilde{\sigma}\setminus\tilde{\nu}
has at least three ideal points.
Then the ideal points of
\alpha(R)
are in
\Lambda
(see
[
Bab20
, Lemma 5.1]
).
Thus
\rho_{\infty}|F
is non-elementary.
∎

Lemma 11.2
.

For each cusp
p
of
C_{\infty}
, there is a neighborhood of
p
foliated by isomorphic admissible loops which develop to simple curves on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
invariant under a one-parameter subgroup in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{t}(m)
.

Proof.

The developing map near a cusp neighborhood is the restriction of the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
; moreover, by taking an appropriate neighborhood, one can assume that the restriction is to a half-plane bounded by a straight line in
\mathbb{C}
invariant under the deck transformation corresponding to the hyperbolic element
\rho_{t}(m)
.

The half-plane is foliated by straight lines parallel to the boundary, and this foliation descends to a desired foliation of the cusp neighborhoods by admissible loops.
∎

Proposition 11.3
.

If
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, then, for every sufficiently large
t>0
, there is a cylinder
A_{t}
in
C_{t}
homotopy equivalent to
m
such that

•

A_{t}
changes continuously in
t\gg 0
;

•

A_{t}
is foliated by admissible loops whose developments are invariant under a one-parameter subgroup
G_{t}
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{t}(m)
;

•

A_{t}
contains the conformally
\epsilon
-thin part of
C_{t}
;

•

C_{t}\setminus A_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S\setminus m
whose boundary components are admissible loops.

Proof.

Consider the cusp neighborhoods of
C_{\infty}
foliated by admissible loops by Lemma
11.2
.
By the convergence of Lemma
11.1
and the stability of the admissible loops, for
t\gg 0
, there is a cylinder
A_{t}
foliated by admissible loops whose developments are invariant under
G_{t}
.
Then it is easy to realize other desired properties.
∎

By Claim
11.1
(
2
), the developing map of
C_{t}\setminus A_{t}
converges uniformly on compact subsets.
By normalizing
\rho_{t}
by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
continuously, so that, for sufficiently large
t>0
, we can, in addition, assume that the axis of the hyperbolic element
\rho_{t}(m)
connects
0
and
\infty
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Then the developing map of the cylinder
A_{t}
is the restriction of the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
to the strip region
R_{t}
bounded by parallel lines, since the boundary components of
A_{t}
develop to
G_{t}
-invariant curves by
Proposition

11.3
.
Since the boundary components of
A_{t}
converge to peripheral loops of
C_{\infty}
, by the continuity of
\operatorname{dev}C_{t}
in
t
, the region
R_{t}
converges to a strip in
\mathbb{C}
with finite width.
Therefore
A_{t}
must converge as
t\to\infty
.
Thus
C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
— this contradicts the divergence of
C_{t}
in the deformation space.
Hence
\rho_{\infty}(m)
cannot be hyperbolic.

12.
\rho_{\infty}(m)
cannot be elliptic

In this section, similarly to the previous section (§
11
), we show that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
cannot be elliptic.
To show this, we assume, to the contrary, that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is elliptic and obtain a contradiction against the convergence of
\rho_{t}
as
t\to\infty
.
By
Theorem

7.4
, we have

Proposition 12.1
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is elliptic.
Then
C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C_{\infty}
on a compact surface minus two points homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
, in the sense that, for every
\epsilon>0
, the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{t}
converges to the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{\infty}
.

Lemma 12.2
.

For each component
F_{\infty}
of
C_{\infty}
, the stabilizer of
\rho_{\infty}(F_{\infty})
by conjugation is a discrete subgroup in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Let
F_{\infty}
be a component of
C_{\infty}
.
Then let
(\sigma,\nu)
be the Thurston parametrization of
F_{\infty}
, and let
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\nu})
be the universal cover of
(\sigma,\nu)
.
Then the rotation angle of the elliptic element
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is, modulo
2\pi
, equal to the total weight, given by
\nu
, of the leaves ending at a puncture (Proposition
5.2
).
Let
\beta_{\infty}\colon\tilde{\sigma}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the equivariant pleated surface.
Pick a leaf
\ell
of
\nu
whose endpoints are at cusps of
\nu
; then
\ell
is an isolated leaf.
Let
\tilde{\ell}
be a leaf of
\tilde{\nu}
which is a lift of
\ell
.
Then its image
\beta_{\infty}(\ell)
is a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Each endpoint of this geodesic is a fixed point of the parabolic element in the image
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}(F))
corresponding to its associated peripheral loop.

As the leaf
\ell
is isolated,

\ell
bounds a component
P
of
\tilde{\sigma}\setminus\tilde{\nu}
, and
P
has at least three ideal points.
Then, for each ideal point
p
of
P
, let
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(F_{\infty})
be such that
\gamma
fixes
p
.
Then
\beta_{\infty}(p)
is fixed by the elliptic element
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma)
.
Therefore, the stabilizer of
\rho_{\infty}(F_{\infty})
is a discrete subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
∎

Similarly to
Proposition

11.3
, the following follows from Lemma
12.1
and Lemma
12.2
:

Proposition 12.3
.

If
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, then for every sufficiently large
t>0
,
there is a cylinder
A_{t}
in
C_{t}
homotopy equivalent to
m
such that

•

A_{t}
changes continuously in
t\gg 0
;

•

A_{t}
is foliated by loops whose developments are invariant under the one-dimensional subgroup
G_{t}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{t}(m)
, and
G_{t}
converges to a one-dimensional subgroup
G_{\infty}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{\infty}(m)
;

•

A_{t}
contains the conformally
\epsilon
-thin part of
C_{t}
homotopic to
m
;

•

C_{t}\setminus A_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S\setminus m
such that the boundary components cover round circles on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Proposition 12.4
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)
is elliptic.
Then
C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
, which is a contradiction as desired.

Proof.

Fix sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, and let
A_{t}
be a cylinder given by Proposition
12.3
.
Let
\phi_{t}\colon\tilde{C}_{t}\to C_{t}
be the universal covering map.
Then the developing map of
\tilde{C_{t}}\setminus\phi_{t}^{-1}(A_{t})
converges uniformly on compact subsets.
Let
\tilde{A}_{t}
be the component of
\phi_{t}^{-1}(A_{t})
invariant under
m\in\pi_{1}(S)
, so that
A_{t}
changes continuously in
t
.
We can normalize
\operatorname{dev}C_{t}
by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
continuously in
t
, such that, for sufficiently large
t>0
, the geodesic axis of
\rho_{t}(m)
connects
0
and
\infty
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Then, the restriction of
\operatorname{dev}C_{t}=f_{t}
to
\tilde{A}_{t}
is the restriction of the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
to an infinite strip in
\mathbb{C}
.
Since
f_{t}
converges on the boundary components of
\tilde{A}_{t}
, thus the restriction of
f_{t}
to
\tilde{A}
converges as
t\to\infty
.
Hence
A_{t}
must converge as
t\to\infty
as a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a cylinder with boundary.
Therefore
C_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
, which is a contradiction.
∎

13.
Limit when
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I

Let
A
be a regular neighborhood of a loop
m
on
S
.
For
t\geq 0
, let
(\tau_{t},L_{t})
be Thurston parameters of
C_{t}
.
Let
\beta_{t}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be its
\rho_{t}
-equivariant pleated surface.
Let
\kappa_{t}\colon C\to\tau
be the collapsing map, and
\tilde{\kappa}_{t}\colon\tilde{C}\to\mathbb{H}^{2}
denote the lift of
\kappa
to the map between their universal covers.
Let
a_{t}
denote the axis of
\rho_{t}(m)\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
(
Definition

3.6
).

Note that a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
S
is defined up to an isotopy of the base surface
S
.
Thus the developing map
f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
of the path
C_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
can be modified by an isotopy
\psi_{t}\colon S\to S
in
t
without changing
C_{t}
.
Finally, recall that
\phi\colon\tilde{S}\to S
is the universal covering map.

Theorem 13.1
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
.
Then the following hold:

(1)

\rho_{t}(m)\neq I
for sufficiently large
t>0
.

(2)

The Fenchel-Nielsen twisting parameter (in
\mathbb{R}
) of
X_{t}
along
m
diverges to either
\infty
or to
-\infty
.

(3)

For every diverging sequence
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
,
there is a subsequence such that

(a)

the axis
a_{t_{i}}
converges to a point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
or a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, denoted by
a_{\infty}
;

(b)

there is a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure in
\mathcal{P}(S\setminus m)
such that, for every
\epsilon>0
, the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{t_{i}}
converges to the
\epsilon
-thick part of
C_{\infty}
uniformly;

(c)

up to an isotopy of
S
in
t
, the restriction of
f_{t_{i}}
to
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
as
t_{i}\to\infty
such that, for each component
\tilde{A}
of
\phi^{-1}(A)
,
its boundary components map onto the ideal points of
a_{\infty}
.

(4)

the pleated surface
\beta_{t_{i}}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t_{i}}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, up to an isotopy of
S
.

Notice that, by the surjectivity in (
3c
),
if
a_{\infty}
is a geodesic, then the different boundary components of
\tilde{A}
map to the different endpoints of
a_{\infty}
.

We will prove (
4
) in the next subsection (§
13.0.1
).
In this section, we will prove the other assertions:
(
1
) will be proved in Lemma

13.8
; (
2
) will be proved in Lemma
13.9
; (
3c
) will be proved in Proposition
13.12
.
The proof of
(
3b
) is similar to the proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

Let
C_{\infty}\cong(\sigma_{\infty},\nu_{\infty})
denote the Thurston parameterization, where
\sigma_{\infty}
be a hyperbolic structure in the Teichmüller space
\mathsf{T}(S\setminus m)
and
\nu_{\infty}
be a measured lamination on
\sigma_{\infty}
.
Then
\sigma_{\infty}
has two cusps.
At each cusp
c
of
\sigma_{\infty}
, there are only finitely many leaves of
\nu_{\infty}
ending at
c
by a basic property of geodesic laminations (
[
CEG87
]
).
Then, since
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
, the total weight of those leaves is a positive
2\pi
-multiple.

Lemma 13.2
.

If

\nu_{\infty}
contains an irrational sublamination, then the holonomy of
C_{\infty}
is non-elementary.

Proof.

Suppose that
\nu_{\infty}
contains an irrational sublamination.
Then, there is a minimal irrational sublamination
N
of
L
, so that every leaf of
N
is dense in
N
.
Let
F
be a (topologically) smallest subsurface of
S
containing
N
, such that
F\subset N
is a
\pi_{1}
-injective.
Let
\ell
be a geodesic loop in
\sigma_{\infty}
which is a good approximation of
N
.
Let
\beta_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the equivariant pleated surface corresponding to
(\sigma_{\infty},\nu_{\infty})
.
Then, for each component
R
of
F\setminus\ell
, the restriction of
\beta_{\infty}
to
R
is a quasi-isometric embedding (
[
Bab10
]
).
Thus
\rho_{\infty}|\pi_{1}R
is non-elementary, immediately implying the lemma.
∎

Using the assumption that
C_{t}
is pinched along a single loop, we prove the following:

Proposition 13.3
.

For each component
F
of
S\setminus m
,
the restriction of
\rho_{\infty}
to
\pi_{1}F
is a non-trivial representation in the representation variety.

Remark 13.4
.

On the other hand, the restriction
\rho_{\infty}|\pi_{1}(F)
may be the trivial representation in the character variety (see
Theorem

14.5
).

Proof.

If
\nu_{\infty}
contains an irrational lamination, by Lemma
13.2
,
\rho_{\infty}
is non-elementary.
Then we can assume, without loss of generality, that
\nu_{\infty}
contains only isolated leaves, and
\nu_{\infty}
divides
\sigma_{\infty}
into ideal polygons.

Since each component of
\sigma_{\infty}
has one or two cusps, there is a leaf
\ell
of
\nu_{\infty}
whose endpoints are at a single cusp
c
of
\sigma_{\infty}
.
Let
D
be a small horodisk quotient neighborhood of
c
.
Then
\ell\setminus D
is a long geodesic segment, and by connecting its endpoints by a horocyclic simple arc in
\partial D
, we obtained a simple loop
\gamma
(which is a good approximation of
\ell
); see
Figure

15
(Left).

Pick a lift
\tilde{\ell}
of
\ell
to the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
of
\sigma_{\infty}
and fix an orientation.
Then there is
\alpha_{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}(S)
representing
\gamma
which takes the oriented (bi-infinite) geodesic
\tilde{\ell}
to an oriented geodesic starting from the endpoint of
\tilde{\ell}
; see
Figure

15
(Right).
Clearly
\beta_{\infty}(\tilde{\ell})
is an oriented geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then, by the equivariant proeprty, the holonomy along
\alpha
takes the oriented geodesic
{\beta}_{\infty}(\tilde{\ell})
to an oriented geodesic starting from the endpoint of
{\beta}_{\infty}(\tilde{\ell})
, and thus
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma)\neq I
.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/NontrivialHolonomy] \put(10.0,10.0){{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}$\sigma_{\infty}$}}
\put(26.0,18.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\gamma$}}
\put(70.0,18.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\tilde{\ell}$}}
\put(81.0,11.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\alpha_{\gamma}\,\tilde{\ell}$}}
\put(68.0,10.0){{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}$\mathbb{H}^{2}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 15.

∎

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
G
be a non-trivial subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Consider the (pointwise) stabilizer of the action
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}\curvearrowright G
by conjugation.
Suppose that the stabilizer is continuous.
Then there is a set
\Lambda
of one or two points of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
fixed pointwise by the action of
G
.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
has a continuous stabilizer. Then, clearly,
G
is an elementary subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
First suppose, in addition, that
G
contains a hyperbolic element
h
.
Then no element in
G
exchanges the fixed points of
h
, as otherwise, the stabilizer cannot be continuous.
Therefore
\Lambda
is the fixed point set of
h
, and all elements in
G\setminus\{I\}
must be hyperbolic or elliptic elements with the same axis.

Next suppose that
G
contains a parabolic element
p
.
Then there is no elliptic element or hyperbolic element in
G
, as otherwise, the stabilizer cannot be continuous.
Then
\Lambda
must be the single fixed point of
p
, and all
G\setminus\{I\}
are all parabolic elements with the same fixed point.

Suppose that
G
contains an elliptic element
e
and contains no hyperbolic element.
Then, similarly,
\Lambda
must be the fixed point set of
e
, and
G
contains no parabolic element.
Moreover
G\setminus\{I\}
are all elliptic elements with a common axis.
Then
\Lambda
is the set of the two endpoints of the axis.
∎

Given a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-surface with a cusp such that the holonomy around the cusp is trivial, its developing map continuously extends to the cusp, so that it is a branched covering map near the cusp.

Lemma 13.6
.

Let
F
be a compact surface with finitely many punctures, such that the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
Let
(f,\rho)
be a developing pair of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C
on
F
such that

•

\rho\colon\pi_{1}(F)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is not the trivial representation,

•

the holonomy around each puncture is trivial, and

•

the stabilizer of
\operatorname{Im}\,\rho
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is continuous; thus let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
be the one- or two-point set in Lemma
13.5
.

Then, there is a cusp
p
of
F
such that
f(p)
is not a point of
\Lambda
.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
minus
\Lambda
admits a complete Euclidean metric invariant under
\operatorname{Im}\rho
, which is unique up to scaling.
Thus, if
f
takes all cusps of
F
into
\Lambda
, then the surface
F
minus finitely many points admits a complete Euclidean metric.
This is a contradiction as the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
∎

The next proposition immediately follows from
Proposition

9.2
.

Proposition 13.7
.

Let
F
be a compact connected surface with two punctures, such that the Euler characteristic of
F
is negative.
Let
C=(f,\rho)
be a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
F
, such that

•

\operatorname{Im}\,\rho
has a continuous stabilizer in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
;

•

the holonomy around each puncture is trivial;

•

the degrees around the two punctures are the same.

Then no cusp of
C
maps to a point of
\Lambda
by
f
, where
\Lambda
is as in Lemma
13.5
.

Let
\tilde{m}
be a lift of
m
to
\tilde{S}
.
Let
Q
and
R
be the adjacent components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(m)
across
\tilde{m}
.
Let
\operatorname{Stab}{Q}

and
\operatorname{Stab}{R}
denote the subgroups in
\pi_{1}(S)
which setwise preserve
Q
and
R
, respectively.
Let
C_{\infty}^{Q},C_{\infty}^{R}
denote the component of
C_{\infty}
corresponding
Q,R
(if
m
is non-separating,
C_{\infty}^{Q}=C_{\infty}^{R}
).

We first prove (
1
) in Theorem
13.1
.

Lemma 13.8
.

For sufficiently large
t>0
,
\rho_{t}(m)\neq I
.

Proof.

Suppose, to the contrary, that there is a diverging sequence
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)=I
for each
i
.
We may, in addition, assume that
C_{t_{i}}
converges to
C_{\infty}
as
i\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets as
i\to\infty
.
Then, as
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)=I
and
C_{t}
is pinched along
m
,
for
i\gg 0
, there is a cylinder
A_{i}
in
C_{t_{i}}
homotopic to
m
such that

•

A_{i}
is bounded by round circles (i.e. the development of each boundary component is a round circle on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
),

•

\operatorname{Mod}A_{i}\to\infty
, and

•

C_{t_{i}}\setminus A_{i}
converges to
C_{\infty}
minus cusp neighborhoods bounded by round circles (in other words, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then
A_{i}
is contained in
\epsilon
-thin part of
C_{t_{i}}
).

We can normalize
\rho_{t_{i}}
so that
\rho_{t_{i}}|\operatorname{Stab}R
converges as
i\to\infty
and the developing map
f_{t_{i}}|R
also converges to a developing map of
C_{\infty}^{R}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then the development of
\tilde{m}
converges to a point
p
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

First suppose that the stabilizer of
\rho_{\infty}|\operatorname{Stab}\,Q
is discrete.
Then, there are elements
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
of
\operatorname{Stab}\,Q
with disjoint fixed point sets on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Pick a sequence
\gamma_{i}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that the restriction of the conjugation
\gamma_{i}\rho_{t_{i}}\gamma^{-1}_{i}\eqqcolon\rho_{t_{i}}^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{Stab}Q
converges as
i\to\infty
.
Therefore, the properties of
A_{i}
imply that
\gamma_{i}
must leave every compact in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
As
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
have disjoint fixed point sets in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, one of the fixed point sets does not contain the puncture point of
C^{Q}_{\infty}.

Therefore either
\rho_{t_{i}}(\alpha_{1})
or
\rho_{t_{i}}(\alpha_{2})
diverges to infinity in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
as
i\to\infty
against the hypothesis.

Next suppose that the stabilizer of
\rho_{\infty}|\operatorname{Stab}\,Q
is continuous.
Then, by Proposition
13.7
and Lemma
13.6
, with respect to the normalization
\rho_{t_{i}}^{\prime}
, no cusp of
C_{\infty}^{Q}
develops to a point of
\Lambda
for
C_{\infty}^{Q}
.
Let
\omega\in\operatorname{Stab}Q
such that
\rho_{\infty}(w)
is non-trivial (
Proposition

13.3
).
Then, by the properties of
A_{i}
,

\rho_{t_{i}}(\omega)
must diverges to
\infty
since the continuous stabilizer preserves
\Lambda
.

This is a contradiction against the convergence of
\rho_{t}
.
∎

Lemma 13.9
.

The Fenchel-Nielsen twist coordinate along
m
must diverge to
\infty
or
-\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
13.8
.
Suppose to the contrary that there is a sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<\dots
such that the Fenchel-Nielsen twist parameter of
C_{t_{i}}
along
m
converges as
i\to\infty
.
We normalize
\rho_{t_{i}}
so that
\rho_{t_{i}}|\operatorname{Stab}R
converges as
i\to\infty
the developing map
f_{t_{i}}|R
also converges to a developing map of
C_{\infty}^{R}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then, similarly to the proof of Lemma
13.8
, one can show that
\rho_{t_{i}}|\operatorname{Stab}\,Q
diverges to infinity, since the cylinder
A_{i}
becomes longer and longer and it pushes
\rho_{t_{i}}|\operatorname{Stab}\,Q
farther and farther away; this contradicts the convergence of
\rho_{t}
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

Then, for each
t>0
, let
\iota_{t}
be some power of the Dehn twist of
S
along
m
such that the twist coordinates of
\iota_{t}C_{t}
along
m
is bounded from above and below in
\mathbb{R}
uniformly in
t>0
.
Then, by Lemma
13.9
, the power must diverge to either
\infty
or
-\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

There is a diverging sequence
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
such that
C_{t_{i}}\to C_{\infty}
as
i\to\infty
uniformly on compact.
Let
F
be a component of
S\setminus m
.
Let
\tilde{F}
be the universal cover of
F
.

First suppose that
\rho_{\infty}|F
has a discrete stabilizer (in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
).
Let
F_{\infty}
be a component of
C_{\infty}
which corresponds to
F
.
Then
\operatorname{dev}F_{\infty}
is the limit of
f_{t_{i}}|\tilde{F}
, so that
\lim_{i\to\infty}f_{t_{i}}
takes each boundary component of
\tilde{F}
to a single point corresponding to a cusp of
C_{\infty}
.

Pick a fundamental domain
D_{i}
in
\tilde{F}
with an arc
s_{i}
on
\partial D_{i}\cap\partial\tilde{F}
such that

s_{i}
descends to a loop
m_{i}
isotopic to
m
, the loop
m_{i}
is contained in the
\epsilon_{i}
-thin part of
C_{t_{i}}
with
\epsilon_{i}\searrow 0
as
i\to\infty
, and the development of
m_{i}
is invariant under a one-dimensional subgroup
G_{i}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\rho_{i}(m)
.
As
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)\to I
, the image of
s_{i}
becomes more and more like a round circle
c_{i}
as
i\to\infty
.

Next suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(F)
has a continuous stabilizer.
Then
\rho_{\infty}(F)
is elementary, and the restriction of
f_{t_{i}}
to
\tilde{F}
may not converge to a local homeomorphism, even up to a subsequence.
Nonetheless, as
C_{t_{i}}
converges to
C_{\infty}
in
\mathsf{P}(S\setminus m)
, clearly we can normalize
\rho_{t_{i}}
for the convergence of developing pairs:

Lemma 13.10
.

Suppose that there is no subsequence of
t_{i}
such that
f_{t_{i}}|\tilde{F}
converges to a developing map of
F_{\infty}
.
Then there is a sequence
\gamma_{i}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that, up to a subsequence,
\gamma_{i}(f_{t_{i}}|\tilde{F},\rho_{t_{i}}|\pi_{1}F)
converges to a developing pair
(h_{\infty},\zeta_{\infty})
of
F_{\infty}
.

Next, without normalization, we show a convergence of the developing map as a continuous map.

Proposition 13.11
.

Suppose that there is no subsequence such that the restriction
f_{t_{i}}|\tilde{F}
converges to a developing map of
F_{\infty}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then
f_{t_{i}}|\tilde{F}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}|\pi_{1}F
-equivariant continuous map
f_{F,\infty}\colon\tilde{F}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets, such that each boundary component of
\tilde{F}
maps to a single point.
Moreover, either
f_{F,\infty}
is a constant map to a fixed point of
\rho_{\infty}|F
or there are open disks
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
on
F
such that
f_{F,\infty}
takes
\tilde{F}\setminus\phi^{-1}(D_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup D_{n})
to a fixed point
p
of
\rho_{\infty}(F)
and each lift
\tilde{D}_{i}
of
D_{i}
to
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p\}
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma_{i}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be the sequence and
(h_{\infty},\zeta_{\infty})
be the normalized limit obtained by Lemma
13.10
.
By the non-subconvergence hypothesis,
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}F)
is an elementary representation.
We divide the proof into cases depending on the types of elementary subgroups.

First suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}F)
contains a loxodromic or elliptic element.
Then, let
\ell
be the axis of the loxodromic or the elliptic element.
Then, there is a corresponding loxodromic or elliptic element in
\operatorname{Im}h_{\infty}
, and let
\ell^{\prime}
be its axis.
By the non-subconvergence hypothesis, there is
\omega\in\pi_{1}F
such that
h_{\infty}(\omega)
is a parabolic element but
\rho_{\infty}(\omega)
is the identity in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Thus

\gamma_{i}
must be a hyperbolic element for sufficiently large
i
such that as
i\to\infty
, the translation length of
\gamma_{i}
diverges to infinity.
In addition,
\operatorname{Axis}(\gamma_{i})
converges to the
\ell^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Let
p
be the limit of the repelling fixed point of
\gamma_{i}
, and let
q
be the limit of the attracting fixed point of
\gamma_{i}
, so that
\{p,q\}
are the endpoints of
\ell^{\prime}
.
Note that as
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}(F))
is elementary,
\rho_{\infty}\pi_{1}(F)
preserves
p
and
q
point-wise.

Take a connected compact fundamental domain
Q
in
\tilde{F}
.
We can assume that
Q\cap\partial\tilde{F}
is disjoint from
q
, by perturbing the loop
m_{i}
on
C_{t_{i}}
if necessary.
For simplicity, we first suppose that
h_{\infty}(Q)
is disjoint from
q
.
Then, letting
f_{i}=f_{t_{i}}
, the restriction
f_{i}|Q
converges to the constant map to
p
uniformly, as
i\to\infty
, and thus
f_{i}\colon\tilde{F}\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to the constant map to
p
uniformly on compact subsets.

Suppose that
h_{\infty}(Q)\cap\{q\}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, by the compactness of
Q
, there are finitely many points of
h_{\infty}^{-1}(q)
in the interior of
Q
.
Pick small disjoint open disk neighborhoods of the points in
h_{\infty}^{-1}(q)
in
Q
.
Then, as the disks are contained in a fundamental domain, their images
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
in
F
are disjoint.
Then, as
\zeta_{\infty}
preserves
q
, the restriction of
f_{i}
to
\tilde{F}\setminus\phi^{-1}(D_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup D_{n})
converges to the constant map to
p
uniformly on compact subsets.
Moreover, for each lift
\tilde{D}_{i}
of
D_{i}
to
\tilde{F}
,
\tilde{D}_{i}
contains a unique point mapping to
q
.
Thus up to an isotopy of
S
, we can in addition assume that
f_{i}|D_{i}
converges to a homeomorphism to
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\{p\}
, as desired.
By Lemma
9.1
and Proposition
9.2
, the boundary components of
\tilde{F}
all map to
p
.

Next, suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(F)
contains a (non-trivial) parabolic element but no hyperbolic and elliptic element.
Let
\omega\in\pi_{1}F
such that
\rho_{\infty}(\omega)
is also a non-trivial parabolic element.
Therefore
\rho_{\infty}
and
\rho_{\infty}^{\prime}
are conjugate to each other, and
(f_{t_{i}},\rho_{t_{i}}|\pi_{1}F)
converges to a developing pair of
F_{\infty}
.
This contradicts the non-subconvergence hypothesis.

Last, suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}F)
is the trivial representation.
This case will be similar to the case when
\rho_{\infty}(\pi_{1}F)
contains an elliptic or a hyperbolic element.
Then the normalized holonomy
\zeta_{\infty}
is a parabolic representation.
Let
p
be the parabolic fixed point of
\zeta_{\infty}
.
We can assume that
\gamma_{i}
is a hyperbolic element for
i
large, and the axis of
\gamma_{i}
converges to a geodesic
\ell
starting from
p
.
Let
q
be the other endpoint of
\ell
.
Pick a connected fundamental domain
Q
in
\tilde{F}
so that
h^{-1}(p)
is disjoint from
\partial Q
.
Suppose in addition that no point of
Q
maps to
p
.
Then, up to a subsequence,
f_{i}|\tilde{F}
converges to a constant map to
q
uniformly on compact subsets.
Suppose there are (finitely many) points of
Q
which map to
p
.
Then, similarly to the case of a hyperbolic and an elliptic representation, take disjoint open ball neighborhoods of those points in
Q
, and let
D_{1},D_{2}\dots D_{n}
be disjoint disks on
F
which lift to those open balls.
Then the desired convergence follows similarly.
∎

By Proposition
13.11
, the restriction of
f_{i}
to
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
We next prove the convergence of the boundary components to complete the proof of (
3c
).

Proposition 13.12
.

For each component
\tilde{A}
of
\phi^{-1}(A)
, let
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
be the representative of
m
preserving
\tilde{A}
.
Then, by taking a subsequence so that
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma))\eqqcolon a_{i}
converges to a subset
a_{\infty}\in\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, which is either a point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
or a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, then
f_{\infty}
takes the boundary components of
\tilde{A}
onto the ideal points of
a_{\infty}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
13.8
,
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)\neq I
for sufficiently large
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Thus, by taking a subsequence, we may in addition assume that
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
converges to
I
tangentially to a unit tangent vector of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
at
I
.
Let
G_{i}
be the one-parameter subgroup of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
which contains
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
, such that the cyclic group generated by
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
is asymptotically dense in
G_{i}
with respect to the intrinsic metric on
G_{i}
.
Then the trajectories of
G_{i}
yields a unique foliation of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
except that, if
\rho_{t_{i}}(\gamma)
is elliptic, only of
\mathbb{H}^{3}\setminus a_{i}
(Figure
16
).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/EquidistanceFromAxis] \end{overpic}

Figure 16.

We have chosen a subsequence
t_{i}
so that
C_{t_{i}}\to C_{\infty}
uniformly on every thick part and the axis
a_{i}
converges to a closed subset
a_{\infty}
of
\overline{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
.
Let
P,Q
be the components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
adjacent across
\tilde{A}
.

Claim 13.13
.

Let
\ell
be the common boundary component of
P
and
\tilde{A}
.
Suppose, to the contrary, that
\lim f_{t_{i}}(\ell)
is not a point, in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, of the limit axis
a_{\infty}
.
Then
\rho_{t_{i}}|Q
diverges to
\infty
in
\textstyle\chi
.

Proof.

Let
\iota_{i}
be some power of the Dehn twist of
S
along
m
so that the Fenchel-Nielsen twist parameter of the remarked Riemann surface
\iota_{i}X_{t_{i}}
along
m
is bounded from above and below uniformly in
i
.

Let
\ell^{\prime}
be the common boundary component of
\tilde{A}
and
Q
.
By Proposition
13.3
, there is
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
belonging to
\operatorname{Stab}Q
such that
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma)
is
not
the identity matrix.
We may in addition assume the axis of
\rho_{t_{i}}(\iota_{i}\cdot\gamma)
converges to the point
f_{F,\infty}(\ell)
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
(if
\rho_{\infty}(\operatorname{Stab}Q)
is elementary, use Lemma
13.6
and Proposition
13.7
).
By the tangential convergence of
\rho_{i}(m)\to I
, the
G_{i}
-invariant foliation
\mathcal{F}_{i}
of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
If
f_{P,\infty}(\ell)
is not the ideal point of
a_{\infty}
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{i}(\iota_{i}\cdot\gamma))
be eventually disjoint from every compact subset in the space of the leaves of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
.
Therefore, since the
G_{i}
-invariant foliations
\mathcal{F}_{i}
limit to
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
,
\operatorname{Axis}(\rho_{i}(\gamma))
also leaves every compact subset of the leaf space of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
.
Hence
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
must diverge to
\infty
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, which is a contradiction.
(Figure
17
.)
∎

This claim completes the proof.

13.12

It remains only to prove the surjectivity in
Theorem

13.1

(
3c
):

Lemma 13.14
.

Suppose that
a_{\infty}
is a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then
f_{\infty}(\ell)
and
f_{\infty}(\ell^{\prime})
are the different endpoints of
a_{\infty}
.

Proof.

By Claim
13.13
.

f_{i}|\ell
converges to the constant map to an endpoint of
a_{\infty}
.

Let
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the power of the Dehn twist along
m
which gives
\iota_{i}\in{\rm MCG}(S)
.
Thus
\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n_{i}})
is a hyperbolic element whose axis
a_{i}
converges to
a_{\infty}
, and its translation length diverges to infinity as
i\to\infty
.
Then the attracting fixed point of
\rho_{i}(\gamma^{n_{i}})
converges to the endpoint of
a_{\infty}
which is not
f_{\infty}(\ell)
.
Thus
f_{\infty}(\ell^{\prime})
must be at the other endpoint.
∎
