13.12

13.0.1.
Convergence of pleated surfaces when
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I

First we compare developing maps of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures and the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
.
Let
\ell
be the geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
0
to
\infty
of
\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Let
\Psi\colon\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\to\ell
be the continuous extension of the nearest point projection
\mathbb{H}^{3}\to\ell
.
Then, the composition is
\Psi\circ\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
is the Epstein map of the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
\mathbb{C}
given by
\exp
.

Recall that, given a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
C=(X,q)
, for
x\in\mathbb{C}
,
\penalty\ d(x)
is the Euclidean distance from
x
to the set of the zeros of the holomorphic differential
q
.
Note that, if
d(x)
is large, then we can naturally embed a large neighborhood of
x
into
\mathbb{C}(\cong\mathbb{E}^{2})
by an isometric map onto its image, so that vertical leaves map into horizontal lines, and horizontal leaves map into vertical lines.

Proposition 13.15
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
R>0
, such that, if
x\in C
satisfies
d(x)>R
, then the Epstein map
\Sigma\colon\tilde{C}\cong\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
is
\epsilon
-close, in the
C^{1}
-topology, to the composition of the collapsing map
\tilde{\kappa}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{2}
and the bending map
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
at every lift
\tilde{x}
of
x
.

Proof of Proposition
13.15
.

Lemma 13.16
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
R>0
, such that, if
z\in\tilde{C}
satisfies
d(z)>R
, then the maximal ball centered at
z
is
\epsilon
-close to the maximal ball of the corresponding exponential map.

Proof.

As the Epstein map
of
C
and
\exp
are close, their developing maps are also close.
This implies the closeness of their maximal balls centered at
z
and their ideal points.
∎

The proposition follows from the above lemma, and
Proposition

4.9
.

13.15

Recall that we have already proved Theorem
13.1
(
1
), (
2
), (
3
) regarding the asymptotic behavior of
C_{t}
using the decomposition of
C_{t}
into the restriction of
C_{t}
to the thin part
A
and its complement.
We prove additional compatibility of the corresponding bending map.

Proposition 13.17
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
.
Then for every diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, up to taking a subsequence, there are a sequence of diffeomorphisms
\iota_{i}\colon S\to\tau_{t_{i}}
representing the marking of
C_{t_{i}}
and a path of cylinders
A_{i}
in
C_{t_{i}}
homotopy equivalent to
m
, such that
in addition to Theorem
13.1
(
1
), (
2
), (
3
), the following holds:

(1)

A
maps to
A_{i}
by
\iota_{i}
;

(2)

\beta_{t_{i}}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t_{i}}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
uniformly on compact subsets;

(3)

for each connected component
F
of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
, the restriction of
\beta_{t_{i}}\circ\kappa_{t_{i}}
to
F
converges to the pleated surface of the corresponding component of
C_{\infty}
or the constant map to an ideal point of
a_{\infty}
(in
Proposition

13.12
);

(4)

letting
\tilde{A}
be a connected component of
\phi^{-1}(A)
in
\tilde{S}
, then
\beta_{t_{i}}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{t_{i}}|\tilde{A}
converges to a map onto
a_{\infty}

uniformly on compact subsets in
\tilde{A}
with respect to a fixed closed disk metric on
\mathbb{H}^{3}\cup\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Proof.

For
t\gg 0
, there is a one-parameter family of loops homotopic to
m
such that their developments are invariant under a unique one-dimensional subgroup
G_{t}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
which contains
\rho_{t}(m)
(as in the proof of
Proposition

13.11
).
Then we can pick a cylinder
A_{t}
in
C_{t}
homotopy equivalent to
m
, such that

•

A_{t}
is foliated by loops whose developments are invariant under
G_{t}
for each
t\gg 0
,

•

C_{t}\setminus A_{t}
converges to
C_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
, and

•

\operatorname{Mod}A_{t}\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.

By the second property,
A_{t}
is contained in a thinner and thinner part of
C_{t}
as
t\to\infty
.
Then, the developing map of
A_{t}
is the restriction of
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
to a
bi-infinite strip

T_{t}
, i.e. a region in
\mathbb{C}
bounded by a pair of parallel lines.
Then its deck transformation group (
\cong\mathbb{Z}
) is generated by a translation of
T_{t}
.
Then
A_{t}
has a natural Euclidean metric by identifying
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{E}^{2}
.

Recall that
A
is a cylinder in
S
homotopic to
m
, and fix a finite volume Euclidean structure on
A
with geodesic boundary (by picking a homeomorphism
A\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\times[-1,1]
).
We can easily pick a marking
\iota_{t}\colon S\to C_{t}
such that

•

\iota_{t}
takes
A
to
A_{t}
((
1
));

•

the restriction of
C_{t}
to
\iota_{t}(S\setminus A)
converges to
C_{\infty}
;

•

\iota_{t}|A
is linear with respect to the Euclidean structures on
A
and
A_{t}
.

Given a component
F
of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
, suppose that
f_{t_{i}}|F
converges to a developing map of the component of
C_{\infty}
.
Then, clearly
\beta_{i}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{i}|F
converges to a pleated surface for the corresponding component of
C_{\infty}
.
By Proposition
13.11
, if
f_{t_{i}}|F
does not converge to a developing map, then
\beta_{i}\circ\tilde{\kappa}_{i}|F
converges to the constant map to an ideal point of the axis limit
a_{\infty}
.
Thus we have (
3
).

Last we prove (
4
).
As we have already shown the convergence of the developing map in the thick part, we need to show that the convergence extends to the convergence on the neck.
As the developing map of some components of
S\setminus m
may degenerate as described in
Proposition

13.11
, accordingly one needs to be careful about the behavior of
\beta_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}
on the neck.

By Theorem
13.1
(
2
), the Fenchel-Nielsen twisting parameter of
C_{t}
along
m
diverges to either
\infty
or
-\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
We can assume that the twisting of
C_{t}
along
m
occurs in
A_{t}
by isotopy of
S
.

(Case One) Suppose that
a_{\infty}:=\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Axis}\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is a bi-infinite geodesic.
Then
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
is hyperbolic if
i
is large enough, and the translation length of
\rho_{t_{i}}(m)
times the number of twist goes to infinity as
i\to\infty
.
For
r>0
, let
U_{i}(r)
be the
r
-neighborhood of
a_{i}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Clearly
U_{i}(r)
is invariant under
\rho_{i}(m)
.
Let
(\tau_{i},L_{i})\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
be the Thurston parameters of
C_{i}
for each
i
.
Pick
\epsilon>0
less than the Bers’ constant, and let
N_{i}=N_{i}^{\epsilon}
be the
\epsilon
-thin part of
\tau_{i}
.
Let
\tilde{N_{i}}
be the lift of
N_{i}
to the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
of
\tau_{i}
invariant under the fixed representative the loop
m
in
\pi_{1}(S)
.
Let
\ell_{i,1},\ell_{i,2}
denote the boundary components of
\tilde{N}_{i}
, which connect the endpoints of the geodesic
a_{i}

Lemma 13.18
.

If
r>0
is sufficiently large, then
\beta_{i}(\tilde{N}_{i})
is contained in
U_{i}(r)
for sufficiently large
i
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{A}
be the lift of
A
to
\tilde{S}
which is invariant under
m\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
adjacent across a lift
\tilde{A}
.
Suppose, to the contrary, that for every
r>0
, the image
\beta_{i}(\tilde{N}_{i})
is
not
eventually contained in
U_{i}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then, either

(i)

for every
r>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,1})
and
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,2})
are both not contained in
U_{i}
, or

(ii)

for every large
r>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, then one of
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,1})
and
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,2})
is contained in
U_{i}
but the other is not.

First, suppose (i).
Then, let
\phi_{i}\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\tau_{i}
be the universal covering map.
Let
P_{i,1}^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}_{i,2}
be the component of
\mathbb{H}^{2}\setminus\phi_{i}^{-1}(N_{i})
.
For each
i=1,2,\dots
and
j=1,2
, pick compact fundamental domains
D_{i,j}
of
\operatorname{Stab}P_{j}\curvearrowright P_{i,j}^{\prime}
, such that
D_{i,j}
converges to a fundamental domain of the
\epsilon
-thick components of
\tau_{\infty}
.
Recall that
U_{i}
is invariant under
\rho_{i}(m)
.
Then, for every
r>0
, if
i
is sufficiently large, both fundamental domains of
P_{i,1}^{\prime}
or
P_{i,2}^{\prime}
map to outside
U_{i}
by
\beta_{i}
.
Therefore, it follows from
Proposition

13.3
and
Proposition

13.7
that
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{1}
or
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{2}
must diverge to
\infty
up to a subsequence, against to the convergence of
\rho_{i}
.

Next we suppose (ii).
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,1})
, not contained in
U_{i}
but
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,2})
is contained in
U_{i}
for sufficiently large
i
.
Then, for every
r>0
, similarly, the fundamental domain
P_{i,1}
of
P_{1}^{\prime}
maps to outside
U_{i}
by
\beta_{i}
if
i
is sufficiently large.
Then, by the assumption of
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,2})
being contained in
U_{i}
, one can similarly show
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{1}
diverges to
\infty
, up to a subsequence.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/DivergingPushedByU.jpg] \put(70.0,20.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$U_{i}^{\prime}(r^{\prime})$}}
\put(80.0,14.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$r^{\prime}$}}
\put(89.0,20.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$a_{i}^{\prime}$}}
\put(43.0,20.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$a_{i}$}}
\put(37.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$r$}}
\put(30.0,25.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$U_{i}(r)$}}
\put(19.0,12.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\rho_{i}(\omega)$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 18.
This figure illustrates the divergence of
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{j}
in the upper half space model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The arrows indicate how the action by an element
\omega
in
\operatorname{Stab}P_{j}
changes, and it diverges as
i
increases in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, where
r^{\prime}>r
and
i^{\prime}>i
.

∎

If follows from
Lemma

13.18
that,
for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, by taking
\delta>0
sufficiently smaller than
\epsilon>0
above, similarly letting
\tilde{N}_{i}^{\delta}
be the
\rho_{i}(m)
-invariant lift of
N_{i}^{\delta}
to the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, the image
\beta_{i}(\tilde{N}_{i}^{\delta})
is
\epsilon^{\prime}
-close to the axis
a_{i}
for sufficiently large
i
.

Recall that we have a convergence of
\beta_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}
on
P_{1},P_{2}
so that, in the limit, the boundary components of
\tilde{A}
map to the endpoints of
a_{\infty}
.
Therefore, by taking an appropriate isotopy of
S
,
\beta_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}
converges to a continuous map, up to a subsequence, such that
\tilde{N}
maps to
a_{\infty}
.

(Case Two)
Suppose that
a_{\infty}
is a single point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.
Pick any horoball
B
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
tangent at
a_{\infty}
.
For each
i
, pick a subset
U_{i}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
converging to
B
uniformly on compact subsets as
i\to\infty
, such that, if
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
is either hyperbolic or elliptic, then
U_{i}
is an
r_{i}
-neighborhood of
a_{i}
for some
r_{i}>0
, and if
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
is parabolic, then
U_{i}
is a horoball centered at the parabolic fixed point of
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
.

For sufficiently large
i
, Let
N_{i}
be the
\epsilon
-thin part of
\tau_{i}
homotopy equivalent to
m
.
Let
\tilde{N}_{i}
be a component of
\psi_{i}^{-1}(N_{i})
.

Lemma 13.19
.

If
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, then
\beta_{i}(\tilde{N}_{i})
is eventually contained in
U_{i}
as
i\to\infty
.
Therefore,
\beta_{i}\circ\kappa_{i}|\tilde{N}
converges to the constant map to the point
a_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(A)
adjacent across the lift
\tilde{A}
of
A
invariant by
\rho_{i}(m)
.
Suppose, to the contrary, for every
\epsilon>0
, the image
\beta_{i}(\tilde{N}^{\epsilon}_{i})
is not eventually contained in
U_{i}
.
Then, at least one of
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,1})
or
\beta_{i}(\ell_{i,2})
is not contained in
U_{i}
for sufficiently large
i
.
Therefore, it follows from using
Proposition

13.3
and
Proposition

13.7
that either
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{1}
or
\rho_{i}|\operatorname{Stab}P_{2}
diverges to
\infty
, up to a subsequence.
∎

13.17

13.0.2.
Convergence of holomorphic quadratic differentials when
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I

We next describe the limit quadratic differential.
In the case that
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
, the singular Euclidean structure
E_{t_{i}}
contains a flat cylinder
A_{t}
homotopic to
m
, such that
\operatorname{Mod}A_{t}\to\infty
and the complex length of its circumference converges to a positive multiple of
\pi/\sqrt{2}
, by Proposition
5.2
.
Therefore

Proposition 13.20
.

Let
C_{\infty}
be the limit of
C_{t}
in Theorem
13.1
(
3b
).
Then, the Schwarzian parameters of
C_{\infty}
consist of a Riemann surface with two punctures homeomorphic to
S\setminus m
and a holomorphic quadratic differential
q_{\infty}
, such that both punctures are a pole of order two and their residues are the same non-zero integer multiple of
\sqrt{2}\pi
.

13.1.
Non-discreteness of holonomy

We in addition show the non-discreteness of the holonomy representation
\rho_{t}
for large
t
.

Theorem 13.21
.

Suppose that
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
.
Then
\operatorname{Im}\rho_{t}\subset{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
is a non-discrete subgroup for sufficiently large
t>0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\rho_{t}(m)\to I
but
\rho_{t}(m)\neq I
(Theorem
13.1
(
1
)).
For each component
F
of
S\setminus m
,
\rho_{t}(\pi_{1}(F))
is nontrivial for sufficiently large
t\gg 0
(Proposition
13.3
).
Recall from
Proposition

13.12
that, if
C_{t_{i}}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure of a punctured surface homeomorphic to
F
for a diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, then, in the limit, its cusp point develops to an endpoint of the limit of the axis of
\rho_{t_{i}}
.
Therefore the subgroup of
\operatorname{Im}\rho_{t}
generated by
\{\,\rho_{t}(m)\gamma\rho_{t}(m)^{-1}\,|\,\gamma\in\rho_{t}(F)\,\}
is non-elementary since the endpoint in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
is not preserve by some non-trivial element in
\rho_{t}(\pi_{1}(F))
by (Lemma
13.6
and Proposition
13.7
).
As
\rho_{t}(m)\to I
, by the Margulis lemma,
\operatorname{Im}\rho_{t}
cannot be discrete.
∎

14.
Examples of exotic degeneration

We construct examples of a path
C_{t}=(f_{t},\rho_{t})
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
S
asymptotically pinched along a loop
m
as
t\to\infty
such that
\rho_{\infty}(m)=I
and
[\rho_{t}]
converges in
\textstyle\chi
as
t\to\infty
, as in the second case of
C
.
We construct two examples: one with
\rho_{t}(m)
hyperbolic and one with
\rho_{t}(m)
elliptic for all sufficiently large
t>0
.

14.1.
Hyperbolic
\rho_{t}(m)
converging to
I
.

Let
E
be the singular Euclidean surface obtained from an
L
-shaped polygon by identifying the opposite edges (Figure
19
).
Then
E
has exactly one cone point, and its cone angle is
6\pi
.
Let
F
be the underlying topological surface of
E
, which is a closed surface of genus two.
Let
E^{\prime}
denote
E
minus the cone point, and let
F^{\prime}
denote the underlying topological surface of
E^{\prime}
.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/LShape] \put(37.9,30.0){$a_{1}$}
\put(60.0,13.0){$a_{2}$}
\put(45.0,27.0){$b_{1}$}
\put(46.0,10.0){$b_{2}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 19.

Let
\ell_{p}
be the (oriented) peripheral loop around the removed cone point.
Let
\xi\colon\pi_{1}(F^{\prime})\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be the holonomy of
E^{\prime}
.
Then, as
F^{\prime}
has a Euclidean structure, the image of
\xi
consists of parabolic elements, and we can assume that its image consists of upper triangular matrices with 1’s on the diagonal.
In particular
\xi(\ell_{p})=I
(as before, by abuse of notation, we regard
\ell_{p}
also as a fixed element of
\pi_{1}(S)
by picking a basepoint of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\ell
.)
Notice that there is a point in the universal cover
\tilde{E}
of
E
corresponding to
\ell_{p}\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
(Namely, by lifting
\ell_{p}
to a loop in the universal cover
E
starting from the base point, there is a unique cone point of
\tilde{E}
in the disk region bounded by the lift.)

Proposition 14.1
.

There is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures,
D_{t}=(h_{t},\xi_{t})
, on
F^{\prime}
converging to
E^{\prime}=(h,\xi)
as
t\to\infty
, such that
\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})
is a hyperbolic translation whose axis converges to a geodesic connecting the global (parabolic) fixed point of
\xi
and the
h
-image of the corresponding singular point of
\tilde{E}
.

Proof.

Note that elements of
\operatorname{Im}\xi
are translations of
\mathbb{C}
.
Pick non-separating simple closed curves
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}
on
E
as in Figure
19
forming a standard generating set of
\pi_{1}(F)
so that

•

for each
i=1,2,

a_{i}
and
b_{i}
intersect in a single point, and
[a_{1},b_{1}][a_{2},b_{2}]=I
,

•

the translation directions of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are the same and the translation direction of
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are the same, and

•

the translation directions of
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are orthogonal for each
i=1,2
.

Let
c
be a separating loop on
E
which separates
\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
and
\{a_{2},b_{2}\}
.
Then, let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be the components of
F\setminus c
which are homeomorphic to a torus minus a disk.

Lemma 14.2
.

Let
q_{i}
be any geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
starting from the global fixed point
p\in\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
of
\operatorname{Hol}E
, and let
H_{i}
be the hyperbolic plane, in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, containing an
\langle a_{i}\rangle
-orbit of
q_{i}
.
For each
i=1,2
,
given any path
h_{i,t}\,(t\geq 0)
of hyperbolic elements in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that

(1)

the axis of
h_{i,t}
is orthogonal to
H_{i}
at a point in
q_{i}
for all
t\geq 0
, and

(2)

h_{i,t}\to I
as
t\to\infty
.

Then, there is a path
\zeta_{i,t}\colon\pi_{1}(F_{i})\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
of homomorphisms which converges to the restriction of
\operatorname{Hol}(E)
to
\pi_{1}(F_{i})
as
t\to\infty
such that
\zeta_{i,t}(c)=h_{i,t}
.

Proof.

The point
p
is contained in the ideal boundary of
H_{i}
.
Let
r_{i}
be a geodesic in
H_{i}
, such that
R(r_{i})R(q_{i})=\xi(a_{i})
, where
R(r_{i})
and
R(q_{i})
are the
\pi
-rotations of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
about
r_{i}
and
q_{i}
, respectively (Figure
20
, Right).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/PathsOfAxies] \put(22.0,15.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$q_{t,i}$}}
\put(33.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$q_{t,i}^{\prime}$}}
\put(12.0,20.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$r_{t,i}$}}
\put(86.0,24.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$q_{i}$}}
\put(69.0,20.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$r_{i}$}}
\par\put(32.0,50.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$p$}}
\put(60.0,30.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$H_{i}$}}
\put(78.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$H_{i}^{\perp}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 20.

Let
H_{i}^{\perp}
be the hyperbolic plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
orthogonal to the hyperbolic plane
H_{i}
along the geodesic
q_{i}
.
As
\operatorname{Axis}(h_{i,t})
is in
H_{i}^{\perp}
and orthogonal to
q_{i}
, we let
q_{i,t}
and
q_{i,t}^{\prime}
be continuous paths of geodesics in
H_{i}^{\perp}
such that

R(q_{i,t})R(q_{i,t}^{\prime})=h_{i,t}
, the geodesics
q_{i,t}
and
q_{i,t}^{\prime}
converge to
q_{i}
as
t\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets, and the
\pi
-rotation
R(q_{i})
exchanges
q_{i,t}
and
q_{i,t}^{\prime}
.
By this symmetry, there is a path of geodesics
r_{i,t}
in
H_{i}
such that, for all
t\gg 0
,

•

there is a hyperbolic plane intersecting
r_{t,i},q_{t,i},q_{i,t}^{\prime}
orthogonally, and

•

d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(r_{i,t},q_{i,t})=d_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(r_{i,t},q_{i,t}^{\prime})
.

Thus by the symmetry,
\operatorname{tr}R(q_{i,t})R(r_{i})=\operatorname{tr}R(q_{i,t}^{\prime})R(r_{i})\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-2,2]
.

The surface
F_{i}\setminus a_{i}
is a pair of pants, and two of its boundary components correspond to
a_{i}
.
Consider the path of homomorphisms
\zeta_{i,t}\colon\pi_{1}(F_{i}\setminus a_{i})\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
for
t>0
, such that the two boundary components corresponding to
a_{i}
map to
R(q_{i,t})R(r_{i})
and
R(r_{i})R(q_{i,t}^{\prime})
— thus the other boundary corresponding to
\partial F_{i}
maps to
R(q_{i,t})R(q_{i,t}^{\prime})=h_{i,t}
(see
[
Gol09
]
).
Then, by Theorem
5.6
, there is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
F_{i}\setminus a_{i}
with holonomy
\zeta_{i,t}
which converges to the component of
E\setminus(c\cup a_{i})
as
t\to\infty
corresponding to
F_{i}\setminus a_{i}
.
As the holonomies along the two boundary components are conjugate, for large enough
t>0
, there is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
\Sigma_{i,t}
on
F_{i}
which converges to the component of
E\setminus c
, so that
\operatorname{Hol}\Sigma_{i,t}|\pi_{1}F_{i}=\zeta_{i,t}
.
In particular, the holonomy of
\Sigma_{i,t}
around the puncture is the hyperbolic element
R(q_{i,t})R(q_{i,t}^{\prime})=h_{i,t}
.
∎

\put(57.0,25.0){$v_{2,t}$}
\put(22.0,21.0){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{-0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{-0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{-0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-1.0pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.92387pt\raisebox{0.38266pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.70709pt\raisebox{0.70709pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.38266pt\raisebox{0.92387pt}[0.0pt]{$u_{2,t}$}\hss}\mbox{$u_{2,t}$}}
\put(51.0,2.0){$q$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 21.

Notice that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are totally geodesic hyperbolic planes in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
tangent at
p
.
Therefore we can, in addition, assume that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are different and
H_{1}^{\perp}=H_{2}^{\perp}\eqqcolon H
.
Pick a geodesic
q
in
H
initiating from
p
contained in the region bounded by the geodesics
q_{1}=H\cap H_{1}
and
q_{2}=H\cap H_{2}
.

Proposition 14.3
.

We can choose, the path of the hyperbolic isometries
h_{1,t},h_{2,t}
(given by Lemma
14.2
) so that their composition
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
is eventually a hyperbolic element whose axis converges to
q
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Pick
h_{1,t}
and
h_{2,t}
such that their axes converge to the parabolic fixed point
p
.
Since
h_{1,t}
and
h_{2,t}
converge to
I
, their product
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
also converges to
I
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

For each
i=1,2
, let
u_{i,t}
be the attracting fixed point, and let
v_{i,t}
be the repelling fixed point of
h_{i,t}
.
We may first assume that the endpoints of
\operatorname{Axis}h_{1,t},\operatorname{Axis}h_{2,t}
lie on
\partial H
in this cyclic order
u_{2,t},v_{1,t},v_{2,t},u_{1,t}
(Figure
21
).
The composition
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
fixes a point on the arc in
\partial H
between
v_{1,t}
and
v_{2,t}
for each
t>0
.
Note that the segment contains
p
.
Then as
\operatorname{Axis}(h_{1,t}),\operatorname{Axis}(h_{2,t})
converge to the parabolic fixed point
p
, there is a fixed point of
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
converging to
p
.
Moreover, as
h_{2,t}\to I
, one can continuously adjust the translation length of
h_{1,t}
so that
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
also fixes the other endpoint of
q
for sufficiently large
t>0
.
Let
s
be the endpoint of the geodesic
q
which is
not

p
.
Then, after this adjustment, clearly
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}(s)=s
holds for all large
t>0
and
h_{2,t}(s)\to s
as
t\to\infty
.
Since the axis of the hyperbolic element
h_{1,t}
converges to the ideal point
p(\neq s)
, the translation length of
h_{1,t}
must converge to zero; thus
h_{1,t}
converges to the identity (so that the condition (2) in
Lemma

14.2
remains satisfied after the modification).

Clearly the composition
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
does
not
fix the endpoints of the axes of the hyperbolic elements
h_{1,t}
and
h_{2,t}
for all large
t>0
.
Therefore
h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
is a hyperbolic element with the axis
q
for sufficiently large
t>0
, which is not the identity.
∎

Let
h_{1,t},h_{2,t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be the paths given by Proposition
14.3
.
Then, by Lemma
14.2
, for each
i=1,2
, we have a path of homomorphisms
\zeta_{i,t}\colon\pi_{1}(F_{i})\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
\zeta_{i,t}(\ell)=h_{i,t}
for
t\gg 0
.
Then there is a unique path
\zeta_{t}\colon\pi(F^{\prime})\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that
\zeta_{t}|\pi_{1}(F_{i})=\zeta_{i,t}
for
i=1,2
; thus
\zeta_{t}(\ell_{p})=h_{1,t}h_{2,t}
.
Then, by the holonomy theorem (
Theorem

5.6
), there is a path
D_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on
F^{\prime}
with holonomy
\zeta_{t}
for
t\gg 0
such that
D_{t}
converges to
E^{\prime}
as
t\to\infty
.

14.1

Remark 14.4
.

Since
\xi_{t}
converges to the parabolic representation
\xi
and the axis of the hyperbolic element
\rho_{t}(\ell_{p})
converges to a geodesic starting from the parabolic fixed point of
\xi
as
t\to\infty
,
by normalizing by an appropriate power
r_{t}
of isometries
\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})
, the conjugation
\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})^{r_{t}}\cdot\xi_{t}\cdot\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})^{-r_{t}}
converges to the trivial representation, and the developing map
\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})^{r_{t}}h_{t}
converges to the constant map to the endpoint of
q
which is not
p
.

14.1.1.
Constructing a closed surface from punctured surfaces

To make a desired example of exotic degeneration,
we take two copies
D_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-surfaces with a single puncture from
Proposition

14.1
, and glue them together with many twists.

Theorem 14.5
.

There is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
C_{t}=(f_{t},\rho_{t})
on a closed surface
S
of genus four with following properties:

•

The conformal structure
X_{t}
is pinched along a separating loop
m
as
t\to\infty
; let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be the connected components of
S\setminus m
.

•

\rho_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
converges in the representation variety as
t\to\infty
.

•

Pick an element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
whose free homotopy class is
m
. Then
\rho_{\infty}(\gamma)=I
, and, for all
t>0
, the holonomy
\rho_{t}(\gamma)
is a hyperbolic element such that its axis
a_{t}
converges to a geodesic
a_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
as
t\to\infty
.

•

Let
\tilde{F}_{1},\tilde{F}_{2}
be the connected components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(m)
which are adjacent across the lift
\tilde{m}
of
m
preserved by
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
.
Then

C_{t}|\tilde{F}_{1}
converges to the developing map of a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a genus two surface minus a point such that the cusp maps to an endpoint of
a_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
.

•

f_{t}|\tilde{F}_{2}
converges to the constant map to the other endpoint of
a_{\infty}
uniformly on compact subsets, and
\rho_{\infty}|\pi_{1}(F_{2})
is the trivial representation.

Remark 14.6
.

In fact,
\operatorname{Im}\rho_{\infty}
consists of parabolic elements with a global fixed point on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, and
therefore the limit representation
\rho_{\infty}
is identified with the trivial representation in the character variety
\textstyle\chi
.
In other words, the frontier of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
-orbit of
\rho_{\infty}
contains the trivial representation.
Thus, there is a path
\alpha_{t}\,(t>0)
in
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
such that
\alpha_{t}\rho_{t}\alpha_{t}^{-1}
converges to the trivial representation.

Proof.

For sufficiently large
t>0
, the
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
D_{t}
with a single puncture from
Proposition

14.1
has a cusp neighborhood
N_{t}
foliated by admissible loops whose developments are invariant under the one-dimensional subgroup
G_{t}
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
\xi_{t}(\ell_{p})
.
We can assume that
N_{t}
changes continuously in
t
and is asymptotically the empty set on
E^{\prime}
as
t\to\infty
.
Note that as
G_{t}
is a one-dimensional subgroup
\rho_{t}(m)
of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, integer powers
\rho_{t}(m)^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
continuously extends to real powers.

First take two copies
\Sigma_{1,t},\Sigma_{2,t}
of
D_{t}\setminus N_{t}
and, since the boundary of
N_{t}
are invariant by the one-parameter subgroup
G_{t}
,
glue them together along their boundary components without adding a twist.
Let
C_{t}^{\prime}=(f_{t}^{\prime},\rho_{t}^{\prime})
be the resulting developing pair.
Then we can normalize by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that the axis of the hyperbolic element
\rho_{t}^{\prime}(m)
is the geodesic
q
for all
t
.
In addition, we can renormalize the developing pair by
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that the restriction of
f_{t}^{\prime}
to
\tilde{F}_{1}
and the restriction of
\rho_{t}^{\prime}
to the stabilizer
\operatorname{Stab}\tilde{F}_{1}
of
\tilde{F}_{1}
in
\pi_{1}(S)
converges to a developing pair of
E^{\prime}
as
t\to\infty
.
Then, as
N_{t}
converges to the empty set, the restriction of
\rho_{t}^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{Stab}\tilde{F_{2}}
leaves every compact in the representation variety, and the restriction of
f_{t}^{\prime}
to
\tilde{F}_{2}
does not converge to a continuous map as
t\to\infty
.

Recall that the holonomy
\rho_{t}^{\prime}
along
m
is a hyperbolic element with axis
q
, and the translation length of
\rho_{t}^{\prime}(m)
goes to zero as
t\to\infty
.
Therefore, when we glue
\Sigma_{1,t},\Sigma_{2,t}
of
D_{t}\setminus N_{t}
, we can continuously add more and more twists along
m
, which conjugates the structure on
F_{2}
by
\rho_{t}^{\prime}(m)
raised to the power of the amount of twist along
q
, so that

•

the restriction of
f_{t}^{\prime}
to
\tilde{F}_{1}
and the restriction of
\rho_{t}^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{Stab}\tilde{F}_{1}
still converges to a developing pair for
E^{\prime}
, and

•

the restriction of
\rho_{t}^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{Stab}\tilde{F}_{2}
converges to the trivial representation, and the restriction of
f_{t}^{\prime}
to
\tilde{F}_{2}
converges to the constant map to the other endpoint of
q
(by
Remark

14.4
) as
t\to\infty
.

We obtained a desired path
C_{t}^{\prime}
.
∎

14.2.
Elliptic
\rho_{t}(m)
converging to the identity

In this section, we construct an example of
C_{t}=(f,\rho_{t})
in Theorem
C
(ii) such that
\rho_{t}(m)
is an elliptic element for all sufficiently large
t>0
and it converges to
I
as
t\to\infty
.

Given an elliptic element
e\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
, normalize the unit disk model
\mathbb{D}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
centered at the origin, so that
\operatorname{Axis}(e)
is contained in the axis of the third coordinate.
Let
\zeta\in(0,2\pi)
be the rotation angle of
e
.
Then, define
b_{e}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
by
x\mapsto(\cos(\zeta x)\sin x,\sin(\zeta x)\sin x,\cos x)
which is equivariant under
\mathbb{Z}\to\langle e\rangle
.

Lemma 14.7
.

Let
r
be a geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Pick a parallel vector field
V\subset T\mathbb{H}^{3}
along
r
such that
V
is orthogonal to
r
.
Then, there are a path of (nontrivial) elliptic elements
e_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
and a continuous function
\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in
t>0
which satisfies the following:

•

e_{t}\to I
as
t\to\infty
.

•

\operatorname{Axis}(e_{t})
orthogonally intersects
r
, and
\operatorname{Axis}(e_{t})
converges to an endpoint of
r
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
as
t\to\infty
.

•

Letting
\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that the angle between
\operatorname{Axis}e_{t}
and
V
is
\theta_{t}\mod 2\pi
, when an orientation of
Axis(e_{t})
is fixed continuously in
t
.

•

Let
u_{t}=2\theta_{t}
. Then the rotation angle of
e_{t}^{u_{t}}
is
\pi
for all
t\geq 0
, so that
e_{t}^{u_{t}}
takes
r
to itself, reversing the orientation.

Proof.

It is easy to construct an example satisfying the first three conditions.
Then adjust the rotation angle of
e_{t}
so that it also satisfies the last condition.

∎

Lemma 14.8
.

Let
e_{t}
be as in Lemma
14.7
.
Let
p
be the endpoint of
r
to which
\operatorname{Axis}(e_{t})
converges.
Pick a round disk
D
in
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
containing
p
such that the hyperbolic plane in bounded by the boundary of
D
is orthogonal to the geodesic
r
.
Then, there is a path
A_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on an annulus
A
with smooth boundary for sufficiently large
t\gg 0
, such that

•

A_{t}
converges to the once-punctured disk
D\setminus\{p\}
as
t\to\infty
as a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure, and

•

the developments of the both boundary components of
A_{t}
are curves equivalent to
b_{e_{t}}
by elements of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

For sufficiently large
t>0
, one can easily construct the fundamental membrane for
A_{t}
for sufficiently large
t>0
(Figure
22
).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/AlmostPuncturedDisk] \end{overpic}

Figure 22.

∎

Proposition 14.9
.

Let
P
be a pair of pants, and pick a boundary component
\ell
of
P
.
Let
\tilde{\ell}
be a lift of
\ell
to the universal cover of
P
.
Consider a (flat) Euclidean cylinder with geodesic boundary, and let
P_{\infty}
be the surface obtained by removing an interior point
p
of
P_{\infty}
; regard
P_{\infty}
as a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on
P
, and let
(h,\xi)
be its developing pair, so that
h
takes
\tilde{\ell}
to a single point
v
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
.

Let
r
be the geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
v
and the parabolic fixed point of
h
, and
let
e_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be a path of (non-trivial) elliptic elements given by Lemma
14.7
for
r
.

Then, there is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
P_{t}=(h_{t},\xi_{t})
on
P
satisfying the following:

(1)

For all
t>0
,

\xi_{t}(\ell)=e_{t}
.

(2)

P_{t}
converges to
P_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
.
Let
\gamma_{t}\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be a path of hyperbolic elements with the axis
r
, such that
\gamma_{t}\operatorname{Axis}(e_{t})
converges to a geodesic
g_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
orthogonal to
r
as
t\to\infty
(so that
\gamma_{t}
is a large hyperbolic translation towards
v
for
t\gg 0
).
Let
H\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the totally geodesic hyperbolic plane orthogonal to
r
and containing
g_{\infty}
.
Then, the developing pair
\gamma_{t}(h_{t},\xi_{t})
normalized by
\gamma_{t}
converges to a developing pair for a round disk minus a point, where the removed point is
v
and the disk is the component of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}\setminus\partial H
containing
v
.

(3)

Let
\ell_{t}
be the boundary component of
P_{t}
corresponding to
\ell
.
Then
\operatorname{dev}P_{t}
along a lift of
\ell_{t}
is
b_{\ell_{t}}
(up to
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
).

(4)

Let
\alpha
be a boundary component of
P
not equal to
\ell
.
Then
\xi_{t}(\alpha)
is a hyperbolic element for all
t\gg 0
(converging to a parabolic element as
t\to\infty
).

Proof.

First we construct an appropriate path of representations
\xi_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(P)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
.
Let
a_{t}
denote
\operatorname{Axis}(e_{t})
.
Pick a pair of geodesics
q_{t},q_{t}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
for each
t>0
such that

•

R(q_{t})R(q_{t}^{\prime})=e_{t}
, where
R(q_{t}),R(q_{t}^{\prime})\in{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
are the
\pi
-rotations of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
about
q_{t},q_{t}^{\prime}
, respectively;

•

q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
change continuously in
t>0
;

•

q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
intersect at the intersection
a_{t}\cap r
;

•

q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
are symmetric about
r
;

•

q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
are orthogonal to
a_{t}
;

•

q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
converge to
r
as
t\to\infty
(see Figure
23
).

There is a path of geodesics
h_{t}\,(t\geq 0)
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that

•

h_{t}
is disjoint from
q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
for all
t\geq 0
, and

•

h_{t}
converges to a geodesic
h
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
sharing an endpoint with
r
as
t\to\infty
, such that the composition
R(r)R(h)
is the parabolic holonomy along a boundary geodesic of
P_{\infty}
.

Indeed one can first find the limit geodesic
h
which satisfies the second condition, then as
q_{t},q_{t}^{\prime}
converges to
r
, one can take a desired path
h_{t}
.

Let
\xi_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(P)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
be such that the holonomy along boundary components are
R(h_{t})R(q_{t}),R(q_{t})R(q_{t}^{\prime}),R(q_{t}^{\prime})R(h_{t})
.
Note that
R(h_{t})R(q_{t}),R(q_{t}^{\prime})R(h_{t})
are hyperbolic elements, as the rotation axes are disjoint, and they converge to the parabolic holonomy along the boundary geodesics of
P_{\infty}
.

Pick a round disk
D
on
P_{\infty}
containing
p
such that
\partial D
on
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
bounds a hyperbolic plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
orthogonal to
r
.
Then, apply Lemma
14.8
to
D
, let
D_{t}
be a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on an annulus converging to
D\setminus\{p\}
, so that it gives the desired path only near the punctured of
P_{\infty}
.

Pick a smaller closed regular neighborhood
D^{\prime}
of the puncture
p
of
P_{\infty}
such that
\partial D^{\prime}
bounds a hyperbolic plane orthogonal to
r
and that
D^{\prime}
is contained in the interior of
D
.
Clearly its complement
K
in
P_{\infty}
and the interior of
D\setminus\{p\}
form an open cover of
P_{\infty}
.
Then
K
is topologically a pair of pants.
Similarly to the proof of
Theorem

5.6
using the stability of transversal sections for the Thurston-Ehresmann principle (
[
Gol22
]
), we can prove that there is a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on a pair of pants
K_{t}
for sufficiently large
t>0
such that
K_{t}
converges to
K
and
e_{t}
is the holonomy of
K_{t}
around the boundary component corresponding to
\partial D^{\prime}
.
Moreover, by deformation nearly the boundary, we can in addition assume that the boundary of
K_{t}
is equivalent to
b_{\ell_{t}}
.

Then, since
K
and
D\setminus\{p\}
form an open cover of
P_{\infty}
, for sufficiently large
t
. by gluing
K_{t}
and
A_{t}
in the overlapping region, we obtained a desired path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
P_{t}
.

Realize
e_{t}
as the compositions of the
\pi
-rotations about
q_{t}
and
q_{t}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 14.10
.

Let
P_{t}=(h_{t},\xi_{t})
be a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on a pair of pants from
Proposition

14.9
.
Then, there is a path
\Sigma_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures on a closed surface
F
minus a point which satisfies the following:

•

There is a subsurface
A
of
F
whose interior contains
p
, such that
A
is homeomorphic to a pair of pants, and
\Sigma_{t}|A=P_{t}
for all large enough
t>0
.

•

\Sigma_{t}
converges to a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure
\Sigma_{\infty}
on
F
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

First we construct the limit structure
\Sigma_{\infty}
.
Take any complete hyperbolic surface
\tau
with a single cusp, such that
\tau
is homeomorphic to a closed surface minus a point, denoted by
F^{\prime}
.
Pick a cusp neighborhood
N
of
\tau
, a horodisk quotient.
The pair of pants
P_{\infty}
has two boundary components and one puncture.
As the two boundary components of
P_{\infty}
lift to horocycles,
we can glue a copy of
\tau\setminus N
along each boundary component of
P_{\infty}
.
We thus obtained a
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structure on a closed surface with a single puncture so that
P_{\infty}
is its subsurface.

There are paths
\zeta_{1,t}
and
\zeta_{2,t}
of representations
\pi_{1}(\tau)\to{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
which converge to the holonomy of
\tau
as
t\to\infty
, such that their images of the peripheral loop are
R(r_{t})R(q_{t}^{\prime})
and
R(q_{t})R(r_{t})
, respectively, which are hyperbolic elements (c.f.
[
Gol09
]
).
Let
\tau_{1,t},\tau_{2,t}
be paths of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures homeomorphic to
\tau\setminus N
for
t\gg 0
such that

\operatorname{Hol}(\tau_{1,t})=\zeta_{1,t}
, and
\operatorname{Hol}(\tau_{2,t})=\zeta_{2,t}
and
\tau_{1,t},\tau_{2,t}
converge to
\tau\setminus N
.
We may in addition assume that the boundary components of
\tau_{1,t},\tau_{2,t}
are invariant under one-dimensional subgroups of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
containing
R(r)R(q_{t}^{\prime})
and
R(q_{t})R(r)
, respectively.

Then by gluing
\tau_{t,1},\tau_{t,2},P_{t}
along their boundary, we obtain a desired path
\Sigma_{t}
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures.
∎

Let
\Sigma_{t}
be the path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures, obtained from Proposition
14.10
, on a compact surface with one boundary component.
Let
R_{t}
be the
\pi
-rotation of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
around the axis
a_{t}
of the elliptic
e_{t}
.
By
Proposition

14.9
(
2
,
3
), we can glue two copies of
\Sigma_{t}
by the involution
R_{t}
, and we obtain a path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures
C_{t}
on a closed surface, so that two copies of
\Sigma_{t}
are embedded in
C_{t}
disjointly up to an isotopy.
Let
m
be the loop along which the two copies are glued.
Then, to obtain a marked projective structure, we need to specify the twisting along
m
.
We glue then so that the Fenchel-Nielsen twisting parameter matches to be
u_{t}
so that, by the
\pi
-rotation along
a_{t}
, the developing maps of adjacent components of
\tilde{S}\setminus\tilde{m}
are identical.
Let
\Sigma_{t}^{1}=(h_{t}^{1},\rho_{t}^{1}),\Sigma^{2}_{t}=(h_{t}^{2},\rho_{t}^{2})
are the subsurfaces of
C_{t}
corresponding to
\Sigma_{t}
.

Theorem 14.11
.

Let
C_{t}=(f_{t},\rho_{t})
be the path of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
-structures as above, and let
m
be the loop on
C_{t}
corresponding to the boundary components of
\Sigma_{t}^{1}
and
\Sigma_{t}^{2}
.
Let
N
be the regular neighborhood of
m
.
Then, by taking an appropriate isotopy of
S
,
C_{t}
satisfies the following.

(1)

\rho_{t}(m)
converges to
I
as
t\to\infty
, and
\rho_{t}(m)
is an elliptic element for all
t>0
;

(2)

the axis of
\rho_{t}(m)
converges to the point
p
of
\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
;

(3)

f_{t}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
converges to a
\rho_{\infty}
-equivariant continuous map
f_{\infty}\colon\tilde{S}\setminus\phi^{-1}(N)\to\mathbb{C}{\rm P}^{1}
, such that
f_{\infty}
is a local homeomorphism in the interior;

(4)

for each connected component
\tilde{N}
of
\phi^{-1}(N)
, the boundary components of
\tilde{N}
map to its corresponding limit given by (
2
).

Proof.

Let
F_{1},F_{2}
be the connected components of
S\setminus N
.
We normalize the developing pair of
C_{t}
by a path of
{\rm PSL}_{2}\mathbb{C}
so that the restriction to
\tilde{F}_{1}
converges to a developing pair for
\Sigma_{\infty}
.
Then (1) and (2) clearly hold.
Moreover, we can take an appropriate isotopy of
S
so that each boundary component of
\tilde{F}_{1}
converges to the corresponding limit point of its corresponding axis.
Since the rotation angle of
e_{t}^{u_{t}}
is
\pi
by
Lemma

14.7
, the restriction of
f_{t}
to
\tilde{F}_{2}
is the same as that to to
\tilde{F}_{1}
(Figure
24
).
Therefore, the restriction of
f_{t}
to
\tilde{F}_{2}
converges to a developing map of
\Sigma_{\infty}
as well.
Thus we have (
3
).
Then, by the equivariant property, we also have (4).
∎

Figure 24.

The left figure is a section of the right figure by a horizontal plane containing
a_{t}
.
It illustrates the rotation about
a_{t}
by
\pi
, and it makes the restriction of
f_{t}
on
F_{1}
coincide with that to
F_{2}
coincide.
